--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening of the famous astronomical textbook "De Sphaera Mundi" (The Sphere of the World). It follows the title information and establishes the structure of the work, providing foundational definitions of the sphere based on classical authorities like Euclid and Theodosius.</meta>

### For young novices seeking entry into the astronomical commonwealth, along a very short and direct path set apart from common tracks: the little work on the sphere by Johannes de Sacrobosco. Along with the most accurate and most useful disputations of Johannes Regiomontanus <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg, a leading 15th-century mathematician</note> against the nonsensical planetary theories of Gerard of Cremona. Also the work dedicated to the most accurate theory of the motions of these same planets by Georg von Peuerbach, woven together in a useful sequence, begins.

**T**RACTATUM — We distinguish this treatise on the sphere into four chapters. We will speak first of what a sphere is, what its center is, what the axis of the sphere is, and what the pole of the world is; how many spheres there are, and what the form of the world is. ¶ In the second, we will speak of the circles of which the material sphere <note>A physical model, like an armillary sphere</note> is composed, and that super-celestial sphere which is understood to be composed through those imaginary circles. ¶ In the third, of the rising and setting of the zodiac signs, the diversity of days and nights for those living in different places, and the division of climates. ¶ In the fourth, of the circles and motions of the planets, and the causes of eclipses.

### Chapter One

**S**phera — A sphere is thus described by Euclid <note>original: "Euclide," referring to the Greek mathematician, c. 300 BCE</note>: A sphere is the transit of the circumference of a half-circle as many times as it is rotated around a fixed diameter until it returns to its own place. That is, a sphere is such a round and solid thing described by the arc of a semicircle being led around. A sphere is also described by Theodosius <note>original: "Theodosio," referring to Theodosius of Bithynia, c. 100 BCE</note> in this way: A sphere is a certain solid contained by one surface, in the middle of which is a point from which all lines drawn to the circumference are equal. And that point is called the center of the sphere. A straight line passing through the center of the sphere and touching its ends to the circumference on both sides is called the axis of the sphere. The two points terminating the axis are called the poles of the world. ¶ Moreover, the sphere is divided in two ways: according to substance and according to accident <note>In medieval philosophy, "substance" refers to what a thing is, and "accident" refers to its external properties</note>. According to substance, it is divided into nine spheres: namely, the ninth sphere, which is called the first motion or the <term>Primum Mobile: the outermost moving sphere in the geocentric model that carries all other spheres</term>; and the sphere of fixed stars, which is named the firmament.

<note>Image: A circular library stamp at the bottom center belonging to the National Central Library of Florence (Biblioteca Nazionale Centrale di Firenze), marked with the Roman numeral XV.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Johannes de Sacrobosco’s fundamental textbook on astronomy, outlining its four-part structure and providing geometric definitions of the sphere, its center, and its axis. It establishes the geocentric cosmological framework, beginning with the division of the heavens into the Ninth Sphere (Primum Mobile) and the Firmament.</summary>

<keywords>Sacrobosco, Regiomontanus, Peuerbach, De Sphaera Mundi, astronomy textbook, Euclid, Theodosius, geometry of the sphere, Primum Mobile, Firmament, geocentrism, Florence National Library</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological description of the universe, moving from the general structure to the specific arrangement of the seven planetary spheres nested within the firmament.</meta>

...and into the seven spheres of the seven planets: of which some are larger and some smaller, according to whether they approach or recede further from the <term>firmament</term> <note>original: "firmamento." In the geocentric (Earth-centered) model, the firmament was the sphere of the fixed stars, which formed the outermost boundary of the planetary system.</note>. Hence, among these, the sphere of Saturn is the largest. The sphere of the Moon is indeed the smallest: as is contained in the following illustration.

->[Illustration: A geocentric (Earth-centered) diagram of the world consisting of eleven concentric circles. The Earth is at the very center, followed by the spheres of the Moon (☾), Mercury (☿), Venus (♀), the Sun (☉), Mars (♂), Jupiter (♃), and Saturn (♄). The outermost ring is the Firmament or Zodiac, divided into the twelve signs: Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces.]<-

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the celestial sphere's properties and begins the description of the cosmological structure of the universe, moving from the geometric definitions of the horizon to the physical arrangement of the elements and the heavens.</meta>

[[page number: 3]]
<margin>Symbols of the planets</margin>

¶ According to the second accidental property <note>In medieval philosophy, an "accident" is a property that is not part of the essential nature of a thing; here, it refers to how the sphere appears based on one's location.</note>, however, the sphere is divided into the **right sphere** and the **oblique sphere**. Those are said to have a right sphere who live under the equinoctial <note>The celestial equator.</note>—if anyone is able to live there. And it is called "right" because neither of the poles is elevated higher than the other for them. Or, because their horizon intersects the equinoctial, and is intersected by it, at right spherical angles. Truly, those are said to have an oblique sphere whoever live around the equinoctial or beyond it. For them, one of the poles is always elevated above the horizon, while the other is always depressed. Or, because their artificial horizon intersects the equinoctial, and is intersected by it, at unequal and oblique angles.

### What the form of the world is.

¶ The universal machine of the world is divided into two: namely, into the **ethereal** and the **elemental** regions. The elemental region, being continually subject to alteration, is divided into four. For the **earth** is situated in the middle of all things as the center of the world; around which is **water**; around the water, **air**; around the air, **fire**, which is pure there and not turbulent, reaching the orbit of the moon, as Aristotle says in the book *On Meteorology* <note>original: "methauroꝝ" referring to Aristotle's *Meteorologica*</note>; for so has the glorious and sublime God disposed these things. And these four are called the elements, which in turn are altered, corrupted, and regenerated by one another. The elements are indeed simple bodies of the cosmos which can least be divided into parts of different forms. From their mixing, the various species of generated things are made. Any of the three <note>Water, air, and fire.</note> surrounds the earth on all sides in a circular fashion, except in so far as the dryness of the earth resists the moisture of the water to protect the life of living creatures. Also, all things besides the earth are mobile. The earth, as the center of the world, by its own weight <term>ponderositate: the natural tendency of heavy bodies to move toward the center of the universe</term> equally fleeing the great motion of the extremes on all sides, possesses the middle of the round sphere.

¶ Around the elemental region, the **ethereal region**—bright and immune from all variation in its immutable essence—proceeds in a continuous circular motion; and this is called by philosophers the **fifth essence** <note>Also known as the "aether" or "quintessence."</note>. Of this there are nine spheres, just as was treated in the previous section: namely, of the **Moon**, <term>☿: Mercury</term> **Mercury**, <term>♀: Venus</term> **Venus**, the **Sun**, <term>♂: Mars</term> **Mars**, **Jupiter**, **Saturn**, and of the **Stars** <note>The "fixed stars" or the firmament.</note>.

[[signature: a 3]]

[[vocabulary: right sphere, oblique sphere, equinoctial (equator), horizon, machine of the world, ethereal region, elemental region, earth, water, air, fire, Aristotle, Meteorology, fifth essence, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn]]

<summary>This page defines the right and oblique celestial spheres based on observer latitude and describes the Aristotelian "machine of the world," consisting of four changeable elements and the immutable fifth essence.</summary>
<keywords>sphaera recta, sphaera obliqua, four elements, fifth essence, geocentrism, Aristotle, planetary symbols, cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological description of the celestial spheres, likely from Johannes de Sacrobosco's "De Sphaera Mundi," a standard medieval textbook on astronomy.</meta>

...of the fixed stars and the ultimate heaven. Any of these higher spheres surrounds the lower spheres in a spherical manner. There are indeed two motions of these. One is the motion of the ultimate heaven <note>The "Primum Mobile" or First Mover, the outermost sphere in medieval cosmology</note> upon the two extremities of its axis, namely the north and south poles <note>original: polum arcticum et antarcticum</note>, moving from the east through the west and returning again to the east; this is divided in the middle by the <term>equinoctial circle: the celestial equator</term>. There is also another motion of the lower spheres along an oblique path, opposite to the first, moving upon its own axes which are distant from the first poles by 23 degrees and 33 minutes <note>This refers to the "obliquity of the ecliptic," the tilt of the Earth's axis relative to its orbit</note>. However, the first motion sweeps all other spheres along with it by its own force once around the Earth within a day and a night; yet those spheres strive against it, such as the eighth sphere <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note> which moves one degree in 100 years <note>This describes the phenomenon of precession</note>. This second motion is divided through the middle by the <term>zodiac: the belt of constellations through which the sun and planets appear to move</term>, under which each of the seven planets has its own sphere in which it is carried by its own motion against the motion of the ultimate heaven. These planets measure out this space in different lengths of time: Saturn in 30 years; Jupiter in 12; Mars in two; the Sun in 365 days and nearly six hours; Venus and Mercury in nearly the same; and the Moon in 27 days and 8 hours.

### On the revolution of the heaven.

The sign that the heaven revolves from east to west is this: the stars which rise in the east are always raised up little by little and successively until they reach the middle of the sky; they are always at the same proximity and distance from one another, and thus, always maintaining their positions, they tend toward their setting continuously and uniformly. There is also another sign: the stars which are near the north pole, which never set for us, move continuously and uniformly around the pole, describing their own circles, and are always at an equal distance and proximity to one another. Thus, by these two continuous motions of the stars—both those tending toward the setting and those that do not—it is evident that the <term>firmament: the sphere of the fixed stars or the sky</term> moves from east to west.

### On the roundness of the heaven.

There is a threefold reason why the heaven is round: likeness, utility, and necessity. For it is a matter of likeness because the sensible world <note>the physical universe perceived by the senses</note> was made in the likeness of the <term>archetypal world: the divine or ideal model of the universe</term>, in which there is no beginning...

<summary>This page describes the dual motions of the celestial spheres—the daily rotation of the highest heaven and the slower, opposite movement of the planets—while providing observational evidence for the sky's rotation and the philosophical reasons for its spherical shape.</summary>
<keywords>celestial spheres, Primum Mobile, zodiac, planetary orbits, precession, circumpolar stars, archetypal world, medieval astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the shape of the universe, specifically why the "sensible world" (the physical universe) must be spherical. It concludes the argument for the circularity of the heavens and begins the proof for the sphericity of the Earth.</meta>

...neither beginning nor end. From this, in likeness to it, the **sensible world** <note>original: "mundus sensibilis"; the physical universe perceived by the senses</note> has a round shape, in which one cannot assign a beginning or an end. 

**Convenience:** because of all <term>isoperimetric bodies: different shapes that have the same surface area</term>, the sphere is the largest; also, of all forms, the round is the most capacious. Since, therefore, it is the largest and round, it is the most capacious. Thus, since the world contains all things, such a shape was useful and convenient for it. 

**Necessity:** because if the world were of another shape besides round—namely three-sided, four-sided, or many-sided—two impossibilities would follow: namely, that some place would be empty, and some body would be without a place, both of which are false, as is evident in the case of raised and rotated angles. 

Also, as **Alfraganus** <note>Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Kathir al-Farghani, a 9th-century Persian astronomer whose works were a primary source for medieval European astronomy</note> says, if the heaven were flat, some part of the heaven would be closer to us than another—specifically, the part directly over our heads. Therefore, a star located there would be closer to us than one at its rising or setting. But those things which are closer to us appear larger. Thus, the sun or another star in the middle of the sky should appear larger than when rising or setting; yet we see the opposite happening. For the sun or another star appears larger when it is in the east or west than in the middle of the sky. 

But since the truth of the matter is not so, the cause of this appearance is that in winter or rainy weather, certain **vapors** rise between our sight and the sun or another star; and since those vapors are a <term>diaphanous body: a transparent or translucent substance</term>, they disperse our visual rays so that they do not perceive the thing in its natural and true size. This is evident from a **penny** <note>original: "denario"; a common silver coin</note> tossed into the bottom of clear water, which, due to a similar dispersion of rays, appears larger than its true size.

### That the earth is round.

That the earth is also round is shown thus: the signs and stars do not rise and set at the same time for all people everywhere. Instead, they rise and set earlier for those who are toward the east; and the cause for them rising and setting sooner for some and later for others is the **bulge of the earth** <note>original: "tumor terre"; referring to the curvature of the Earth's surface</note>.

<summary>This page explains why the universe is spherical based on geometric efficiency and physical necessity, while also using atmospheric refraction to explain why the sun looks larger on the horizon. It then begins proving the Earth's sphericity by noting that stars rise at different times in different locations.</summary>
<keywords>sphericity of the universe, isoperimetric bodies, Alfraganus, atmospheric refraction, celestial appearance, curvature of the earth, medieval astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs for the spherical shape of the Earth, moving from general arguments about the universe to specific observations of the stars and the behavior of water. It follows the discussion of how atmospheric conditions might affect our perception of celestial bodies.</meta>

This is clearly shown by those things that occur in the heights of the heavens. For one and the same **lunar eclipse** <note>original: "eclipsis lune"</note> which appears to us in the first hour of the night appears to those in the East around the third hour of the night. From this, it is certain that night came to them earlier, and the sun set for them earlier than for us. The cause of this is nothing other than the **curvature of the earth** <term>original: "tumor terre"; literally a "swelling," used here to describe the physical bulge or convex shape of a sphere</term>.

That the earth also has a curvature from the **north** <note>original: "septentrione"</note> to the **south** <note>original: "austrum"</note> and vice-versa is shown in this way. For those living toward the north, certain stars are of **perpetual appearance** <note>original: "sempiternę apparitionis"; these are circumpolar stars that never dip below the horizon from the viewer's perspective</note>, namely those that are close to the **arctic pole**. Others, however, are of perpetual occultation <note>stars that never rise above the horizon for a specific location</note>, such as those that are close to the **antarctic pole**. 

If, therefore, someone were to travel from the north toward the south, they could travel so far that the stars which were previously of perpetual appearance to them would now begin to set. And the further they approached the south, the more they would move toward setting. Furthermore, that same person would be able to see stars which had previously been of perpetual occultation to them. The reverse would happen to someone traveling from the south toward the north. The cause of this thing is the curvature of the earth.

Also, if the earth were flat from east to west, the stars would rise as quickly for those in the West as for those in the East, which is clearly false. Likewise, if the earth were flat from north to south and vice-versa, the stars which were of perpetual appearance to someone would always appear to them no matter where they traveled, which is also false. But the reason the earth appears flat to human sight is because of its immense size.

### ¶ That water is round.

That water also has a curvature and approaches a round shape is shown thus. Let a mark be placed on the seashore and let a ship leave the port; let it move so far away that an eye located at the foot of the mast cannot see the mark. However, while the ship remains in that position, the eye of the same person, if placed at the top of the mast, will clearly see that mark. Yet the eye of the one at the foot of the mast ought to see the mark better than the one at the top, as is shown by drawing lines from each to the mark. There is no other cause for this than the **curvature of the water** <note>original: "tumor aquę"</note>. For we must exclude all other impediments, such as clouds and rising vapors. Also, since water is a **homogeneous body** <term>a substance that is the same throughout; medieval science held that such elements naturally sought a spherical shape</term>, the whole...

<summary>This page provides empirical proofs for the Earth's sphericity using the timing of lunar eclipses and the shifting visibility of stars, and introduces the classic "ship and mast" proof for the curvature of the oceans.</summary>
<keywords>lunar eclipse, curvature of the earth, North and South poles, circumpolar stars, curvature of water, mast observation, medieval astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the Earth's position in the universe. Following the discussion on the Earth's curvature on the previous page, the author now addresses the Earth's placement at the center of the cosmos and its size relative to the stars.</meta>

...is of the same nature as its parts: but the parts of water—as happens in droplets and in the dewdrops on plants—naturally seek a round shape: therefore, so does the whole of which they are parts.

## That the Earth is the Center of the World.

That the Earth is situated in the middle of the **firmament** <term>original: "firmamentum"; in medieval cosmology, this is the outermost sphere of the universe containing the fixed stars</term> is shown in this way. To those existing on the surface of the Earth, the stars appear to be of the same size whether they are in the middle of the sky, or near their rising, or near their setting; and this is because the Earth is equally distant from them. For if the Earth were closer to the firmament in one part than in another, someone standing on the part of the Earth’s surface that was closer to the firmament would not see exactly half of the heavens. 

However, this is refuted by **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Roman mathematician and astronomer whose geocentric model dominated scientific thought for over a millennium</note> and all the philosophers, who say that wherever a person may be, six signs of the **zodiac** rise for them and six set, and half of the sky is always visible to them, while the other half remains hidden. 

This is also a sign that the Earth is like a center and a point with respect to the firmament; because if the Earth were of any significant size relative to the firmament, it would not be possible for half the sky to be seen. Likewise, if one imagines a flat surface passing through the center of the Earth and dividing it into two equal parts—and consequently dividing the firmament itself—an eye placed at the center of the Earth would see half of the firmament. The same eye, existing on the surface of the Earth, would see that same half. 

From these things, it is gathered that the distance from the surface to the center of the Earth is **negligible** <note>original: "insensibilis", literally "insensible" or "unnoticeable" to the senses</note>, and consequently the size of the whole Earth is negligible in comparison to the firmament. **Alfraganus** <note>Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Kathir al-Farghani, a 9th-century Persian astronomer whose "Elements of Astronomy" was a primary source for medieval scholars</note> also says that the smallest of the fixed stars visible to the eye is larger than the entire Earth; yet that star is like a point compared to the firmament. Therefore, this is even more true of the Earth, since it is even smaller than such a star.

## Concerning the Immobility of the Earth.

That the Earth is held immovably in the center of all things, even though it is exceedingly heavy, is suggested by its weight. For every heavy thing naturally tends toward the center. The center is indeed a point in the middle of the firmament; therefore, since the Earth is exceedingly heavy, it naturally tends toward that point. Likewise, whatever moves from the center toward the circumference of the sky is said to "ascend"; if the Earth moved away from the center, it would therefore be "ascending," which is considered an impossibility.

<summary>This page presents medieval proofs that the Earth sits at the exact center of the universe and is effectively a "point" in size compared to the vastness of the heavens. It uses the visibility of the zodiac signs and the gravitational tendency of heavy objects to explain why the Earth remains stationary at the cosmos's core.</summary>

<keywords>Ptolemy, Alfraganus, geocentrism, firmament, zodiac, gravity, immobility of Earth, medieval astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the conceptual proofs of the Earth’s location to its physical measurement and the geometric construction of the celestial sphere. It provides the specific calculations for the Earth's circumference and diameter before introducing the various circles (like the Equator) used to map the heavens.</meta>

### On the Absolute Size of the Earth.

¶ The total circumference of the earth, by the authority of the philosophers **Ambrose Theodosius Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Roman provincial and author whose commentary on Cicero’s *Dream of Scipio* was a primary source for medieval geography</note> and **Eratosthenes** <note>original: "Euristenis"; Eratosthenes (c. 276–194 BC) was the Greek polymath who first calculated the Earth’s circumference with remarkable accuracy</note>, is defined as containing 252,000 **stadia** <term>an ancient Greek unit of distance; based on the author's later calculations, this equates to roughly 25,000 to 30,000 miles</term>. This is calculated by assigning 700 stadia to each of the 360 parts of the **zodiac** <note>The 360 degrees of the celestial circle</note>. For if an **astrolabe** <term>a portable instrument used by astronomers to determine the position of celestial bodies</term> is taken during the clarity of a starry night, and the pole is sighted through both holes of the **sighting arm** <note>original: "medicliniu[m]"; the movable sighting rule or alidade on an astrolabe</note>, the number of degrees where the sighting arm stands should be noted. Then, let a "world-measurer" travel directly north from the south until, in another clear night, the pole is sighted as before and the sighting arm stands one degree higher. After this, let the distance of this journey be measured, and it will be found to be 700 stadia. Then, by giving 700 stadia to each of the 360 degrees, the circumference of the earthly globe will be found. From these figures, according to the rule for the circle and diameter, the diameter of the earth can be found in this way: subtract the twenty-second part from the circumference of the earth, and a third part of the remainder—which is 80,181 stadia and a half and a third of one stadium—will be the diameter or the thickness of the earthly globe.

¶ Chapter Two: On the circles from which the material sphere is composed, and that celestial sphere which is understood to be composed by imagining it through this one.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'H' with a floral and vine pattern in the background, set within a square frame.</image-desc>

Of these circles, some are greater and some are lesser, as is evident to the senses. For a **great circle** in a sphere is said to be that which, described on the surface of the sphere above its center, divides the sphere into two equal halves. A **lesser circle** is that which, described on the surface of the sphere, does not divide it into two equal halves, but into unequal portions. Among the greater circles, we must first speak of the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>. The equinoctial is, therefore, a certain circle dividing the sphere into two equal halves, which is equidistant from both poles in every one of its parts. And it is called "equinoctial" because when the sun passes through it—which happens twice a year, namely at the beginning of **Aries** <note>The spring equinox, around March 21</note> and the beginning of **Libra** <note>The autumnal equinox, around September 23</note>—there is an **equinox** <note>equal length of day and night</note> across the entire earth. For this reason, it is also called the "equalizer of day and night," because it makes the artificial day equal to the night. It is also called the **belt of the first motion** <note>original: "cingulus primi motus"; in geocentric cosmology, the "first motion" is the daily rotation of the outermost sphere of the universe, which carries all other spheres with it from east to west</note>. From which it should be known that the first motion...

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the celestial sphere's geometry, moving from the physical measurements of the Earth's diameter to the dynamics of the heavens and the specific nomenclature of the celestial poles. It connects the macrocosm of the universe to the microcosm of the human soul.</meta>

...is called the motion of the **first mover** <term>original: "primi mobilis"; the outermost sphere of the medieval universe which imparts motion to all other spheres</term>. This refers to the ninth sphere, or the final heaven, which moves from the east through the west and returns again to the east. This is also called the **rational motion** <note>Medieval thinkers believed the rotation of the highest heaven mirrored the "orderly" movement of human reason</note>, following the likeness of the movement of reason within the **microcosm** <term>literally "little world"; the human being seen as a reflection of the entire universe</term>. That is to say, just as human contemplation starts with the Creator, moves through the study of created things, and returns to rest in the Creator. The second motion, which belongs to the firmament and the planets, is contrary to this: it moves from the west through the east and returns again to the west. This is called the **irrational** or **sensual motion**, following the likeness of the movement of the microcosm which goes from corruptible things toward the Creator and returns again to corruptible things. Therefore, the "belt" of the first motion is so named because it girds or divides the **first mover**—that is, the ninth sphere—into two equal parts, sitting equidistant from the poles of the world.

In this regard, it should be noted that the pole of the world which is always visible to us is called the **northern**, **arctic**, or **boreal** pole. It is called **Septentrional** <note>original: "septentrionalis"</note> from the *septentrio*, which is the Lesser Bear; the name comes from *septen* [seven] and *trion* [ox], because the seven stars in that constellation move slowly like oxen, being so close to the pole. Others say those seven stars are called *septentriones* as if they were *teriones* [threshers], because they "tread" <note>original: "terunt"</note> the parts around the pole. It is called **Arctic** from the word *arctos*, which means the Greater Bear, as it is situated near the Greater Bear. Finally, it is called **Boreal** because it is in that region from which the **Boreas** <note>the North Wind</note> blows. The opposite pole is called the **Antarctic**, placed as if "against the arctic." It is also called the **Meridional** pole, because it is in the direction of the south <note>literally "midday," the direction of the sun at noon</note>, and it is also called the **Austral** pole, because it is in the region from which the **Auster** <note>the South Wind</note> blows. These two stable points in the firmament are called the poles of the world because they terminate the axis of the sphere, and the world revolves around them; one of them is always visible to us, while the other is always hidden. Thus **Virgil** <note>Publius Vergilius Maro (70–19 BC), the Roman poet; this quote is from his *Georgics*, a poem on agriculture and nature</note> says in the first book of the *Georgics*:

-> This pole is always high above us; but that other, <-
-> the black Styx and the deep spirits of the dead see beneath their feet. <-

### On the zodiac circle.

¶ There is another circle in the sphere which intersects the **equinoctial** <note>the celestial equator</note> and is intersected by it into two equal parts. One half of it inclines toward the north, and the other toward the south. This circle is called the **zodiac**, from the word *zoe* <note>Greek for "life"</note>...

<summary>This page describes the two primary motions of the heavens—the "rational" motion of the outermost sphere and the "sensual" motion of the planets—while providing the etymological origins for the names of the North and South Poles.</summary>
<keywords>first mover, microcosm, rational motion, celestial poles, Septentrional, Arctic, Virgil, Zodiac, equinoctial, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the celestial poles to the definition and division of the Zodiac. It establishes the etymology of the Zodiac and explains its geometric properties and its role in astronomical phenomena like eclipses.</meta>

...which means "life," because according to the motion of the planets beneath it, all life in lower things <note>Referring to the terrestrial world below the moon's sphere</note> is governed. Or it is named from *zodion* <note>original: "zodion"</note>, which means "animal." This is because, since it is divided into twelve equal parts, each part is called a **sign**, and each has a special name taken from the name of an animal. This is either because of some shared property between the sign and the animal, or because of the arrangement of the **fixed stars** <term>stars that appear to stay in a permanent position relative to each other</term> in those parts, which resemble such animals. 

This circle is called the **Sign-bearer** <term>original: "signifer"</term> in Latin, because it carries the signs or is divided into them. By **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC) whose physical theories dominated medieval science</note> in his book *On Generation and Corruption*, it is called the **oblique circle**. He says there that according to the approach and retreat of the sun along this oblique circle, the generation and decay of all lower things occur. 

The names of the signs, their order, and their number are shown in these verses: 
*Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo;* 
*Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius* <note>original: "architenes," literally "the bow-holder"</note>*, Capricorn* <note>original: "caper"</note>*, Aquarius* <note>original: "amphora"</note>*, and Pisces.* 

Each sign is divided into thirty **degrees** <term>original: "gradus"</term>. From this, it is clear that in the whole zodiac there are 360 degrees. Furthermore, according to astronomers, each degree is divided into sixty **minutes**; each minute into sixty **seconds**; each second into sixty **thirds**, and so on down to tenths. Just as the zodiac is divided by the astronomer, so too is every circle in the sphere—whether large or small—divided into similar parts. 

However, while every other circle in the sphere except the zodiac is understood as a simple line (like a circumference), the zodiac alone is understood as a surface having a width of twelve degrees, of the sort we have just discussed. From this, it is evident that some speak falsely in astrology when they say the signs are **square**, unless they are misusing the term and calling a rectangle a "square." For a sign has thirty degrees in length, but twelve in width. 

The line that divides the zodiac around its circumference, so that it leaves six degrees on one side and six on the other, is called the **ecliptic line**. It is so named because when the sun and moon are aligned directly beneath it, an **eclipse** of the sun or moon occurs. A solar eclipse happens if there is a **new moon** <term>original: "novilunium"</term> and the moon is placed directly between our view and the solar body. A lunar eclipse happens at the **full moon** <term>original: "plenilunium"</term> when the sun is diametrically opposed to the moon. Thus, an eclipse of the moon is nothing other than the Earth coming between the bodies of the sun and the moon.

<summary>This page defines the Zodiac as the "Sign-bearer," explaining its division into twelve signs of thirty degrees each and its physical dimensions as a band rather than a simple line. It also introduces the ecliptic line and explains the celestial mechanics behind solar and lunar eclipses.</summary>
<keywords>Zodiac, ecliptic, Aristotle, degrees, minutes, seconds, solar eclipse, lunar eclipse, zodiacal signs, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the Zodiac, moving from its basic division into degrees to its orientation relative to the celestial equator and various geometric definitions of a "sign." It concludes by introducing the Colures.</meta>

The **Sun** certainly always stays exactly on the **ecliptic** <term>the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</term>, but all other planets deviate either toward the north or toward the south; however, they are sometimes directly under the ecliptic. That part of the **zodiac** which deviates from the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term> toward the north is called "northern," "boreal," or "arctic." Those six signs from the beginning of **Aries** to the end of **Virgo** are called the northern signs. The other part of the zodiac which deviates from the equinoctial toward the south is called "meridional," "southern," or "antarctic." The six signs from the beginning of **Libra** to the end of **Pisces** are called the meridional or southern signs. When it is said that the Sun is "in Aries" or in another sign, it should be understood that this preposition "in" is used to mean "under," according to how we currently define a sign.

In another sense, a **sign** is defined as a **four-sided pyramid** whose base is that surface we call a sign, and whose vertex <note>the pointed tip or peak</note> is at the center of the Earth. According to this definition, properly speaking, we can say the planets are "in" the signs <note>Because they are physically located within the volume of that pyramid</note>.

In a third way, a sign is understood by imagining six circles passing through the **poles of the zodiac** and through the starting points of the twelve signs. These six circles divide the entire surface of the sphere into twelve parts that are wide in the middle but narrower near the poles of the zodiac; each such part is called a sign and takes its specific name from the sign captured between its two boundary lines. According to this definition, even the stars located near the poles are said to be "in" the signs.

Furthermore, imagine a solid body whose base is a sign (according to the definition just given), but whose sharp edge lies along the axis of the zodiac. In this fourth sense, such a body is called a sign; by this definition, the whole universe is divided into twelve equal parts called signs, and thus whatever exists in the world is located within one sign or another.

### Concerning the Two Colures.

There are two other **great circles** <term>circles that divide a sphere into two equal halves</term> in the sphere which are called **colures**, whose function is to distinguish the **solstices** and **equinoxes**. The word "colure" comes from the Greek *colon* <note>original: "colon"</note>, which means "limb" or "member," and *uros* <note>original: "vros"</note>, which means "wild ox." This is because, just as the upright tail of a wild ox—which is one of its limbs—forms a semicircle and not a complete circle, so the colure always appears to us as imperfect or incomplete <note>Medieval astronomers noted that because of the tilt of the sphere, these circles often appeared "cut off" or incomplete relative to the horizon</note>.

<summary>This page defines the northern and southern divisions of the Zodiac and provides four distinct geometric ways to conceptualize a "sign," concluding with the etymology of the Colures.</summary>
<keywords>Ecliptic, Northern Signs, Southern Signs, Geometric Signs, Colures, Solstice, Equinox, Johannes de Sacrobosco, Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the celestial sphere. The previous page concluded by introducing the Colures, which are the great circles that intersect at the celestial poles to mark the solstices and equinoxes.</meta>

...since only one half of it appears: the other, however, is hidden from us.

The **colure** <term>a great circle on the celestial sphere that passes through both poles</term> distinguishing the solstices thus passes through the poles of the world, through the poles of the **zodiac**, and through the points of the sun's maximum **declination** <term>the angular distance of a heavenly body north or south of the celestial equator</term>—that is, through the first degrees of **Cancer** and **Capricorn**. Wherefore the first point of Cancer, where this colure intersects the zodiac, is called the point of the **summer solstice** <term>the point where the sun reaches its highest northern point, creating the longest day</term>; because when the sun is in it, it is the summer solstice, and the sun cannot approach any closer to our **zenith**. The **zenith** is a point in the firmament directly placed above our heads. The arc of the colure, however, which is captured between the point of the summer solstice and the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>, is called the **maximum declination** of the sun. And it is, according to **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer of antiquity</note>, 23 degrees and 51 minutes. According to **Almeon** <note>The Caliph Al-Ma'mun (reigned 813–833 AD), who commissioned new astronomical measurements in Baghdad</note>, however, it is 23 degrees and 33 minutes. 

Similarly, the first point of **Capricorn**, where the same colure intersects the zodiac on the other side, is called the point of the **winter solstice** <term>the point where the sun is furthest south, creating the shortest day</term>; and the arc of the colure captured between that point and the equinoctial is called the other maximum declination of the sun, and it is equal to the first one. 

The other **colure** indeed passes through the poles of the world and through the first points of **Aries** and **Libra**, where the two **equinoxes** <term>the two times each year when day and night are of equal length</term> occur; wherefore it is called the colure distinguishing the equinoxes. These two colures intersect each other at the poles of the world at right spherical angles. The signs of the solstices and equinoxes are made clear by these verses: *These two solstices are made by Cancer and Capricorn; but Aries and Libra make the nights equal to the days.* <note>original Latin: "hec duo solsticia faciūt cācer capricor:n⁹ sed noctes equāt aries ⁊ libra diebus."</note>

### Concerning the meridian and the horizon.

There are also two other major circles in the sphere: namely, the **meridian** and the **horizon**. The **meridian** is a certain circle passing through the poles of the world and through our zenith. And it is called "meridian" because wherever a person may be, and at whatever time of year, when the sun reaches its meridian by the motion of the firmament, it is midday <note>Latin: *meridies*</note> for that person. For a similar reason, it is called the "circle of mid-day." It should be noted that cities, where one is further to the east than the other, have different meridians. The arc of the equinoctial captured between two meridians is called the **longitude of cities** <term>geographic longitude</term>. If, however, two cities have the same meridian, then they are equally distant from the east and the west. The **horizon**, moreover—

<summary>This page defines the two Colures (the circles marking solstices and equinoxes) and provides the measurements for the sun's maximum declination according to Ptolemy and Al-Ma'mun. It also introduces the Meridian as the circle determining midday and geographic longitude.</summary>
<keywords>Colures, Solstice, Equinox, Zenith, Declination, Ptolemy, Al-Ma'mun, Meridian, Longitude, Horizon</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the celestial sphere. The previous page ended while describing the winter solstice point, where the colure intersects the zodiac at the first degree of Capricorn.</meta>

...is the circle dividing the lower hemisphere from the upper. Whence it is called the **horizon** <term>the boundary between the earth and the sky</term>, that is, the "limiter of sight." It is also called the circle of the hemisphere. There are two types of horizons: the **right** and the **oblique** (or sloping). Those who have their **zenith** <term>the point directly overhead</term> on the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term> possess a **right horizon** and a **right sphere**. This is because their horizon is a circle passing through the **poles of the world** <term>the north and south celestial poles</term>, dividing the equinoctial at right spherical angles. For this reason, it is called a "right horizon" and a "right sphere." 

Conversely, those for whom the pole of the world is elevated above the horizon have an **oblique** or sloping horizon, because their horizon intersects the equinoctial at unequal and oblique angles. Whence it is called an **oblique horizon** and an **oblique** or sloping sphere. 

The zenith above our heads is always the pole of our horizon. From this, it is clear that however much the pole of the world is elevated above the horizon, the distance of the zenith from the equinoctial is exactly the same. This is demonstrated as follows: Since on any natural day each **colure** <term>a great circle passing through the celestial poles</term> is joined twice to the **meridian** (or is essentially the same as the meridian), whatever is proven about one applies to the other. Let us, therefore, take a quarter part of the colure that distinguishes the solstices, which runs from the equinoctial to the pole of the world. Then, take another quarter part of the same colure that runs from the zenith to the horizon (since the zenith is the pole of the horizon). Because these two segments are both quarters of the same circle, they are equal to one another. According to the rules of geometry, if equal parts—or a shared common part—are subtracted from equals, the remainders will be equal. Therefore, if we subtract the common arc <note>The shared segment of the circle between the zenith and the celestial pole</note> that lies between the zenith and the pole of the world, the remaining parts will be equal: namely, the elevation of the pole of the world above the horizon and the distance of the zenith from the equinoctial.

## ¶ Concerning the four minor circles.

Having spoken of the six major circles <note>The Horizon, Meridian, Equinoctial, Zodiac, and the two Colures</note>, we must now speak of the four minor ones. It should be noted that when the sun is in the first point of **Cancer** (the point of the summer solstice), it describes a certain circle through the daily rotation of the firmament. This circle is the furthest one described by the sun toward the **arctic** <term>northern</term> pole. For this reason, it is called the "circle of the summer solstice," as explained above, or the **summer tropic**. It takes its name from the Greek word *tropos* <note>original: "tropos", meaning a turn or change in direction</note>, which signifies a "turning," because at that point the sun begins to turn back toward the lower hemisphere.

<summary>This page defines the horizon and explains the mathematical relationship between the height of the North Star (the celestial pole) and one's latitude (zenith distance from the equator). It also introduces the Tropic of Cancer as the first of the four minor celestial circles.</summary>
<keywords>horizon, zenith, right sphere, oblique sphere, equinoctial, celestial pole, latitude, geometry, Tropic of Cancer, summer solstice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric proofs concerning the celestial sphere. The previous page discussed the relationship between the horizon, the equinoctial (equator), and the elevation of the poles. This page moves to defining the tropics and the arctic circles based on the sun's path and the tilt of the zodiac.</meta>

...and recede from us. The Sun, when it is again located in the first point of Capricorn <note>the start of the zodiac sign Capricorn, marking the winter solstice</note> or the winter solstice: by the rotation of the firmament <term>the outermost celestial sphere that carries the stars</term>, it traces a certain circle which is the final circle traced by the sun on the side of the antarctic pole <term>the south celestial pole</term>. Whence it is called the circle of the winter solstice or the **winter tropic** <note>original: "tropicus hyemal"; from the Greek 'tropos' meaning 'turn,' as the sun appears to turn back toward the equator at this time</note>: because then the sun turns back toward us. Since, however, the **zodiac** <term>the belt of the heavens containing the paths of the planets</term> is tilted away from the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>, the pole of the zodiac will also be tilted away from the pole of the world. Therefore, as the **eighth sphere** <note>In the medieval geocentric model, the eighth sphere contains the fixed stars and the zodiac</note> moves, the zodiac—which is part of that eighth sphere—will move around the axis of the world; likewise, the pole of the zodiac will move around the pole of the world. That circle which the pole of the zodiac traces around the northern arctic pole of the world is called the **Arctic Circle**. That circle which the other pole of the zodiac traces around the southern antarctic pole of the world is called the **Antarctic Circle**. 

However great the maximum declination <term>the furthest distance north or south of the equator</term> of the sun is from the equinoctial, the distance from the pole of the world to the pole of the zodiac is exactly the same; this is demonstrated as follows. Take the **colure** <term>a great circle passing through the poles</term> that distinguishes the solstices, which passes through both the poles of the world and the poles of the zodiac. Since all quarters of one and the same circle are equal to each other, the quarter of this colure extending from the equinoctial to the pole of the world will be equal to the quarter of the same colure extending from the first point of Cancer <note>the summer solstice point</note> to the pole of the zodiac. Therefore, if we subtract the common arc from those equal quarters—the arc that lies between the first point of Cancer and the pole of the world—the remaining parts will be equal: namely, the maximum declination of the sun and the distance from the pole of the world to the pole of the zodiac. 

Since, however, every part of the Arctic Circle is equidistant from the pole of the world, it is clear that the portion of the colure between the first point of Cancer and the Arctic Circle is almost double the maximum declination of the sun. Alternatively, it is double the arc of the same colure intercepted between the Arctic Circle and the northern pole of the world, which arc is also equal to the maximum declination of the sun. For since this colure, like other circles on the sphere, consists of 360 degrees, a quarter of it will be 90 degrees. Since the maximum declination of the sun, according to **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Roman astronomer</note>, is 23 degrees and 51 minutes, and the arc between the Arctic Circle and the northern pole of the world is the same number of degrees: if these two are joined together—which makes nearly 48 degrees—and subtracted from 90, the remainder...

<summary>This page defines the Tropics and the Arctic/Antarctic circles using the geometry of the celestial sphere. It proves that the tilt of the Earth's axis (the distance between the celestial poles and the zodiac poles) is equal to the sun's maximum declination from the equator.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric proofs concerning the celestial sphere. The previous page discussed the relationship between the horizon, the equinoctial (equator), and the elevation of the poles, specifically concluding a thought on the measurement of the sun's maximum distance from the equator.</meta>

...will be 42 degrees: which is the length of the arc of the colure <term>one of two great circles that intersect at the celestial poles, passing through the equinoxes or solstices</term> that lies between the first point of Cancer and the arctic circle. And thus it is evident <note>original: "pʒ" / "patet"</note> that this arc is almost double the maximum declination <term>the furthest distance north or south of the equator</term> of the sun. <note>In medieval astronomy, if the sun's maximum declination—the tilt of the Earth's axis—was rounded to 24 degrees, then the Arctic Circle would be 24 degrees from the pole, and 42 degrees from the Tropic of Cancer (90 - 24 - 24 = 42).</note>

¶ It should be noted that the equinoctial <term>the celestial equator</term> along with the four lesser circles are called the five parallels, meaning they are equidistant. This is not because the distance from the first to the second is the same as the distance from the second to the third—for this is false, as has already been shown—but because any two circles joined together remain equidistant from each other in every part. These are called: the equinoctial parallel, the parallel of the summer solstice, the parallel of the winter solstice, the arctic parallel, and the antarctic parallel.

¶ It should also be noted that the four lesser parallels—namely the two tropics, the arctic parallel, and the antarctic parallel—distinguish five zones or regions in the heavens. Whence Virgil [says] in the *Georgics*: <note>Virgil was a famous Roman poet; the *Georgics* is his poem on agriculture and the natural world.</note>

> Five zones possess the sky: of which one is always reddened by the flashing sun and always scorched by fire. <note>original: "Quinque tenent caelum zonae: quarum una corusco. Semper sole rubens: et torrida semper ab igni." Virgil, Georgics, Book I, 233-234.</note>

An equal number of regions are also distinguished on the earth, placed directly beneath the aforementioned zones. Whence Ovid [says] in the first [book] of the *Metamorphoses*: <note>Ovid was a Roman poet; the *Metamorphoses* is a narrative poem describing the creation and history of the world.</note>

> And an equal number of regions are pressed upon the earth. Of which that which is in the middle is not habitable because of the heat. Deep snow covers two; he placed as many between both, and gave them a temperate climate, where flame is mixed with cold. <note>original: "Totidemque plagae tellure premuntur. Quarum que media est. non est habitabilis estu. Nix tegit alta duas: totidem inter utrasque locavit. Temperiemque dedit mixta cum frigore flamma." Ovid, Metamorphoses, Book I, 48-51.</note>

¶ Therefore, that zone which is between the two tropics is called uninhabitable because of the heat of the sun, which is always running between the tropics. Likewise, the region of the earth placed directly beneath it is called uninhabitable <note>At this time, many scholars believed the "Torrid Zone" at the equator was too hot for human life.</note> because of the heat of the sun running over it. 

But those two zones which are bounded by the arctic circle and the antarctic circle around the poles of the world are uninhabitable because of excessive cold, as the sun is furthest removed from them. The same is to be understood regarding the regions of the earth placed directly beneath them. 

However, those two zones—of which one is between the summer tropic <note>the Tropic of Cancer</note> and the arctic circle, and the other which is between the winter tropic <note>the Tropic of Capricorn</note> and the antarctic circle—are habitable. They are tempered by the heat of the torrid zone existing between the tropics and the cold of the extreme zones which are around the poles of the world. Understand the same for the regions of the earth placed directly beneath them.

<meta>This page transitions from the geometric description of the celestial spheres and zones (concluding with a quote from Virgil's Georgics on the previous page) to the practical application of these circles in determining the rising and setting of zodiac signs, specifically how these terms are used differently by poets and astronomers.</meta>

### ¶ Chapter three: On the rising and setting of the signs; on the diversity of days and nights; and on the diversity of climates.

**S**ignoz <note>The woodcut initial 'S' begins the word "Signorum" (of the signs).</note> The rising and setting of the signs is understood in two ways: namely, as they relate to the poets, and as they relate to the astronomers. Therefore, the rising and setting of the signs according to the poets is threefold: namely, **cosmic**, **chronic**, and **heliacal**.

The **cosmic** rising—or mundane rising—occurs when a sign or star rises above the horizon in the east during the day. And although in any "artificial day" <term>the period from sunrise to sunset</term> six signs rise in this manner, nevertheless, by way of excellence <note>original: "antonomasice"</note>, that sign is said to rise cosmically with which and in which the sun rises in the morning. This is called the proper, principal, and daily rising. 

An example of this rising is found in the *Georgics* <note>A poem by the Roman poet Virgil concerning agriculture</note>, where he teaches the sowing of beans and millet in the spring, while the sun is in Taurus, thus: "The white Bull <note>Taurus</note> with golden horns opens the year, and the Dog-star <note>Sirius; original: "canis"</note> gives way and sets before the opposing constellation." 

The **cosmic setting**, however, is considered in relation to opposition: namely, when the sun rises with a certain sign, the sign opposite to it sets cosmically. Of this setting, it is said in the *Georgics* where he teaches the sowing of wheat at the end of autumn while the sun is in Scorpio: since Scorpio rises with the sun, Taurus (the sign opposite to it where the Pleiades are) sets. He says: "Let the morning daughters of Atlas <note>The Pleiades; original: "atlantides"</note> first be hidden before you commit the owed seeds to the furrows."

The **chronic rising**—or temporal rising—is when a sign or star emerges above the horizon in the east after the setting of the sun; that is, "chronically" or at night. It is called "temporal" because the time used by mathematicians <term>in this period, the term "mathematician" usually referred to astronomers or astrologers</term> begins with the setting of the sun. 

We have an example of this rising in Ovid’s *Letters from the Black Sea* <note>original: "Ouidio de ponto"</note>, where he complains of the delay of his exile, saying: "The risen Pleiades have made four autumns." This signifies that four years <note>The OCR reads "quadragintaquattuor" (44), which is likely a scribal error or OCR artifact for "quattuor" (4), as Ovid refers to four years of exile.</note> had passed since he was sent into exile. 

However, Virgil intended the Pleiades to set in autumn; therefore, the poets seem to contradict one another. But the reason for this is that according to Virgil they set **cosmically**, while according to Ovid they rise **chronically**. Both can easily occur on the same day, but at different times: for the cosmic setting relates to the morning time, whereas the chronic rising relates to the evening. The chronic setting is determined by opposition.

<summary>This page explains the "poetic" definitions of the rising and setting of zodiac signs—specifically cosmic and chronic—using classical examples from Virgil and Ovid to illustrate how stars can be said to rise and set simultaneously depending on the time of day.</summary>

<keywords>cosmic rising, chronic rising, Virgil, Ovid, Georgics, Pleiades, Taurus, Scorpio, astronomical definitions, poetic astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the rising and setting of celestial signs. It transitions from the poetic (cosmic and heliacal) definitions to the more technical definitions used by astronomers, specifically focusing on how the orientation of the celestial sphere (right vs. oblique) affects the time it takes for signs to rise.</meta>

Thus Lucan says: "Then the short night urged on the Thessalian arrows." <note>original: "Tunc nox thessalicas urgebat parua sagittas"; Lucan was a Roman poet, and 'Thessalian arrows' refers to the constellation Sagittarius.</note> A **heliacal rising**—or solar rising—occurs when a sign or star can be seen because of the sun’s distance from it, whereas previously it could not be seen due to the sun’s proximity. Ovid provides an example of this in his book *Fasti* <note>A Latin poem explaining the Roman calendar</note> as follows: "Now light Aquarius has sunk with his slanted urn." And Virgil in the *Georgics*: "And the Gnosian star of the burning Crown descends." <note>original: "Gnosiaque ardentis descendit stella corone"; this refers to Ariadne's Crown (Corona Borealis), which was said to be from Gnossos in Crete.</note> This star, being near Scorpio, was not seen while the sun was in Scorpio. A **heliacal setting** occurs when the sun approaches a sign and, by its presence and brightness, does not allow that sign to be seen. An example of this is in the verse mentioned before, namely: "The Bull and the Dog-star, yielding to the opposing star, set." <note>This refers to the quote from Virgil on the previous page.</note>

## On the rising and setting of the signs according to astronomers.

Next follows the rising and setting of the signs as astronomers understand them, and first in the **right sphere**. <term>The right sphere (sphera recta) is the celestial globe as viewed from the equator, where the poles lie on the horizon.</term> It must be known that in both the right and the **oblique sphere** <term>the celestial globe viewed from a specific latitude where the pole is elevated</term>, the equinoctial circle <note>the celestial equator</note> always rises uniformly; that is, equal arcs rise in equal amounts of time. For the motion of the heavens is uniform, and the angle which the equinoctial makes with the oblique horizon does not vary at different hours. However, the parts of the zodiac do not necessarily have equal **ascensions** <note>the time or arc of the equator that rises alongside a sign</note> in either sphere. For the more "rightly" a part of the zodiac rises, the more time it takes in its rising. The sign of this is that six signs rise during a long or short artificial day, and similarly during the night. It should be noted, therefore, that the rising or setting of any sign is nothing other than that part of the equinoctial rising which rises with that sign as it ascends above the horizon; or that part of the equinoctial setting which sets with another sign as it descends below the horizon. A sign is said to rise "rightly" when a larger part of the equinoctial rises with it, and "obliquely" when a smaller part rises with it. The same is to be understood regarding the setting. And it should be known that in a right sphere, the four quarters of the zodiac—beginning at the four points, namely the two solstices and the two equinoxes—are equal in their ascensions; that is, the amount of time consumed by a quarter of the zodiac in its rising is equal to...

<summary>This page explains heliacal rising and setting using classical poetry before defining the astronomical mechanics of how zodiac signs rise and set relative to the celestial equator.</summary>
<keywords>heliacal rising, heliacal setting, Lucan, Ovid, Virgil, right sphere, oblique sphere, equinoctial circle, zodiac, ascension</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the rising and setting of celestial signs in the right sphere (as seen from the equator), specifically explaining the mathematical relationship between the zodiac and the equinoctial circle.</meta>

At the same time, a quadrant of the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term> corresponding to it finishes rising. Nevertheless, the parts of those quadrants vary; nor do they have equal ascensions, as will now be shown. For there is a rule: any two equal arcs of the **zodiac** equally distant from any of the four aforementioned points <note>The four cardinal points: the two equinoxes (Aries and Libra) and the two solstices (Cancer and Capricorn).</note> have equal ascensions. And from this it follows that **opposite signs** have equal ascensions. And this is what **Lucan** <note>A Roman poet whose work *Pharsalia* is often cited by medieval scientists for its astronomical descriptions.</note> says when speaking of Cato's journey into Libya toward the equinoctial:

*They do not pass obliquely; nor does Scorpio come forth more upright than Taurus; nor does Aries give his time to Libra; nor does Astraea command the slow Pisces to descend. Chiron is equal to the Twins; and the same for the burning Crab, the watery Capricorn, nor is the Lion raised up more than its counterpart.* <note>original: "Non obliqua meant: nec tauro rectior exit Scorpius: aut aries donat sua tpa librę: Aut aſtrea iubet lentos deſcendere piſces. Par geminis chiron: & idẽ qđ charcinus ardens. humidus egloceros: nec plus leo tollitur vna." Lucan uses poetic names: Astraea is Virgo, Chiron is Sagittarius, and Egloceros is Capricorn.</note>

Here Lucan says that for those living under the equinoctial, opposite signs have equal ascensions and settings. The opposition of the signs is held in this verse:

->*Libra-Aries, Scorpio-Taurus, Sagittarius-Gemini, Capricorn-Cancer, Aquarius-Leo, Pisces-Virgo.*<- <note>original: "Eſt li.ari. ſcoꝛ.tau. ſa.gemi. capri.can. a.le. piſ.vir." This is a mnemonic verse for the six pairs of zodiacal opposites.</note>

And it should be noted that such an argument is not valid: "These two arcs are equal, and they begin to rise at the same time, and always a greater portion of one has risen than of the other; therefore, that arc will finish rising more quickly of which a greater part was always rising." A clear **refutation** <note>original: "Instantia"; in medieval logic, an "instance" or example used to disprove a general rule.</note> of this argument is found in the parts of the aforementioned quadrants. 

For if a quadrant of the zodiac is taken—the one from the beginning of Aries to the end of Gemini—a greater part of the zodiacal quadrant is always rising than of the equinoctial quadrant corresponding to it; and yet those two quadrants finish rising at the same time. Understand the same concerning the quadrant of the zodiac from the beginning of Libra to the end of Sagittarius. Likewise, if the quadrant of the zodiac from the beginning of Cancer to the end of Virgo is taken, a greater part of the equinoctial quadrant always rises than of the zodiacal quadrant corresponding to it; and yet those two quadrants finish rising at the same time. Understand the same concerning the quadrant of the zodiac from the first point of Capricorn to the end of Pisces. 

In the **oblique sphere** or tilted sphere, however, the two halves of the zodiac are made equal to their ascensions. I say those "halves" which are taken from the two equinoctial points: because the half of the zodiac which is from the beginning of Aries to the end of Virgo rises with the half of the equinoctial corresponding to it. Similarly, the other half of the zodiac rises with the remaining half of the equinoctial. 

Par / <meta>Catchword: Equal</meta>

<summary>This page explains the geometry of "ascensions" (how signs rise over the horizon), demonstrating that at the equator, opposite signs rise in equal time. It uses both mathematical rules and poetic evidence from Lucan to describe how quadrants of the zodiac align with the celestial equator.</summary>
<keywords>ascensions, right sphere, oblique sphere, Lucan, opposite signs, zodiacal quadrants, equinoctial, celestial geometry</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical explanation of how the signs of the zodiac rise relative to the celestial equator, transitioning from a discussion of the "right sphere" (the equator) to the "oblique sphere" (other latitudes) and concluding with an explanation of why the length of a solar day varies throughout the year.</meta>

However, the parts of those halves vary according to their ascensions: because in that half of the **zodiac** which is from the beginning of **Aries** to the end of **Virgo**, a larger part of the zodiac always rises than of the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>; and yet those halves finish rising at the same time. Conversely, it happens in the remaining half of the zodiac, which is from the beginning of **Libra** to the end of **Pisces**: for a larger part of the equinoctial always rises than of the zodiac; and yet those halves finish rising at the same time.

Hence the objection made above against the previous argument appears more clearly here: The arcs that follow from Aries to the end of Virgo in the **oblique sphere** <term>the sky as viewed from latitudes away from the equator</term> decrease their ascensions compared to the ascensions of those same arcs in the **right sphere** <term>the sky as viewed from the equator</term>, because less of the equinoctial rises. And the arcs that follow from Libra to the end of Pisces in the oblique sphere increase their ascensions over the ascensions of those same arcs in the right sphere, because more of the equinoctial rises. I say they increase by as much as the arcs following Aries decrease.

From this it is clear that two equal <margin>Add.</margin> and opposite arcs in the **declined sphere** <term>a sphere where the poles are not on the horizon; another term for the oblique sphere</term> have their combined ascensions equal to the ascensions of those same arcs in the right sphere taken together; because the amount of the decrease on one side is equal to the addition on the other. For although the arcs are equal to each other, however much one is smaller, the other recovers that amount; and thus the equalization is clear.

The rule in the oblique sphere is that any two equal arcs of the zodiac, equally distant from either of the two equinoctial points <note>The points where the ecliptic and the celestial equator intersect, i.e., the first points of Aries and Libra.</note>, have equal ascensions.

From the aforementioned, it is also clear that **natural days** <term>the full 24-hour period from one noon to the next</term> are unequal. For a natural day is one revolution of the equinoctial around the earth, along with that part of the zodiac through which the sun travels in its own motion against the firmament in the meantime. But since the ascensions of those arcs are unequal—as is clear from what was said before regarding both the right and oblique spheres—and since the additions of those ascensions are considered, those natural days will necessarily be unequal. In the right sphere, this is for a single reason: namely, because of the **obliquity of the zodiac** <note>The tilt of the sun's path relative to the celestial equator.</note>. In the oblique sphere, however, it is for two reasons: namely, because of the obliquity of the zodiac, and

<summary>This page explains the mathematical differences in how zodiac signs rise in different latitudes and uses these variations to prove why solar days (natural days) are not of uniform length throughout the year.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the variation in day length, specifically looking at how the Sun’s path (the parallels) interacts with the horizon in different geographical locations (the right versus the oblique sphere).</meta>

...by the obliquity of the **oblique horizon** <term>the horizon at latitudes away from the Earth's equator</term>. A third cause is usually assigned: the **eccentricity** <term>the off-center nature</term> of the Sun’s circle. 

It should also be noted that as the Sun moves from the first point of **Capricorn** through **Aries** to the first point of **Cancer**, it describes 182 parallels <note>These represent the daily paths of the sun over the half-year it takes to move between the tropics</note> by the **rotation of the firmament** <term>the daily 24-hour movement of the heavens</term>. Regarding these parallels: even if they are not perfectly circles but rather spirals, since there is no perceptible error in this, no fuss should be made if they are called "circles." Among these circles are the two **tropics** and the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>. Likewise, the Sun describes these same circles by the rotation of the firmament as it descends from the first point of Cancer through **Libra** to the first point of Capricorn. 

These circles are called the "circles of the natural days" <note>A "natural day" in medieval astronomy refers to a full 24-hour period of day and night combined</note>. However, the arcs of these circles that are above the horizon are called the "arcs of the artificial days" <note>An "artificial day" refers only to the period of daylight</note>. Those arcs which are below the horizon are the "arcs of the nights." 

Therefore, in the **right sphere** <term>the sky as viewed from the Earth's equator</term>, because the horizon of the right sphere passes through the poles of the world, it divides all of these circles into equal halves. For this reason, for those living under the equinoctial, the arcs of the days are exactly equal to the arcs of the nights. From this it is clear that for those living at the equator, it is always the **equinox**, no matter what part of the firmament the Sun is in.

In the **declined sphere** <term>the sky as viewed from latitudes where the poles are tilted</term>, however, the **oblique horizon** divides only the equinoctial into two equal parts. Thus, when the Sun is in either of the two equinoctial points <note>the first points of Aries or Libra</note>, the arc of the day equals the arc of the night, and there is an equinox across the entire Earth. 

But the oblique horizon divides all other circles into unequal parts, such that in all the circles from the equinoctial up to the **tropic of Cancer**, and in the tropic of Cancer itself, the arc of the day is greater than that of the night—that is, the arc above the horizon is greater than the arc below the horizon. Consequently, during the entire time the Sun moves from the beginning of Aries through Cancer to the end of **Virgo**, the days increase over the nights; they increase more the closer the Sun approaches Cancer, and they decrease the further it recedes from it. 

Conversely, the same happens regarding days and nights while the Sun is in the southern signs. In all the other circles which the Sun describes between the equinoctial and the **tropic of Capricorn**, the part of the circle below the horizon is greater and the part above is smaller. Thus, the arc of the day is smaller than the arc of the night. And according to the proportion of these smaller arcs...

<summary>This page explains why day and night are equal at the equator (the right sphere) but vary in length at other latitudes (the oblique sphere) as the Sun moves between the northern and southern tropics.</summary>
<keywords>oblique horizon, right sphere, equinox, natural day, artificial day, tropic of Cancer, tropic of Capricorn, celestial equator, day length variation</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how the Sun’s position relative to the celestial equator (the equinoctial) and the viewer's latitude affects the length of days and nights, transitioning into an explanation of the rising times of the zodiac signs.</meta>

...days longer than nights. And the closer the circles <note>the daily paths of the Sun</note> are to the **winter tropic** <term>the Tropic of Capricorn</term>, the more the days are shortened. From this, it is seen that if two circles are taken at an equal distance from the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term> on opposite sides, the length of the arc of the day in one is equal to the length of the arc of the night in the other. From this, it follows that if two **natural days** <note>a full 24-hour period</note> are chosen in the year that are equally distant from either of the **equinoxes** but on opposite sides, the length of the **artificial day** <note>the period of sunlight</note> of one is equal to the night of the other, and vice versa. 

But this is according to the common perception of the fixed horizon. For reasoning judges more accurately by the movement of the Sun against the **firmament** <term>the background of stars</term> within the tilt of the **zodiac** <term>the path the Sun appears to follow through the constellations</term>. Indeed, the more the **pole of the world** <note>the North Star/Celestial Pole</note> is elevated above the horizon—the further north one travels from the equator—the longer the summer days are when the Sun is in the northern signs. But it is the opposite when the Sun is in the southern signs: for then the days are more greatly shortened compared to the nights.

¶ It should also be noted that the six signs of the zodiac from the beginning of **Cancer** through **Libra** to the end of **Sagittarius** have their **ascensions** <term>the time it takes for a sign to rise above the horizon</term> in the **oblique sphere** <term>latitudes away from the equator</term> which, when added together, are greater than the ascensions of the six signs from the beginning of **Capricorn** through **Aries** to the end of **Gemini**. Hence, those first six signs mentioned are said to rise "rightly" <term>signs of long ascension</term>, while these other six rise "obliquely" <term>signs of short ascension</term>.

Whence **Virgil** <note>original: "Virgilius"; the author quotes a mnemonic verse attributed to Virgil but common in medieval astronomy</note> says: *They travel straight; they fall obliquely from the sign of Cancer until Sagittarius is finished; but the other signs are born on a downward slope and descend by a straight path.*

And when we have our longest day in summer—namely, when the Sun is at the beginning of Cancer—then during the day, the six signs that rise "directly" <note>taking more time to rise</note> come up; but during the night, the six that rise "obliquely" <note>taking less time to rise</note> come up. Conversely, when we have the shortest day of the year—namely, when the Sun is at the beginning of Capricorn—then during the day, the six signs rising obliquely come up, but during the night, the six rising directly. However, when the Sun is at either of the two equinoctial points, then during the day, three signs rising directly and three signs rising obliquely come up; and similarly during the night. For there is a rule: no matter how short or long the day or night may be, six signs always rise during the day and six during the night. Neither more nor fewer signs rise because of the length or shortness of the day or night.

b 4 <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical symmetry between day and night lengths at different times of the year and introduces the concept of "long" and "short" ascension—why certain zodiac signs take longer to rise than others depending on the season.</summary>
