<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the rising times of the zodiac signs and how the tilt of the Earth's axis (the zodiac) relative to the equator (the equinoctial) affects the length of days and nights for people at different latitudes, specifically focusing on those living at the equator.</meta>

the space of time in which half of a sign <note>half of a zodiac sign, or 15 degrees</note> rises is equal; in any **artificial day** <note>the period of daylight</note> and similarly in the night, there are 12 natural hours. However, in all other circles that are to the side of the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>—either on the southern or northern side—the days or nights increase or decrease according to whether more or fewer signs rise directly or obliquely during the day or night.

## On the diversity of days and nights which occurs for those living in different places on the earth.

¶ It should be noted that for those whose **zenith** <note>the point in the sky directly above an observer</note> is on the **equinoctial circle** <term>the equator</term>, the sun passes through the zenith of their heads twice a year: namely, when it is at the beginning of **Aries** or at the beginning of **Libra**. At those times, they have two "high solstices" <note>original: "alta solstitia"</note>, because the sun passes directly over their heads. They also have two "low solstices" <note>original: "ima solstitia"</note> when the sun is at the first points of **Cancer** and **Capricorn**; they are called "low" because then the sun is furthest removed from the zenith of their heads. 

From the aforesaid, it is clear: since they always have an **equinox** <note>equal day and night</note>, they will have four solstices in a year: two high and two low. It is also clear that they have two summers, specifically when the sun is in either of the equinoctial points or near them. They also have two winters, specifically when the sun is in the first points of Cancer and Capricorn or near them. 

And this is what **Alfraganus** <note>Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Kathīr al-Farghānī, a 9th-century Persian astronomer</note> says: that our summer and winter are for them uniform and of the same nature; because the two seasons which are summer and winter for us are two winters for them. The explanation is clear from those verses of **Lucan** <note>A Roman poet whose 'Pharsalia' often included astronomical lore</note>: *It is discovered that this is the place where the circle of the high solstice strikes the middle of the circle of the signs.* <note>original: "Deprensum est hunc esse locum quo circulus alti Solstitij medium signorum percutit orbem."</note> 

For there, Lucan calls the equinoctial the "circle of the high solstice," in which two high solstices occur for those living under the equinoctial. He calls the **zodiac** the "circle of signs," which is "middle"—that is, centered or divided into two halves—which the equinoctial "strikes"—that is, divides. For those people, it also happens that they have four shadows in a year. For when the sun is in either of the equinoctial points, then in the morning their shadow is cast toward the west, and in the evening toward the east. At midday, however, they have a shadow...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial phenomena for people living at the equator (the equinoctial), specifically how the sun's position affects their shadows and the visibility of polar constellations, before moving on to those living between the equator and the Tropic of Cancer.</meta>

...perpendicular when the sun is directly over their heads. But when the sun is in the northern signs <note>the constellations of the zodiac north of the celestial equator</note>, then their shadow is cast toward the south. When the sun is in the southern signs, then the shadow is cast toward the north. For these people, even those stars that are near the poles rise and set, just as they do for others living near the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>. Whence Lucan <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus, a Roman poet</note> says: "Then Roman madness moved the remote Orestae and Armenian leaders; whose sky, already turned toward the south, does not however see the whole of the **Great Bear** <note>original: "arcton"</note> submerged. And swift **Boötes** <note>a constellation near the Great Bear</note> shines there in the brief night." <note>original: "Tūc furor extremos mouit roman⁹ Orestas. Armeniosqz duces: quorum iam flexus in austrum. Aether non totam mergi tamen aspicit arcton. Lucet ⁊ exigua velox ibi nocte boetes."</note> Therefore, it is submerged and shines very little. Likewise, Ovid says regarding the same star: "The guardian of the Erymanthian bear <note>referring to the constellation Boötes or Arctophylax</note> is dipped into the ocean, and troubles the sea waters with his constellation." <note>original: "Tingitur oceano custos erimanthidos vrse. Aequoreasqz suo sidere turbat aquas."</note> 

But in our location <note>the author's latitude in Europe</note>, those stars never set. Whence Virgil says: "This pole is always high above us, but that one black **Styx** <note>the river of the underworld</note> and the deep spirits of the dead see beneath their feet." <note>original: "hic vertex nobis semper sublimis at illum. Sub pedibus styx atra vidēt manesqz profundi."</note> And Lucan says: "An unsetting axis, most bright with the twin Bears." <note>original: "Axis inociduus gemina clarissim⁹ arcton."</note> Likewise Virgil in the *Georgics* says: "The Bears, fearing to be submerged in the water of the ocean." <note>original: "Arctos oceani metuentes equore mergi."</note>

->**Those whose zenith is between the equinoctial and the tropic of Cancer.**<-

Moreover, for those whose **zenith** <note>the point in the sky directly above the observer</note> is between the equinoctial and the **tropic of Cancer**, it happens twice in a year that the sun passes directly over their heads; which is evident in this way: Let a circle be understood parallel to the equinoctial passing through the zenith of their heads; that circle will intersect the **zodiac** <term>the sun's annual path</term> in two places equidistant from the beginning of Cancer. 

Therefore, the sun, being in those two points, passes through the zenith of their heads. Because of this, they have two summers and two winters, four solstices, and four types of shadows—just like those living under the equinoctial. Some say that Arabia is in such a location. Whence Lucan, speaking of the Arabs coming to Rome to aid Pompey, says: "You Arabs have come into a world unknown to you, marveling that the shadows of the groves do not fall to the left." <note>original: "Ignotū vobis arabes venistis i orbem. Vmbras mirati nemorum non ire sinistras."</note> 

This is because in their own regions, at different times their shadows were to the right, at times to the left, at times perpendicular, at times to the east, and at times to the west. But when they had come to Rome—which is located near the Tropic of Cancer <note>The author uses "Rome" here to represent the northern temperate zone where shadows always point north at noon</note>—they then always observed northern shadows.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial geometry and human habitation. The previous page established how those at the equator see the sun and stars; this page moves northward to those living at the Tropic of Cancer and in the temperate zones, including the author’s own location in Europe.</meta>

## For those whose zenith is at the Tropic of Cancer.

Indeed, for those whose **zenith** <term>the point in the sky directly above an observer</term> is at the **Tropic of Cancer**, it happens that once a year the sun passes directly through the zenith of their heads—specifically when the sun is at the first point of the constellation **Cancer**. At that time, during one hour of one single day in the entire year, their shadow is perfectly perpendicular <note>meaning the sun is so directly overhead that no shadow is cast to the side</note>. In such a location is situated the city of **Syene** <note>modern-day Aswan in Egypt</note>. Whence Lucan <note>a Roman poet</note> says: "With Syene never turning the shadows." <note>original: "Umbras nusquam flectente Syene"</note> Understand this to refer to the midday of that one specific day; throughout the rest of the entire year, their shadow is cast toward the north.

## For those whose zenith is between the Tropic of Cancer and the Arctic Circle.

For those whose zenith lies between the Tropic of Cancer and the **Arctic Circle**, it happens that the sun never passes directly through the zenith of their heads. For these people, the shadow is always cast toward the north. This is our own location <note>the author is writing from a European perspective</note>. It should also be noted that **Ethiopia**, or at least some part of it, is located near the Tropic of Cancer. Whence Lucan says: "And the soil of the Ethiopians, which is not pressed upon by any region of the **zodiac** <note>original: "signiferi... poli," the 'sign-bearing pole' or celestial path of the planets</note>; with its knee bent, the last hoof of the curved **Bull** <note>the constellation Taurus</note> would advance." <note>original: "Aethiopumque solum quod non premeretur ab ulla. Signiferi regione poli: in poplite lapso. Ultima curvati procederet ungula tauri."</note>

For some say that in this passage, the word "sign" is used equivocally for both the twelfth part of the zodiac and for the physical shape of the animal; for the animal is mostly contained within the sign that bears its name. Thus, although the **Bull** is mostly within the zodiac, it extends its foot beyond the Tropic of Cancer, and in this way it "presses upon" Ethiopia, even though no actual part of the zodiac circle presses upon it. For if the foot of the Bull of which the author speaks were extended toward the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>, so that it was directly in line with **Aries** or another sign, then Ethiopia would be "pressed upon" by Aries or **Virgo** and other signs. This is evident if one considers a circle parallel to the equator drawn through the zenith of the Ethiopians themselves, and through Aries and Virgo or other signs.

But since physical reason contradicts this—for the inhabitants would not be so blackened <note>referring to the dark skin of people in tropical regions</note> if they were born in a temperate, habitable region—it must be said that the part of Ethiopia of which Lucan speaks is actually beneath the **equinoctial circle** <term>the equator</term>, and that the foot of the Bull of which he speaks extends toward that equator. But a distinction is then made between the **cardinal signs** and the regions. For the cardinal signs are said to be the two signs in which the **solstices** <term>the points where the sun reaches its highest or lowest point</term> occur, and the two in which...

And according to this, it is clear that since **Ethiopia** is under the equinoctial line <note>the celestial equator</note>, it is not pressed upon by any region of the zodiac, but only by the two cardinal signs: namely **Aries** and **Libra**.

### For those whose zenith is on the Arctic Circle.

Now, for those whose **zenith** <term>the point in the sky directly above an observer</term> is on the **Arctic Circle**, it happens at a certain time of the day and year that the zenith above their heads is the same as the pole of the **zodiac**. At that moment, they have the zodiac—or the **ecliptic** <term>the sun's apparent path across the sky</term>—acting as their horizon. And this is what **Alfraganus** <note>a famous 9th-century Persian astronomer</note> means when he says that there the circle bends above the circle of the hemisphere.

But since the **firmament** <term>the sphere of the stars</term> moves continuously, the circle of the horizon will intersect the zodiac in an instant; and since they are "great circles" on the sphere, they will divide each other into equal parts. Therefore, immediately one half of the zodiac emerges above the horizon and the other half is suddenly depressed below the horizon. This is what Alfraganus means when he says that there, six signs [of the zodiac] set suddenly, and the other six rise along with the entire **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator</term>. Furthermore, when the ecliptic is their horizon, the entire **Tropic of Cancer** will be above the horizon, and the entire **Tropic of Capricorn** will be below the horizon. Thus, when the sun is at the first point of Cancer, they will have one day of twenty-four hours, with only an instant for a night, because in that instant the sun touches the horizon and immediately emerges; that point of contact serves as the night. The opposite happens for them when the sun is at the first point of Capricorn. For then they have one night of twenty-four hours, and only an instant for a day.

### For those whose zenith is between the Arctic Circle and the Pole of the World.

For those whose zenith is between the Arctic Circle and the northern **Pole of the World** <note>the North Pole of the celestial sphere</note>, it happens that their horizon intersects the zodiac at two points equidistant from the beginning of Cancer. During the revolution of the firmament, it happens that the intercepted portion of the zodiac always remains above the horizon. From this it is clear that as long as the sun is in that intercepted portion, there will be one continuous day without night. Therefore, if that portion is the size of one sign, there will be a continuous day of one month without night; if it is the size of two signs, it will be two months long, and so on. Likewise, it happens for these same people that the intercepted portion of the zodiac...

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of celestial geometry at high latitudes and moves to the extreme case of the North Pole (the Arctic Pole). It continues the explanation of how the signs of the zodiac rise and set in unusual sequences when viewed from these northern regions before transitioning into a definition of the polar day and night.</meta>

...from those two points equidistant from the beginning of **Capricorn**, it remains always below the horizon; hence, when the sun is in that intercepted portion, there will be one night without day, whether short or long according to the size of the intercepted portion. However, the remaining signs that rise and set for them do so in reverse order. They rise in reverse order, such as **Taurus** before **Aries**, **Aries** before **Pisces**, and **Pisces** before **Aquarius**. And yet, signs that are twice opposite <note>signs 180 degrees apart</note> rise in the correct order and set in reverse, such as **Scorpio** before **Libra**, and **Libra** before **Virgo**. Nevertheless, those signs set directly which were rising in reverse order, such as **Taurus**...

### For those whose zenith is at the North Pole.

¶ Now, for those whose **zenith** <term>the point in the sky directly above an observer</term> is at the **Arctic Pole** <note>the North Pole</note>, it happens that their **horizon** is the same as the **equinoctial line** <term>the celestial equator</term>. Since the equinoctial line intersects the **zodiac** <term>the belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs</term> into two equal parts, so too their horizon leaves one half of the zodiac above it and the other half below. 

Hence, when the sun moves through that half which is from the beginning of **Aries** until the end of **Virgo**, there will be one continuous day without night; and when the sun runs through that half which is from the beginning of **Libra** until the end of **Pisces**, there will be one continuous night without day. Therefore, one half of the entire year is one **artificial day** <term>the period from sunrise to sunset</term>, and the other half is one night. Thus, the whole year there is one **natural day** <term>a full 24-hour cycle, or here, a full solar year</term>. 

But since the sun there is never depressed more than 23 degrees below the horizon, it might seem to them that there is a continuous day without night. For even among us, "day" is said to exist before the rising of the sun above the horizon <note>referring to twilight</note>. This, however, is according to common perception. For there is no **artificial day** according to physical reasoning except from the rising of the sun until its setting below the horizon. 

Regarding the idea that light might seem to be perpetual there—because day is said to begin before the sun is raised 18 degrees above the earth, as **Ptolemy** <note>a 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer</note> says (though other masters say 30 degrees, namely the length of one zodiac sign)—it must be said that the air there is cloudy and thick. For the solar ray existing there is of weak power and raises more vapors from the earth than it can consume; therefore, it does not clear the air, and it is not truly day.

### On the division of the climates.

It also addresses the scientific definition of a "day" versus common perception and the role of atmospheric density in polar light.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the celestial phenomena observed at the North Pole to terrestrial geography. It establishes the physical division of the Earth into quadrants and introduces the classic concept of the "seven climates" of the habitable world, moving from the mathematical division of the sphere to the practical boundaries of human life.</meta>

¶ Imagine a certain circle on the surface of the earth placed directly beneath the **equinoctial** <term>the celestial equator; here, the terrestrial equator</term>. Let another circle be understood on the surface of the earth passing through the east and the west, and through the poles of the world <note>this refers to a prime meridian circle</note>. These two circles intersect each other in two places at spherical right angles and divide the whole earth into four quarters. One of these is our habitable land: specifically, that which is intercepted between the semicircle drawn from east to west and the **Arctic Pole** <note>the North Pole</note>. 

Yet, not all of that quarter is habitable, because the parts of it near the equator are uninhabitable on account of excessive heat. Similarly, the parts near the Arctic Pole are uninhabitable on account of excessive cold. Therefore, let one line be understood equidistant from the equator, dividing the parts of the quarter that are uninhabitable on account of heat from the habitable parts which are toward the north. Let also another line be understood equidistant from the Arctic Pole, dividing the parts of the quarter that are uninhabitable (which are toward the north) on account of cold from the habitable parts which are toward the equator. Between these two extreme lines, let six lines parallel to the equator be understood, which, together with the two previous lines, divide the total habitable part of the quarter into seven portions which are called the **seven climates**, as is evident in the present figure.

->**Uninhabitable land on account of cold**<-

| **Seventh climate: Through the Rhipaean Mountains** <note>original: *diaripheos*. The Rhipaean Mountains were a mythical range in the far north.</note> |
| :---: |
| **Sixth climate: Through the Borysthenes** <note>original: *diaboriſtenes*. Referring to the Dnieper River in modern Ukraine.</note> |
| **Fifth climate: Through Rome** <note>original: *diaromcs* (likely a typo for *diaromes*).</note> |
| **Fourth climate: Through Rhodes** <note>original: *diarbodos* (typo for *diarhodos*).</note> |
| **Third climate: Through Alexandria** <note>original: *dialexandrios*.</note> |
| **Second climate: Through Syene** <note>original: *diaſyenes*. Syene is modern Aswan in Egypt.</note> |
| **First climate: Through Meroë** <note>original: *diameroes*. Meroë was an ancient city in modern Sudan.</note> |

->**Torrid zone: Uninhabitable on account of heat**<-

These bands are bounded by the extremes of frozen and scorched lands, with each climate named after a major geographical landmark or city.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "seven climates" introduced on the previous page, shifting from the general definition of a climate to a detailed mathematical and geographical breakdown of the first four zones based on day length and the altitude of the North Pole.</meta>

¶ Now, a **climate** <term>clima; a latitudinal band of the earth</term> is said to be that span of the earth through which the **sundial** <term>horologium; the measurement of time and shadows</term> varies noticeably. For a summer day is of a certain length in one region and is noticeably shorter in a region closer to the south. Therefore, the span across which the length of the day begins to vary perceptibly is called a climate. Nor is the same sundial reading observed at the beginning and the end of this space; for the hours of the day vary noticeably, and thus the sundial does as well.

¶ The middle of the **first climate** is where the maximum length of the day is 13 hours, and the elevation of the pole of the world above the circle of the hemisphere is 16 degrees; it is called the climate **of Meroë** <note>original: "diameroes"; referring to the ancient city of Meroë in modern Sudan</note>. Its beginning is where the length of the longest day is 12 hours and a half and a quarter of an hour <note>12 hours and 45 minutes</note>, and the pole is elevated above the horizon by 12 degrees and a half and a quarter of a degree <note>12.75 degrees</note>. Its width extends to the place where the length of the longest day is 13 hours and a quarter, and the pole is elevated above the horizon by 20 degrees and a half, which is a terrestrial distance of 440 miles.

¶ The middle of the **second climate** is where the longest day is 13 hours and a half, and the elevation of the pole above the horizon is 24 degrees and a quarter of a degree. It is called the climate **of Syene** <note>original: "diasyenes"; referring to Syene, modern Aswan, Egypt</note>. Its width, however, is from the boundary of the first climate to the place where the longest day becomes 13 hours and a half and a quarter of an hour <note>13 hours and 45 minutes</note>, and the pole is elevated 27 degrees and a half; the distance on the earth is 400 miles.

¶ The middle of the **third climate** is where the length of the longest day becomes 14 hours, and the elevation of the pole above the horizon is 30 degrees and a half and a quarter of one part <note>30.75 degrees</note>. It is called the climate **of Alexandria** <note>original: "dialexandrios"; passing through Alexandria, Egypt</note>. Its width is from the boundary of the second climate to the place where the longest day is 14 hours and a quarter, and the height of the pole is 33 degrees and two-thirds; this distance on the earth is 350 miles. 

¶ The middle of the **fourth climate** is where the length of the longest day is fourteen hours and a half, and the latitude of the axis <note>the elevation of the celestial pole</note> is 36 degrees and two-fifths. It is called **of Rhodes** <note>original: "diarhodos"; passing through the island of Rhodes</note>. Its width indeed is from the boundary of the third climate to the place where the length of the longest day is 14 hours and a half...

<summary>This page defines the concept of a "climate" as a region where the length of a day and sundial shadows vary perceptibly, then provides specific coordinates and distances for the first four climates of the habitable world.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the specific breakdown of the seven climates, detailing the fifth, sixth, and seventh zones. It provides the mathematical boundaries for each based on day length, the angle of the North Pole, and physical distance in miles, ultimately explaining why the climates narrow as they approach the pole.</meta>

and a fourth part of one hour; the elevation of the pole, however, is 39 degrees. This span of earth is 300 miles.

¶ The middle of the **fifth climate** is where the longest day is 15 hours, and the elevation of the pole is 41 degrees and a third of one degree. It is called the climate **of Rome** <note>original: "diaromes"; literally "passing through Rome"</note>. Its width indeed is from the boundary of the fourth climate up to where the length of the day is 15 hours and a fourth of one hour, and the elevation of the **axis** <note>referring to the celestial axis or the North Pole</note> is 43 degrees and a half; this span of earth is 255 miles.

¶ The middle of the **sixth climate** is where the longest day is 15 hours and a half, and the pole is elevated above the horizon by 45 degrees and two-fifths of one degree. And it is called the climate **of the Borysthenes** <note>original: "diaborystenes"; referring to the Borysthenes, the ancient Greek name for the Dnieper River in Eastern Europe</note>. Its width indeed is from the boundary of the fifth climate up to where the length of the day is longer, namely 15 hours and a half and a fourth of one hour <note>15 hours and 45 minutes</note>; and the elevation of the axis is 47 degrees and a fourth of one degree. This distance of the earth is 212 miles.

¶ The middle of the **seventh climate** is where the greatest length of the day is 16 hours, and the elevation of the pole above the horizon is 48 degrees and two-thirds. It is called the climate **of the Rhipaean Mountains** <note>original: "diariṕheos"; referring to the legendary Riphean Mountains of the far north</note>. Its width indeed is from the boundary of the sixth climate up to where the maximum day is 16 hours and a fourth of one hour, and the pole of the world is elevated above the horizon by 50 degrees and a half. This span of earth is 185 miles.

¶ Beyond the boundary of this seventh climate, although there may be many islands and human habitations, whatever they may be, since they are of **poor habitation** <note>referring to the harsh, cold conditions unsuitable for "civilized" living by ancient standards</note>, they are not counted under a climate. Therefore, the total variation between the starting boundary of the climates and their final boundary is three hours and a half, and a difference in the elevation of the pole above the horizon of 38 degrees.

Thus, therefore, the width of each climate is evident from its beginning toward the **equinoctial** <term>equinoctialem; the celestial equator</term> to its end toward the North Pole; and so the width of the first climate is greater than the width of the second, and so on. The length of a climate, however, can be called the line drawn from east to west, equidistant from the equator. Thus, the length of the first climate is greater than the length of the second, and so on; which happens because of the narrowing of the **sphere** <term>spherę; the curvature of the earth as it tapers toward the poles</term>.

<summary>This page completes the description of the seven traditional latitudinal zones (climates), identifying the fifth through seventh climates by their central locations (Rome, the Dnieper, and the Riphean Mountains). It explains that these zones represent the habitable world, beyond which the environment is too harsh for inclusion in the system.</summary>
<keywords>fifth climate, sixth climate, seventh climate, Rome, Borysthenes, Rhipaean Mountains, latitude, day length, terrestrial miles, sphere curvature</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the geography of the Earth’s climates to celestial mechanics. Having defined the seven habitable zones in the previous chapter, the text now begins Chapter Four, which focuses on the geometric circles and mathematical movements of the sun, moon, and planets that govern the heavens.</meta>

# Chapter four concerning the circles and motions of the planets:
## And concerning the causes of the eclipses of the sun and moon.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap initial 'N' featuring interlaced white vine-stem or floral patterns on a dark hatched background.</image-desc>It should be noted that the sun has a single circle through which it is moved upon the surface of the ecliptic line <note>the apparent path of the sun across the sky</note>; and it is an **eccentric** circle. Indeed, an eccentric circle is not just any circle, but only such a one that, while dividing the earth into two equal parts, does not have its center shared with the center of the earth, but rather outside of it. Now, the point in the eccentric circle that approaches closest to the firmament <note>the highest sphere of the stars</note> is called the **aux** <note>original: "aux"; an Arabic-derived term for apogee, the point furthest from Earth</note>, which is interpreted as the "elevation." The opposite point, however, which is at the greatest distance from the firmament <note>and thus closest to Earth</note>, is called the **opposition of the aux** <note>perigee</note>. There are, however, two motions of the sun from the west to the east: one of which is its own proper motion in its eccentric circle, by which it is moved nearly 60 minutes <note>one degree</note> in every day and night. The other indeed is the slower motion of its own sphere upon the poles of the axis of the circle of the signs <note>the zodiac</note>, and it is equal to the motion of the sphere of the fixed stars, namely, one degree in 100 years <note>this refers to the precession of the equinoxes</note>. From these two motions, therefore, its course in the circle of the signs from west to east is gathered, through which it traverses the circle of the signs in 365 days and nearly a quarter of one day, except for a tiny amount that is of no sensible consequence.

Every planet, however, besides the sun, has three circles: namely, the **equant**, the **deferent**, and the **epicycle** <note>these are the geometric components of the Ptolemaic system used to explain non-uniform planetary motion</note>. The equant of the moon is a circle concentric with the earth, and it is on the surface of the ecliptic. Its deferent, however, is an eccentric circle, nor is it on the surface of the ecliptic; rather, one half of it declines toward the north, and the other toward the south. And the deferent intersects the equant in two places. And the figure of the intersection is called the **dragon** <note>original: "draco"; a reference to the moon's nodes</note>, because it is wide in the middle and narrower toward the ends. That intersection, therefore, through which the moon moves from the south to the north is called the **head of the dragon** <note>the ascending node</note>. The other intersection, through which it moves from the north to the south, is called the **tail of the dragon** <note>the descending node</note>. The deferents and equants of any planet are equal. And it should be known that both the deferent and the equant of **Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury** are eccentric and outside the surface of the ecliptic; and yet those two

<summary>This page introduces the geometric models of medieval astronomy, defining eccentric circles, the apogee (aux), and the two distinct motions of the sun. It also begins to describe the more complex three-circle system of the planets and the lunar nodes, metaphorically called the "dragon."</summary>
<keywords>eccentric circle, aux, apogee, solar motion, zodiac, equant, deferent, epicycle, lunar nodes, head of the dragon, tail of the dragon</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary orbits from page 16, specifically concluding the definition of the eccentric circle before moving into epicycles and lunar eclipses. The text follows the structure of Johannes de Sacrobosco’s "De Sphaera Mundi," a foundational medieval astronomical textbook.</meta>

...are in the same surface. Every planet, moreover, except for the sun, has an **epicycle**. An epicycle is a small circle along whose circumference the body of the planet is carried; and the center of the epicycle is always carried along the circumference of the **deferent** <note>the "carrying" circle that represents the planet's main orbit around the Earth</note>. If, therefore, two lines are drawn from the center of the earth so that they enclose the epicycle of any planet—one on the eastern side and the other on the western side—the point of contact on the eastern side is called the **first station**; the point of contact on the western side is called the **second station**. And when a planet is in either of those stations, it is said to be **stationary**.

The upper arc of the epicycle intercepted between the two stations is called **direction** <note>forward motion in the order of the zodiac signs</note>; and when the planet is in that arc, it is then said to be **direct**. The lower arc of the epicycle intercepted between the two stations is called **retrogradation** <note>backward motion</note>; and a planet existing there is said to be **retrograde**. To the moon, however, no station, direction, or retrogradation is assigned. Thus, the moon is not called stationary, direct, or retrograde because of the speed of its motion in its epicycle <note>the moon moves so quickly that it never appears to stop or move backward from Earth's perspective</note>.

## ¶ On the eclipse of the moon.

Since the sun is larger than the earth, it is necessary that at least half of the sphere of the earth is always illuminated by the sun. The shadow of the earth, extended into the air, is a **pyramid** <note>a cone-shaped shadow</note> that diminishes in roundness until it ends upon the surface of the circle of the signs <note>the zodiac or ecliptic</note>, and it is inseparable from the **nadir** of the sun. Now, the **nadir** of the sun is the point directly opposite the sun in the firmament. 

Hence, when at the full moon the moon is in the **head** or in the **tail of the dragon** <note>original: "capite vel in cauda draconis"; these are the lunar nodes, the two points where the moon's tilted orbit crosses the path of the sun</note> under the nadir of the sun, then the earth is placed between the sun and the moon. Since the moon has no light except from the sun, it is in truth deprived of light. This is a **total eclipse** over the whole earth if the moon is directly in the head or tail of the dragon. It is a **partial eclipse**, however, if it is near or within the limits determined for an eclipse. 

An eclipse always happens at the full moon or thereabouts. Because the moon is not in the head or tail of the dragon at every opposition—that is, at every full moon—nor positioned directly under the nadir of the sun, it is not necessary for it to suffer an eclipse at every full moon, as is clear in the present figure which follows below.

<summary>This page defines the mechanics of planetary epicycles, explaining why planets appear to stop or move backward (retrograde), and details the geometric causes of lunar eclipses occurring at the lunar nodes.</summary>
<keywords>epicycle, deferent, stationary, direct motion, retrograde, lunar eclipse, nadir, lunar nodes, head of the dragon, tail of the dragon, full moon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of lunar eclipses. Having established how the Earth's shadow causes a lunar eclipse, the text now transitions to the mechanics of a solar eclipse, specifically focusing on the positioning of the moon at the "nodes" or "dragon's head and tail."</meta>

->**Lunar Eclipse**<-
<note>The diagram shows the Sun with a face on the right, the Earth in the center casting a shadow, and the Moon with a face on the left entering that shadow.</note>

->**Solar Eclipse**<-
<note>The diagram shows the Sun on the right, the Moon in the center blocking the Sun's light, and an observer or the Earth on the left.</note>

¶ However, when the moon is in the **head or tail of the dragon** <note>original: "capite vel cauda draconis"; these are the lunar nodes, the two points where the moon's orbit intersects the ecliptic (the sun's apparent path). Eclipses can only occur when the moon is near these points</note>, or near the boundaries mentioned above, and is in **conjunction** <term>alignment</term> with the sun, then the body of the moon is placed between our sight and the solar body. Therefore, it will shade the brightness of the sun for us, and so the sun suffers an **eclipse**. This happens not because the sun actually loses its light, but because it is hidden from us due to the **interposition of the moon** between our sight and the sun. From this, it is clear that a solar eclipse does not always occur during a conjunction or at the **new moon** <note>original: "novilunio"</note>.

¶ It should also be noted that when there is a lunar eclipse, the eclipse is visible from every part of the earth; but when there is a solar eclipse, this is by no means the case. Instead, a solar eclipse occurs in one **climate** <note>original: "climate"; in medieval geography and astronomy, a 'climate' refers to a specific latitudinal zone of the habitable world</note> and not in another. This happens...

<summary>This page explains the astronomical cause of a solar eclipse, noting that it is a localized visual phenomenon caused by the moon blocking the sun, unlike a lunar eclipse which is visible globally.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Johannes de Sacrobosco’s "De sphaera mundi." It finishes the explanation of solar eclipses by discussing parallax and then addresses the famous theological exception: the eclipse reported during the crucifixion of Christ. It ends with a formal colophon.</meta>

...due to the **diversity of parallax** <term>aspectus; the difference in the apparent position of a celestial body when viewed from different points on Earth</term> in different **climates** <note>latitudinal zones</note>. Whence **Virgil** most elegantly touched upon the natures of both eclipses in a summary, saying: “The various failings of the moon and the labors of the sun.” <note>original: "Defectus lune uarios solisqȝ labores"; a quote from Virgil's *Georgics*, Book II. To the ancients, an eclipse was often described as the sun or moon "toiling" or suffering.</note> From the aforesaid, it is clear that since a solar eclipse occurred during the **Passion of the Lord** <note>the crucifixion of Jesus Christ</note>, and that same Passion occurred at the **full moon**, that solar eclipse was not natural; rather, it was miraculous and contrary to nature. This is because a solar eclipse ought to happen only at the **new moon** <note>original: "nouilunio"</note> or very near to it. For this reason, it is read that **Dionysius the Areopagite** <note>A traditional figure believed to be a witness to the eclipse from Heliopolis; medieval scholars used his testimony to prove the miraculous nature of the darkness during the Crucifixion</note> said during that same Passion: “Either the God of nature suffers, or the **machine of the world** <term>mundi machina; the complex, interlocking celestial spheres that govern the universe</term> is being dissolved.”

The Little Work on the Sphere by **Johannes de Sacrobosco** is finished.

# ¶ The Preface of Johannes Regiomontanus to his Disputations against the Deliriums of the "Theories of the Planets" by Gerard of Cremona.
## Johannes Regiomontanus sends heartiest greetings to all students of the liberal arts.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'P' featuring intricate white foliate and vine patterns on a dark stippled background, enclosed within a square double-line border.</image-desc>

After we published an index of the works that we shall entrust to our printing presses <note>original: "librarijs nostris"; here referring to his pioneering scientific printing office in Nuremberg</note> to be produced, it soon seemed worthy of reproach to some—who, as I have heard, are inflamed more by a desire to hurt than to help—that we attempt to change the works of certain writers, and to reject others entirely by introducing new translations. Furthermore, they complain that we do not fear to contradict many upright and ancient authors, nor to wipe away the commentaries of certain more recent ones, even naming them explicitly—which, as those critics think, is a bitter thing to do. 

To you, however, who would rather study ready and correct copies than either compose new ones or copy out faulty ones, it must not be denied at all that this was done willingly and by design. This was not to diminish the authority of another, but so that the studies of **mathematics**—which for centuries now have been defiled in many ways and almost abandoned by everyone—might be wiped clean of every stain as far as possible and brought to light. This, indeed, must be done both by the duty of changing many things and by translating them anew. 

Moreover, we believe it to be the mark of a **just man** and a **free mind** <note>original: "liberalis ingenij"; referring to the "liberal" or free-thinking nature of a true scholar</note> to contradict any ancient writers if they have erred as humans do, prompted by the example of almost all those who have ever composed anything new. Finally, that we have not spared the names of writers ought not to seem out of place, since certain poor souls, captured by excessive credulity, grant so much to the festive titles of books and the antiquity of authors that whenever they are about to dispute any matter, they think the highest and most valid point of argument must always be borrowed from **authority** <note>The medieval "scholastic" method relied heavily on citing established authors; Regiomontanus is advocating for a more "modern" approach based on mathematical proof rather than reputation</note>—that is, trusting more in someone else’s assertion, whatever it may be...

<summary>This preface marks a turning point in scientific history, as Regiomontanus defends his decision to use the new technology of the printing press to correct and even discard centuries-old astronomical errors found in traditional textbooks. He argues that mathematical truth should supersede the "authority" of ancient or famous authors.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Regiomontanus, Gerard of Cremona, printing press, mathematical reform, astronomical theory, Renaissance humanism, critique of authority, liberal arts</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues Regiomontanus's critique of the poor state of mathematical education in his time. He argues that the works of ancient authors are often treated with uncritical reverence simply because they are old, while the actual science has stagnated due to the laziness of teachers and the corruption of manuscripts.</meta>

...to things which are most certain to reason. The death of men who have composed something in their lives brings about a certain singular indulgence: so that those whom we might perhaps have neglected while they were still alive, we embrace more religiously now that their lives are ended. Either this is because it is not permitted to oppose their opinions—lest it be believed to happen through envy or insolence—or it is because we are reluctant to examine and more shrewdly judge the views of others, since that can rarely be done without great labor. Hence, I would believe it has happened that many literary studies have taken on the appearance of some dream or an old woman's tale because of overly careless reading and submissive commentaries.

But truly, even if that infection is common to almost all **liberal studies**, in **mathematics** it is altogether shameful and intolerable. For these sciences, which by the confession of everyone always bear a perpetual certainty, have been boiled down to a certain dreg by the laziness of our age. This has gone so far that in the <term>science of the stars</term><note>The author uses "scientia siderali" to refer to the combined field of astronomy and astrology, which were not strictly separated at this time.</note>—it would be tedious to list them all—we neglect almost all authors except for **Gerard of Cremona** and **Johannes de Sacrobosco** <note>original: "Joannem de Sacro Busto"; the 13th-century author of the standard textbook "The Sphere."</note>. Indeed, we are celebrated as astronomers simply because we have seen their commentaries—namely the <term>Theories of the Planets</term> and the <term>Material Sphere</term>, as they call them.

But when we have also touched upon some beginnings of numbers, tables, and predictions, then at last we are believed to be perfect in every way. From this, some of us are assigned to public lectures, clearly to produce students who are just like we teachers are. Others are summoned to the consultations of princes; strengthened by their applause, we do not blush to pour out our ravings before the public and the common crowd. It is truly shameful to recount how much insult often comes to us from this—and indeed not undeservedly, since because of our blindness we are heralds of our own stupid ineptitude. 

But these things, since the penalty attached to them is lighter, need less criticism than the practice of those who act less discreetly in correcting copies of sciences, however recondite. For this, unless I am mistaken, is a crime: to obscure the opinions of noble authors by substituting the infections of one's own ignorance, and to infect posterity with corrupted copies of books. For who does not know that the wonderful **art of printing** <note>original: "mirificam illam formandi artem"; literally "that wonderful art of forming," referring to the recent invention of the printing press by Gutenberg and others.</note> recently devised by our countrymen, is of so much harm to mortals if it produces faulty [texts]...

<summary>Regiomontanus criticizes the decline of astronomy and mathematics, blaming the reliance on outdated textbooks like Sacrobosco and the "deliriums" of uneducated teachers. He warns that the new technology of printing, while wonderful, is dangerous if used to spread incorrect or corrupted scientific texts.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Regiomontanus, Gerard of Cremona, Johannes de Sacrobosco, printing press, astronomy, mathematical education, scientific accuracy, Nuremberg</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the importance of textual accuracy in the early printing industry, specifically regarding geographical works. The author is criticizing the "correctors" (editors) employed by printing houses.</meta>

...that the volumes of books might be disseminated, it is of great benefit that the copies be properly corrected. I cannot restrain myself from offering one example of a rather bold "corrector" who recently presented a Latin translation of Strabo’s *Geography* <note>Strabo (64 BCE–24 CE) was a Greek geographer whose work was rediscovered and translated during the Renaissance.</note>—made some time ago—to Roman booksellers to be printed. I would rather laugh at the situation than record in writing the overconfidence of such a pseudo-intellectual.

In the third volume, where the text discusses the maximum length of the day for those living between Rome and Naples, this man claims the longest day consists of fifteen "solstitial hours" <note>original: *horarum solstitialium*</note>. Finding this term "solstitial" used repeatedly there, he reveals his own lack of knowledge and lack of culture; in a single word, he exposes a double ignorance. 

For while the Greek author <note>Strabo</note> uses the term "equinoctial hours" <note>original: *ωρῶν ἰσημερινῶν* (*horōn isēmerinōn*)</note>—which in Latin is *horarum equinoctialium*—that fool wondered how "equinoctial" hours could possibly exist on the day of a solstice, given that the equinox and the solstice are very different things. Therefore, he renamed those hours after the solstice, completely ignorant of why hours are called "equinoctial" even when they are counted on the day of a summer or winter solstice <note>The author is mocking the editor for not knowing that "equinoctial hours" is a technical term for our standard 60-minute hours, as opposed to "seasonal hours" which varied in length.</note>. 

Surely no one would attribute such a fault to the translator Guarino <note>Guarino da Verona (1374–1460), a famous humanist scholar whose translations were highly regarded.</note>. For earlier, not far from the start of the second volume, he notes that the longest day among the Britons consists of nineteen equinoctial hours. He would not say "solstitial" like this petty grammarian; rather, he would derive the adjective *solstitiale* from "solstice" only by imitating the poet Lucan <note>A Roman poet (39–65 CE) known for his epic *Pharsalia*.</note> when he wrote of "the solstitial head of the swift Lion" <note>Referring to the sun entering the zodiac sign of Leo during the summer solstice.</note>.

Do you really trust such a "corrector"—or rather, a "corrupter"—with manuscripts that are already difficult and full of obstacles to be amended, my friend? And what, I ask, if the original manuscript was already marred by the translator's negligence, or wrongly altered by some "hungry" <note>Literally "famished," implying a struggling or unscrupulous worker doing a rushed job for pay.</note> bookseller? 

Indeed, both of these faults can be seen in the work currently circulating as the *Geography* of Claudius Ptolemy. In that version, the literal text does not match the Greek author's meaning because of the poor translation by Jacopo d'Angelo of Florence <note>Jacopo d'Angelo (c. 1360–1411); his 1406 translation of Ptolemy was the standard version but was often criticized by later humanists for its errors.</note>; nor do the maps of individual provinces retain the form established by Ptolemy, having instead suffered frivolous alterations by some starving hack. Therefore, whoever...

<summary>The author critiques the incompetence of contemporary editors and translators, specifically mocking a "corrector" of Strabo who misunderstood technical astronomical terms and slamming Jacopo d'Angelo’s flawed translation of Ptolemy's Geography.</summary>

<keywords>Strabo, Guarino da Verona, Jacopo d'Angelo, Claudius Ptolemy, book history, printing errors, equinoctial hours, geography, Renaissance humanism, Lucan</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of poorly edited geographical texts. After mocking a specific editor's misunderstanding of "equinoctial hours" in Strabo's work, the author now turns his attention to the state of Ptolemy's *Geography* and defends his own right to critique established scholars despite his modest circumstances in Germany.</meta>

...will think he possesses the *Cosmography* <note>original: *Cosmographiam*; in the Renaissance, Ptolemy's *Geography* was frequently titled *Cosmographia*.</note> of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 CE) was the most influential geographer of antiquity.</note>, yet he will not be able to show even a shadow of such a great work. And no one will fail to believe me when I say briefly that this work has not yet been translated into Latin—especially if one learns that because of its difficulty, it was for a long time lost even among the Greeks and would have completely perished had it not been recovered by the vigilance of a certain monk named Maximus <note>Maximus Planudes (c. 1260–1305) was a Byzantine monk and scholar who famously rediscovered a manuscript of Ptolemy’s *Geography* and reconstructed its maps.</note>. But these things will be discussed in a fuller treatise elsewhere.

Now returning to where I left off: lest I—while censuring the faults of others—seem to exempt myself from that ridiculous flock of astronomers as if I were innocent and liable to no error, I now declare that I will bear criticism equally. Indeed, I will be deeply grateful to almost everyone who inspects my editions and judges them, however maliciously. Even though I know, by the advice of Horace and Quintilian <note>Horace in *Ars Poetica* and Quintilian in *Institutio Oratoria* famously advised that writers should not rush to publish, suggesting they set work aside for several years to ensure its quality.</note>, that they should not be rushed, nevertheless something must be attempted in the vigor of my youth, lest I seem to indulge only my belly after the manner of cattle.

I suspect, however, there will be some who will charge me with the crime of insolence: that I, living in Germany—not to say in a land of barbarians—lacking books, and removed from the company of learned men, should dare to attack so many celebrated figures. But these people—unless my mind deceives me—will grant me pardon if they weigh the goal of my purpose rather than the status of the writer or his fortune. For, so that everyone may more freely and fully inspect, judge, correct, and revise my attempts, behold, I offer myself and my multiple interpretations <note>original: *interpretamento*; likely referring to his translations or commentaries.</note> to the public, not fearing to try my luck for the sake of the Republic of Letters <note>original: *republica litteraria*; the international community of scholars and intellectuals.</note>.

And let this present little midnight-oil production <note>original: *lucubratiuncula*; a diminutive of *lucubratio*, meaning work done by lamplight at night.</note> be like a foretaste of the universal commentary through which we shall spend the remainder of our life—whatever amount God grants—as its measure. Finally, I urge benevolent readers to examine our efforts according to their own ability—not without reward, unless someone wishes to neglect the celebration of their own name. Which I certainly promise to do in our works when it is appropriate <note>The author is offering to credit those who help him correct his work.</note>. To my rivals, however, no small pleasure will come if they catch a man who dared to attempt such unusual things in an error. But lest we make too long a preface, let us begin to proceed.

<summary>The author explains the rarity of Ptolemy's work, rediscovered by Maximus Planudes, and defends his own right as a young German scholar to publish and critique established texts for the benefit of the scholarly community.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Maximus Planudes, Germany, Republic of Letters, textual criticism, Horace, Quintilian, Geography</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's transition from critiquing geographical works to addressing astronomical texts. Having previously discussed the errors in Latin translations of Ptolemy, the author now introduces a famous textbook on planetary motion and recounts his own experiences in Rome following the death of Pope Pius II.</meta>

The *Theories of the Planets* <term>Theories of the Planets (*Theoricae Planetarum*): A standard medieval astronomical textbook that explained the mathematical models of how planets move.</term>, published—as it is said—by Gerard of Cremona <note>Gerard of Cremona (c. 1114–1187) was a prolific translator of scientific works from Arabic into Latin.</note>, has for a long time now been read in all general universities. It is a slim work indeed, yet it has been trusted and approved by many great minds. Everywhere you may encounter many "insane" commentators on it, who attempt to bolster their own errors with geometric proofs. They will soon understand how fruitlessly they have spent their sleepless nights when they see a dialogue that was snatched from our hands long ago. We once composed this work for sport in the city of Rome, and now, through its explanation, we greet all students of the science of the stars.

->The Viennese Student &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; The Cracovian Student<-
->.A. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; .L.<-

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'S' decorated with complex intertwining floral and foliate motifs, contained within a square border.</image-desc>

If anyone should by chance ask why I have come this morning especially to this shrine of the holy Apostle Peter, let him know first of all that I have come to pray that, since Pius <note>Pope Pius II (reigned 1458–1464), a renowned humanist scholar before his papacy, had recently died.</note> is deceased, a pontiff may succeed him who has sincere religion at heart. I pray for one who dares to oppose Muhammad <note>original: *mahumeto*; referring to Sultan Mehmed II of the Ottoman Empire, who was the primary external threat to Christendom at the time.</note>, the enemy of the Christian name, and who—once foreign enemies have finally been crushed—might strive to remove our internal discords. My hope is that at last, with all ambition and wicked greed extinguished, good morals may be resumed anew, and hereafter all the best arts may return to the light—arts which seem to be neglected in this turbulent age of ours. For, to pass over other kinds of studies in silence, the liberal arts urge us on, especially those which they call the mathematical arts:

<summary>The author introduces a critique of Gerard of Cremona's astronomical textbook and a dialogue set in Rome, while also offering a prayer at St. Peter's for a worthy successor to the late Pope Pius II.</summary>
<keywords>Theoricae Planetarum, Gerard of Cremona, astronomy, Rome, Pope Pius II, Mehmed II, mathematics, liberal arts, Vienna, Krakow</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between two students, Johannes (the Viennese student) and his friend (the Cracovian student), set in Rome during the transition between popes. They discuss the corruption of astronomical science and the current political atmosphere surrounding the papal election.</meta>

...which have so miserably faded from the minds of mortals that today you find very few who have followed them with sufficient learning. Most people, however—and this is miserable to say—can hardly bear even to hear their names with a calm mind, considering nothing pleasant except that which satisfies the accursed hunger for gold <note>original: "auri sacram famem," a famous phrase from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (3.57) referring to the corrupting power of greed.</note>. Meanwhile, however, that which promises knowledge of the stars affects some men; not indeed for the exercise of the mind, as would be proper, but for the sake of glory or profit. These men, neglecting the proper order while they hurry too much to predict the future <note>Here, the author distinguishes between technical astronomy (mathematical motions) and judicial astrology (predicting the future for money).</note>, pass over almost the entirety of astronomy which concerns the motions of the heavens. As a result, they are commonly held up to ridicule everywhere. And not unjustly so, since they do not strive to abstain from their own errors, the causes of which they do not even know. But who is calling me? The great crowd does not allow me to distinguish the person.

**L.** I bid you greetings once again, excellent Johannes.

**A.** I am glad you have arrived safely, my sweetest friend.

**L.** I greeted you a while ago, but you didn't respond. How are things going?

**A.** I am just walking about <note>original: "Deambulatur," meaning to stroll or pace, common in academic or philosophical dialogue.</note>. But what business do you have here?

**L.** I am waiting.

**A.** Oh, lucky you, who have the grace of waiting! For they say this is the path to attaining ecclesiastical wealth.

**L.** Haha. You too will be blessed for that reason, you who linger so much at the sacred shrine.

**A.** May God grant it. But right now I am waiting for the Nicene <note>This refers to Cardinal Bessarion (1403–1472), the Titular Bishop of Nicaea, who was a great patron of Johannes Regiomontanus.</note>, my master; not, so to speak, for favors.

**L.** But I am waiting for my master, who is to be a guard at the conclave <term>Conclave: The locked meeting of cardinals gathered to elect a new Pope; historically, participants were physically "locked in" until a decision was reached.</term>.

**A.** You, then, will be hidden in that prison for these three days; you will have this advantage, at least: that the election of the new Pope will be known to you as soon as possible.

**L.** And what then?

**A.** So that from there you can reveal his fortunes.

**L.** Correct indeed; such a door is believed to be the way to explore those things.

**A.** Others think rather that one should observe the day on which he sits upon the Apostolic throne, crowned with the triple crown <note>The Papal Tiara, representing the three-fold authority of the Pope.</note>. But let us put those things aside. God must be prayed to most of all, so that He may breathe upon those beginnings; and one must devoutly beseech Him to command Mercury <note>In astrological terms, Mercury governs communication, intellect, and trade; the speaker hopes for a "happy ray" or favorable influence for the new papacy.</note> to temper his people with a happy ray.

**L.** I agree that should be done. But to return to our usual and more pleasant conversations, you remind me opportunely when you mention Mercury.

**A.** What has come to mind, I ask?

**L.** The common *Theories of the Planets* <term>Theories of the Planets (*Theoricae Planetarum*): A standard medieval astronomical textbook that explained the mathematical models of how planets move.</term> have returned to my mind: especially that passage where it speaks of proportional minutes <term>Proportional minutes: Small fractions used in astronomical calculations to determine planetary positions between set intervals in printed tables.</term>...

...is dealt with concerning the motions of Mercury. For the meaning of that passage by no means seems to correspond to the numerical tables.

**A.** Whether they ought to be read or rather neglected, someone else shall judge shortly.

**L.** Then I was a fool to have placed such faith in them in vain, seeing that I have read them so many times with such great attention.

**A.** You have fulfilled the duty of an excellent man; for thanks should be given not only to those who speak well, but also to those who err. For through the latter we are made more cautious, while through the former we are made better.

**L.** By Hercules, until now, as I look around at all these commentaries which attempt to explain these *Theories*, they note nothing erroneous, and indeed nothing foolishly said.

**A.** If the author himself published anything somewhat obscure in his writings, or perhaps taught unlearnedly, it is the duty of a commentator to illuminate the former more fully, but the latter must be explained with proper moderation. I wish the text of the *Theories* were given to us, so that we might examine each point more systematically.

**L.** We have a friend nearby from whom it will be necessary to seek it.

**A.** Go then quickly to seek it. <note>L exits to find the book.</note> I see that I have undertaken a risky task today, since I dare to re-examine <note>original: "retractare," implying a critical revision or challenging of a text.</note> the writings of another that have long been held as indubitable. But wherever there is need, our Euclid and Ptolemy <note>The foundational authorities of geometry and astronomy, respectively.</note> will stand by as advocates. And if it is necessary to take testimony from elsewhere, a wealth of sources will provide it, so I hope. For in this way, unless my mind deceives me, the license for slandering will be taken away from many. Unless this were done, I would fear that many would speak against me without reason. Perhaps they would say: "Why does this newcomer arrogate so much to himself that he does not blush to challenge an ancient author?" But I see my friend returning. Where is the little book we were seeking?

**L.** Here it is.

**A.** It is enough. Now I think we should begin with small matters, which could be tolerated to some extent if they were interpreted with a certain moderation. After a few introductory remarks, it says: "The mean..."

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Johannes (A) and his friend (L). They are currently critiquing the specific geometric definitions found in the "Theorica Planetarum," particularly focusing on the Sun's motion and the inaccuracies in how the "mean motion" is geometrically represented in that text.</meta>

"...that motion of the Sun is called the arc of the zodiac falling between the line going out from the center of the earth to the firmament—I mean a line parallel to the line going out from the center of the eccentric through the center of the body of the sun to the zodiac."

**L.** What do you note in these words?

**A.** It is a small thing: he unnecessarily extends the radius of the eccentric, which ends at the center of the sun, all the way to the zodiac. For this line, when extended to the zodiac, designates neither the mean place of the sun nor the uniform velocity of its motion in the zodiac. Instead, it is sufficient for the line to be drawn from the center of the eccentric and terminated at the center of the sun. For this line declares the velocity of the sun in its own eccentric; and then one may draw a line parallel to it from the center of the world <note>In the Ptolemaic system, the "center of the world" refers to the Earth.</note>.

**L.** That note seems small.

**A.** Hahaha! And it is small in truth.

**L.** Why do you laugh when you mention the truth?

**A.** A certain "hungry little Greek" <note>original: "gręculus," a derogatory diminutive often used by Renaissance humanists to mock contemporary Byzantine scholars or their perceived pedantry</note> came to mind, whom this line, extended from the center of the eccentric through the center of the sun to the zodiac, drove to madness. For he says that the mean motion of the sun in the zodiac is terminated at such a line.

**L.** Can anyone hold an opinion so rashly? Especially since this line does not cut off equal arcs from the zodiac in equal times—which is the proper function of the line of mean motion <note>Mean motion must, by definition, move at a constant rate; L is pointing out that a line from an eccentric center would not appear to move at a constant rate when projected onto the zodiac from the Earth's perspective.</note>. But I would like to know this man.

**A.** He will be made known to you after this. For now, just continue the reading you have started.

**L.** Pay attention then: "**Which equation** <term>equation: in historical astronomy, this is the 'correction' or difference added to or subtracted from the mean motion to find the true position of a planet</term> **is zero when the sun is in the apogee or opposite the apogee; but when it is in the middle lengths, it is at its maximum.**" What, I ask, do you understand by the term "middle length"?

**A.** By "longer length," Ptolemy understands the line which extends from the center of the world to the apogee <term>apogee: also called the 'aux,' the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</term> of the eccentric. By the "nearer length," he understands the line stretched from the center of the world to the opposite of the apogee <note>The perigee, or the point closest to Earth.</note>. Sometimes, however, he calls the points on the eccentric that terminate these lines the "longer" and "nearer" lengths. But he is accustomed to call the "middle length" the line going out from the center of the world to the circumference of the eccentric that is equal to the radius of the eccentric, or the point on the eccentric that terminates such a line. It is called...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Johannes (A) and his friend (L). They are discussing the geometric definitions of the Sun's orbit, specifically critiquing how "mean distance" and "maximum equation" are defined in astronomical textbooks compared to Ptolemy’s original work.</meta>

...however, the **mean distance** <note>In an eccentric orbit, this is the distance from the Earth to the Sun when the Sun is exactly at its average distance, halfway between its closest and farthest points.</note> is so called because it exceeds the nearer distance by the same amount that it is itself exceeded by the farther distance. Anyone who understands the mean distance of the eccentric otherwise departs from the mind of Ptolemy. To enable the point of this type of mean distance to be determined, a diagram must be used. 

Let circle *a.b.c*, drawn upon center *d*, represent the eccentric of the Sun. On its diameter *a.c*, let the **farther** <note>apogee</note> and **nearer** <note>perigee</note> distances be located. Let the center of the world <note>the Earth</note> be *e*. With the eccentricity *d.e* divided in the middle at point *f*, let a perpendicular line *f.b* be drawn from *f* to the diameter *a.c*. I say that its endpoint *b* is the mean distance of the eccentric. 

For when the two lines *b.d* and *b.e* are extended, the two sides *b.f* and *f.d* of triangle *b.d.f* will be equal to the two sides *b.f* and *f.e* of triangle *b.e.f*. Since both angles at *f* are right angles, therefore, by the fourth proposition of the first book of **Euclid’s** *Elements* <note>The SAS (Side-Angle-Side) theorem of congruence.</note>, the line *b.e* will be equal to the radius *b.d* of the eccentric itself. 

Furthermore, the line *e.a* (that is, of the farther distance) exceeds the radius *a.d* of the eccentric—and thus exceeds the line *b.e*—by the amount of the eccentricity *d.e*. Likewise, the radius *d.c* (and therefore the line *b.d* <note>The text reads *b.c*, likely a scribal error for the radius *b.d*</note> which is equal to it) exceeds the nearer distance *e.c* by that same eccentricity. 

<image-desc>A circular astronomical diagram showing an eccentric orbit. A vertical diameter is labeled 'a' at the top and 'c' at the bottom. The center of the circle is marked 'd'. Below 'd' on the same vertical line are points 'f' and 'e' (the center of the world). A horizontal line connects 'f' to point 'b' on the left side of the circumference. Lines also connect point 'b' to 'd' and 'e'. Another point 'g' is marked on the lower left of the circumference.</image-desc>

Therefore, when the Sun occupies point *b*, it will be said to exist at the **mean distance** of the eccentric according to the mind of Ptolemy. 

**L.** That pleases me. But I shall continue with the text unless you wish to add something else. 

**A.** Wait a moment, until I have drawn a perpendicular line *e.g* from the center of the world *e* to the diameter of the eccentric *a.c*, meeting the circumference of the eccentric at point *g*. 

**L.** Proceed as you please. 

**A.** Our Ptolemy demonstrated that the greatest **diversity** <term>diversity: also known as the "equation of the center," this is the difference between where the Sun appears to be and where it would be if its motion were perfectly uniform</term> in its motion happens to the Sun when it is at point *g*. 

**L.** Therefore, we shall not find the maximum equation of the Sun at point *b* (the mean distance), just as...

<summary>Johannes uses Euclidean geometry and a diagram to define the Sun's "mean distance" on an eccentric orbit, distinguishing it from the point of "maximum diversity" (equation of the center).</summary>

...as that good author of the *Theoricae* <note>Referring to the widely used medieval textbook *Theorica Planetarum Communis*, often attributed to Gerard of Cremona.</note> used to assert. **A.** You conclude correctly. ...If that man had seen this, he would have reasonably defined the **proportional minutes** <term>proportional minutes: fractions used to adjust planetary positions when a planet is not at its furthest or closest point from Earth</term> as being the sixtieth parts of that excess—not indeed of two lines, as is his custom, but of the two **maximum equations of the argument** <term>equation of the argument: the angular correction required due to a planet's movement on its epicycle</term>. He says, "A certain circle, concentric with the world and existing in the heavens, equal in size to the Moon's eccentric, carries these intersections; and it lies in the surface of the **circle of signs** <note>the Zodiac</note> or in the path of the Sun." For even if I easily understand these two intersections, their motion does not seem possible by means of a circle concentric to the world as he claims. Therefore, it is no wonder why so few have a full knowledge of the **planetary theories** <note>original: *theoricarum*; likely referring to the *Theorica Planetarum*, the standard textbook being critiqued here</note>, since the matter itself has been handed down in such a diminished way. Enough now regarding the **luminaries** <note>the Sun and the Moon</note>; now we must move on to the **three superior planets** <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>. Each of these, he says, "has two equal eccentric circles arranged in the same flat surface, which are immobile except for the movement of the **eighth sphere** <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note> and the daily motion around the Earth from east to west." What do you think of this text, my friend?

**L.** He asserts that neither of the two circles moves except for the motion of the eighth sphere and the **first mover** <term>first mover: the *primum mobile*, the outermost sphere that drives the 24-hour rotation of the heavens</term>. I judge this to be far from the truth, since one of them is supposed to carry the epicycle toward the east.

**A.** Hear other words from this man. He says: "The center of the epicycle is at the **mean distance** of the **equant** <term>equant: a mathematical point used to explain the varying speeds of planets; it was controversial because it violated the principle of perfectly uniform circular motion</term> when the diameter of the epicycle stands perpendicular to the world-diameter that passes through the centers of the eccentrics." **L.** You are right. For who would not rather adhere to the opinion of the most famous Ptolemy than to the foolishness of this man?

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Johannes (A) and Lucas (L) as they critique the standard astronomical textbooks of the day. Having previously discussed the Moon's motion, they now move to the complex problem of Mercury, the planet with the most difficult orbit to model in the pre-telescopic era.</meta>

And since these three lines move equally, they will always be parallel: either they will all be one line, or two will be the same, and so on. He <note>Referring to the author of the textbook they are critiquing</note> designated two lines, one of which stretches from the center of the **eccentric** <term>eccentric: a circular orbit where the Earth is not at the exact center</term> through the center of the Sun; the other is extended from the center of the **equant** <term>equant: an imaginary mathematical point used to explain the varying speeds of planets; a planet appears to move at a constant speed only when viewed from this point</term> of Mercury through the center of its **epicycle** <term>epicycle: a small circle on which a planet moves, while the center of that small circle itself moves along a larger circle (the deferent)</term>; and a third line outgoing from the center of the world and parallel to the two aforementioned lines.

**L.** Why do you think this passage should be disregarded?

**A.** Because those lines are never parallel as he asserts, except when the center of Mercury’s epicycle is in one of the two **nodes** <term>nodes: the two points where a planet's tilted orbit crosses the path of the Sun (the ecliptic)</term>, which usually happens twice in the space of a solar year. Only then are the two eccentric circles of the Sun and Mercury—and therefore the aforementioned lines—placed in a single flat surface <note>a geometric plane</note>. This is required for the lines to be parallel. However, when the center of the epicycle is located anywhere else, those lines cannot be parallel because they do not exist in the same flat surface. The line of the **mean motion** <note>the average speed of a planet, ignoring its minor fluctuations</note> of the Sun is always one thing, and that of Mercury is another, except when the line of the Sun's mean motion is at the common intersection of the ecliptic and the eccentric of Mercury, or is contained within the surface of Mercury's eccentric itself.

**L.** This is a profound insight, so much so that no one can fully understand this matter if they are ignorant of how intersecting surfaces relate to one another.

**A.** A little further down, his own words summarize it this way: "The center of the epicycle, the **apogee** <note>original: *aux*; the point in an orbit furthest from the center</note> of the eccentric, and any point on the eccentric always describe equal angles around the center of the equant in equal times."

**L.** What about that? Does the center of the epicycle not have the rule of its motion in the center of the equant?

**A.** That is indeed how it stands.

**L.** And it is believed that what happens to the center of the epicycle also happens to the other points of the eccentric—namely, that they move equally around the center of the equant.

**A.** Our geometry will demonstrate that this does not happen for the other points of the eccentric.

**L.** I shall listen gladly.

**A.** On line *a.c.*, let there be the center of the equant. Let *g* be the center of a **small circle** <note>In Mercury's model, the center of the eccentric moves on its own small circle to account for its unique orbit</note>, and *f* the point upon which that small circle *e.m.g.* is described. We will now place the center of the eccentric carrying the epicycle at the top of the small circle—specifically at point *e*—and upon it the eccentric itself, *a.b.d.* Now it will be shown that point *c* of the eccentric (which marks the end of the eccentric's diameter passing through the center of the epicycle) moves irregularly around the center of the equant.

**L.** Why did you choose point *c* specifically and not another?

<meta>This page continues the technical dialogue between Johannes (A) and Lucas (L).
