**A.** So that the process may be shorter and easier to understand, we can demonstrate the same concerning the remaining points, although it may be more difficult. **L.** Proceed, then, as you have begun. **A.** Next, let it be that the center of the **eccentric** <term>eccentric: a circular orbit where the Earth is not at the exact center</term> carrying the **epicycle** <term>epicycle: a small circle on which a planet moves, while the center of that small circle itself moves along a larger circle (the deferent)</term> <note>original: "eccentrici deferentis epicyclum"</note> reaches point **m**, the middle of the small western semicircle, by the law of its own motion. This should be done so that the radius of the small circle **f-m** is perpendicular to the line **a-c**. Once **m** is made the center, let the eccentric **k-b-l-d** be described again. Finally, from the center of the **equant** <term>equant: a mathematical point used to explain the varying speeds of planets; a planet appears to move at a constant speed only when viewed from this point</term> **g**, let line **g-k** emerge, making right angles to the line **a-c** and meeting the circumference of the eccentric at point **k**, in which it will be necessary to find the center of the epicycle.

**L.** That is so. For the center of the eccentric is carried around the center of the small circle, and the center of the epicycle around the center of the equant, equally in opposite directions.

**A.** Likewise, let a line be drawn from point **k** coming to point **l** of the diameter of the eccentric, and another line **l-g**. Therefore, in that time in which the center of the epicycle was moved from **a** to **k**, the point diametrically opposite to it has come from **c** to **l**; and for that reason, it has described the angle **c-g-l** at the center of the equant.

**L.** I do not deny it.

**A.** Let the center of the epicycle proceed again until it is led to the opposite of the **apogee** <term>apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from the center of motion</term> <note>original: "augis"</note> of the equant; and therefore the center of the eccentric should coincide with the center of the equant, which is **g**. Finally, it must happen that the point of the eccentric diametrically opposite the center of the epicycle is found in the upper part of line **a-c**.

**L.** I certainly admit it.

**A.** In the second period of time, then, the center of the epicycle described a right angle around the center of the equant, just as in the first; and therefore those times must have been equal.

**L.** There is no doubt.

**A.** In the second time also, that other point described the angle **l-g-a** at the center of the equant. Thus, in two equal times, [it described] two angles, **c-g-l** and **l-g-a**, around the center—

...described at the equant. <note>original: "equantis descripsit"</note> **C.** Of course. But I would like you to show that those two angles are equal. **A.** I will demonstrate this by example. For in triangle $k. g. l.$, we established the specific angle $k. g. f.$ as a right angle. Therefore, the remaining specific angle $f. g. l.$ will be less than a right angle—unless you wish to grant that the three angles of a rectilinear triangle can be greater than two right angles. **C.** Not at all, as the thirty-second proposition of the first book of the *Elements* <note>Euclid's *Elements* 1.32 states that the interior angles of a triangle sum to two right angles (180 degrees).</note> forbids it. **A.** Moreover, the two angles $c. g. l.$ and $l. g. a.$ are equal to two right angles, unless the thirteenth proposition of the first book <note>Euclid's *Elements* 1.13: if a straight line stands on another straight line, the adjacent angles equal two right angles.</note> lies. For this reason, angle $c. g. l.$ will be considered greater than a right angle. **C.** That is the center. **A.** Therefore, in equal times, that other point diametrically opposite the center of the epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</term> described unequal angles at the center of the equant <term>equant: a mathematical point used to account for the observed speed changes in planetary orbits</term>. **C.** It is clear, then, that not just any point of the eccentric <term>eccentric: a circular orbit that is not centered on the Earth</term> describes equal angles at the center of the equant in equal times. **A.** You would do me a great favor if you would read the following text; meanwhile, I shall rest for a moment and listen. **C.** Listen, then:

> But there are two places in which the center of the epicycle comes closest to the center of the earth, and it cannot come so close in other places. These two places are the points near the opposite of the apogee <term>apogee (aux): the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</term> of the equant, marking the tangent lines of the small circle upon which the center of the deferent <term>deferent: the large circular path along which the center of the epicycle moves</term> moves, and passing through the center of the earth.

**A.** That is worth noting. But continue as you began. **C.** When, therefore, he says the center of the epicycle is at its apogee, it immediately begins to move toward the east in its deferent, and similarly, the center of the deferent begins to move toward the west in its small circle. **A.** This is true. **C.** And when the center of the epicycle is at the head of the tangent line—which head is near the opposite of the apogee of the equant—then the center of the deferent is at the point of contact of the small circle with that same line. At that time, the apogee of the deferent is at its greatest distance from the apogee of the equant, and then the center of the epicycle is at the opposite of the apogee <note>the perigee, or the point closest to the Earth</note> of the deferent. Therefore, it is then at its closest approach to the earth, because the center of the deferent then descends further in its small circle, and the opposite of the apogee of the deferent is moved further from the center of the earth. This is easily apparent to one looking at the diagram and understanding the motion. And as long as the center of the epicycle remains in that arc of the deferent...

...[the point] which is between the two points or heads of the tangent lines—which heads are near the opposite of the apogee <term>apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth; its opposite is the perigee</term> of the equant—will always be at the opposite of the apogee of the deferent <term>deferent: the large circular path along which the center of a smaller circle (the epicycle) moves</term>. And this follows from the fact that as much as the epicycle travels in one part, the center of the deferent travels an equal amount in the other. Therefore, they will always be on the same line passing through the center of the Earth and through the center of the deferent; and yet the center of the epicycle never approaches the center of the Earth as closely as it does at the heads of the tangent lines.

**A.** Be quiet now. It would have been far better never to have published these theories than to have poured out so many and such great absurdities.

**L.** Is it not true that everything has been well stated?

**A.** They are not.

**L.** Where, I ask, is the error?

**A.** The center of the epicycle, in its mean motion, being distant from the apogee of the equant <term>equant: a mathematical point used to explain the observed speed changes of planets</term> by four common signs <note>original: "signa communia"; in ancient astronomy, a "sign" is a 30-degree arc of the zodiac, so four signs equal 120 degrees</note>, is closer to the center of the world <note>the Earth</note> than when it is distant from that same apogee of the equant by three signs <note>90 degrees</note>, just as was demonstrated in the *Epitome of the Almagest* <note>original: "breviario Almaiesti", referring to the influential astronomical textbook by Regiomontanus</note>.

**L.** What then?

**A.** Then, however, the line contained between the center of the epicycle and the center of the world has 55 parts (where there are 60 in the radius of the eccentric deferent) and furthermore 33 minutes of one part.

**L.** By what reasoning, I pray, do you conclude that?

**A.** The science of plane triangles <term>plane triangles: trigonometry performed on flat surfaces</term> makes all these things clear to us.

**L.** And when the center of the epicycle is at the tangent line, by how many of the aforesaid parts is it removed from the center of the world?

**A.** By 56 parts and approximately 22 minutes.

**L.** How then can it be at its maximum proximity to the center of the world, as he asserts, if it is found to be closer to it in that other mentioned position?

**A.** That good man <note>likely a sarcastic reference to the author of the theory being critiqued</note> has wasted his labor.

**L.** Is there anything else worthy of a black mark <note>original: "nigra dignum litura", a Latin idiom for a correction or a mark of error</note>?

**A.** There is. For he says that the center of the eccentric deferent is at the point of contact while the center of the epicycle is on the tangent line itself.

**L.** How will you demonstrate that this is an error?

**A.** By reducing the claimant to an impossibility <term>reductio ad impossibile: a method of proof which demonstrates that a premise is false because it leads to a logical contradiction</term>. In order that this may be more easily understood, we must proceed with a linear figure <note>a geometric diagram</note>. Let there be, then, on line *a-c*, the point *f*, the center of the small circle *c-b-g*; let point *g* be the center of the equant and *n* be the center of the world. Finally, let the mark *a* represent the apogee of the equant, and *c* the opposite of its apogee. And with the line *k-b* drawn through the center of the world, touching the small circle at point *h*, let the center of the epicycle be understood to be at point

[...at point] k of the tangent line: the center of the eccentric deferent <term>eccentric deferent: a circular path whose center is offset from the center of the Earth</term> is at point b, just as that author has stated. Therefore, by the nineteenth proposition of the first book of the *Elements*, angle n-f-b will be greater than angle f-n-b. Thus, by the fifteenth proposition of the same book, and by applying common knowledge, it will be greater than angle k-n-c. Since this angle [k-n-c] is exterior to triangle k-g-n, it will be greater than angle k-g-n by the sixteenth proposition of the first book. Whence, by common knowledge, angle g-f-b will be greater than angle k-g-n—the very angles we previously concluded were equal!

**L.** It is finished! <note>original: "conclamatum est"; a Latin expression meaning "it is all over" or "the matter is settled"</note> For now you have reduced the proponent to an impossibility, since no quantity can be both equal and unequal to another. with a circle imagined upon center **g** according to the length of line **gn**, its circumference will pass through point **f** because of the equality of the two lines **gn** and **gf**, and through point **b** by the converse of the thirty-third [proposition of Euclid's *Elements*]: since angle **b** is a right angle. For which reason line **gb** will also be a radius <term>radius: half the diameter of a circle; Latin: "semidiameter"</term> of the same circle, equal to the radius **gf**, which is also equal to **fb** itself. Therefore, triangle **fgb** will be equilateral, and so angle **fgb** will be equal to angle **gfb**. But angle **gfb** together with angle **afb** are equal to two right angles by the thirteenth [proposition] of the first [book of Euclid]. Wherefore, by common knowledge, the two angles **fgb** and **agk** will equal two right angles; and therefore, by the reasoning of the fourteenth [proposition] of the first [book], the two lines **bg** and **gk** join together directly and are one line.

**L.** How beautifully you have made me more certain of that which I was seeking, from which we might again be able to refute the opinion <note>original: "sententiam"</note> of this man [Peuerbach]. But are the remaining parts of the text read so far all sound?

For "as long," he says, "as the center of the epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger one</term> is in that arc of the deferent <term>deferent: the large circular path that carries the center of a planet's epicycle</term> which is between the two points where the ends of the tangent lines meet—which ends are near the opposite of the apogee <note>perigee, or the point in an orbit closest to Earth</note> of the equant—it will always be in the opposite of the apogee of the deferent," and so forth.

**L.** I would like to hear by what reasoning you would destroy that [assertion].

**A.** Resume, then, line **ac** with the small circle and the two straight lines touching it, between which the center of the epicycle is understood to be in the region of the opposite of the apogee of the equant: for example <note>original: "verbi gratia"</note>, at point **k**. Let a straight line be drawn through point **k** and the center of the world <note>the Earth</note>, intersecting the circumference of the small circle at point **l**. If, therefore, as he [Peuerbach] asserts, the center of the epicycle is at the opposite of the apogee of the eccentric, it is necessary that the center of the eccentric deferent also be found on line **kl**.

**L.** No one will deny it. For it is always necessary that these four points—the apogee of the eccentric, its center, the center of the world, and the opposite of the apogee—be in [line]...

to be contained in one straight line. **A.** Moreover, the center of the eccentric <term>eccentric: a circular orbit where the Earth is not at the exact center</term> never leaves the circumference of the small circle. For which reason, the center of the carrying eccentric will necessarily be established at point **L**. **L.** What then follows? For whatever you assume is certain. **A.** You will hear immediately, if you first join the center of the equant <term>equant: a mathematical point used to account for the observed changes in speed of planetary motion</term> with the center of the epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle (the deferent)</term> through line **g-k**. **L.** Done. **A.** Now you will resume the syllogism <note>syllogism: a form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</note> made a little earlier: I conclude that line **n-l** is longer than the radius <term>radius: the distance from the center of a circle to its edge; Latin: "semidiametro"</term> of the small circle **f-l**, and therefore angle **l-f-n** is greater than angle **f-n-l**. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Andreas (A) and Leo (L), focusing on a mathematical critique of planetary tables and the positioning of the epicycle's center. It specifically addresses discrepancies in the "equations of the arguments" used to calculate planetary positions, comparing the values in existing astronomical tables against the geometric models proposed by Peuerbach and Ptolemy.</meta>

I would say nothing else except that this argument of his, having no logical form or place, just as it assumes nothing about the center of the world, so it is unable to conclude anything about it. For it would be no less permissible to infer that the center of the epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle</term> and the center of the deferent <term>deferent: the large circular path that carries the epicycle</term> are always in one straight line with any other point of line **a-c**.

**L.** Do you have anything else to say on this matter?

**A.** Now that is enough.

**L.** Therefore, the order [of the text] leads me to the place which struck my mind at the beginning of our conversation.

**A.** What is it?

**L.** "The equations of the arguments," <note>equation of the argument: a correction factor used in ancient astronomy to determine a planet's actual position relative to its mean motion</note> he says, "which are written in the tables, are the equations as if the center of the epicycle had always been at the intersection of the equant <term>equant: a point from which a planet's motion appears uniform</term> circle with the deferent." And a little further down: "Wherefore it is necessary," he says, "that there be three pairs of proportional minutes <term>proportional minutes: small fractions used to adjust calculations when a planet is not at its furthest or closest point from Earth</term>, namely proportional minutes at the furthest distance <note>original: "longitudinem longiorem," referring to the apogee</note>; and they are the excesses of the line going out from the center of the earth to the center of the epicycle while it is at the apogee <note>original: "auge"</note> of the deferent, to the line going out from the same center of the earth to the intersection of the circles: an excess, I say, divided into sixty parts." What do you think of these words?

**A.** The equations of the arguments written in the tables are not computed for such an intersection of circles as he says; but rather according to the mind of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Roman-era mathematician whose geocentric model dominated astronomy for 1,400 years</note> for that position of the center of the epicycle where its distance from the center of the world is equal to the radius <term>radius: semidiameter</term> of the deferent, which he himself divided into sixty equal parts according to his custom. For if you calculate correctly, the maximum equation of the argument which occurs when the center of the epicycle is at the aforementioned intersection does not exceed twenty-one degrees and twenty-five minutes. However, that which the tables have comprises twenty-two degrees and two minutes. For while the center of the epicycle is distant from the apogee of the equant by its mean motion for about fifty-eight degrees and twenty minutes, it is positioned at the intersection of the deferent and equant circles. Then, however, its distance from the center of the world has sixty-one parts (where sixty are in the radius of the deferent) and furthermore thirty-seven minutes of one part. All these things anyone having knowledge of plane triangles will easily admit.

**L.** What about the proportional minutes?

**A.** He errs in two ways: partly because [he takes] those proportional minutes for the excess of the lines—as in the other planets—not for the [excess] of the equations of the argument...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary models, moving from Mercury to Venus and the Sun, and concluding with a debate on the precise definition of lunar eclipse measurements. The text is a dialogue, likely between an instructor (A) and a student (L), typical of the Renaissance humanistic teaching style used by Regiomontanus.</meta>

...considers the maximum or relative proportions: partly because he observes the position of the <term>epicycle</term><note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one; used to explain the complex motions of planets.</note> in the aforementioned intersection when extracting these proportional minutes; since the epicycle should rather be placed in that position where it is sixty parts distant from the center of the world. And to the equations of the <term>arguments</term><note>The angle used to calculate a planet's position within its epicycle.</note> occurring there, the two equations should be referred which usually occur at the maximum and minimum distances of the center of the epicycle from the center of the world. 

But these things are more abundantly explained in our *Breviarium* <note>The "Breviarium" or "Epitome," this very text, which serves as a condensed guide to Ptolemy’s Almagest.</note>. Moreover, my teacher **Georg Peuerbach** <note>original: "Georgius meus Purbachius," a celebrated astronomer and Regiomontanus's mentor.</note> taught most clearly in his *Theories* <note>Referring to the "Theoricae Novae Planetarum," a standard astronomical textbook of the era.</note> how it should be understood that Mercury has triple proportional minutes. But we seem to have played enough with Mercury for now. I think we should now proceed further.

**L.** Indeed, I do not wish to overlook the fact that a little further down he seems to suggest that three lines are parallel: one indeed from the center of the Sun's <term>eccentric</term><note>A circular orbit that is not centered on the Earth.</note> passing through the center of the Sun; another from the center of the <term>equant</term><note>A mathematical point used to explain the observed speed changes in planetary orbits.</note> of **Venus** through the center of her epicycle; and the third which they call the line of mean motion. It is concluded from what you discussed above regarding Mercury that this happens only twice in a solar year.

**A.** Quite right. Finally, turn your attention to reading what follows.

**L.** Everything seems clear.

**A.** Do you think that should be passed over in silence: where he says "Minutes of the case" <note>original: "Minuta casus," also known as minutes of incident; the distance the Moon travels from the start of an eclipse to its midpoint.</note> refers to the minutes of the sky which the **Moon** traverses from the beginning until the middle of the **eclipse**, if the whole is not obscured, and so forth?

**L.** Will you despise this definition? Can't everyone define a thing according to their own judgment?

**A.** The first founders of the arts are permitted to do so; but certainly not the commentators or those who follow. Rather, they ought to conform their definitions and opinions to the primary author.

**L.** Perhaps he should have imitated **Ptolemy**.

**A.** Not only Ptolemy, but also others who define the "minutes of the case" as those which the Moon traverses from the beginning to the middle of the eclipse by overtaking the Sun in a partial eclipse; but in a total eclipse, from the start of the eclipse until the beginning of total obscuration. Thus, they understand the "minutes of the case" to be that excess by which the true motion of the Moon in such a time exceeds the true motion of the Sun, not the simple motion of the Moon itself. This definition corresponds to the mind of Ptolemy in the sixth book, seventh chapter of the *Great Composition* <note>original: "Magna Compositio," the Latin title for Ptolemy’s "Almagest."</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the geometric alignment of the Sun and Venus and debates the technical definition of "minutes of the case" in lunar eclipses, emphasizing the need for commentators to remain faithful to Ptolemy's original Almagest.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Venus, Sun, Georg Peuerbach, epicycle, equant, lunar eclipse, minutes of the case, Ptolemy, Almagest</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical critique of astronomical definitions, specifically focusing on the timing of eclipses and the calculation of planetary coordinates. The dialogue between the instructor (A) and the student (L) clarifies the distinction between measurements relative to the ecliptic (the Sun's path) and the celestial equator.</meta>

...of its own. But in all tables, such <term>minutes of incidence</term><note>original: "minuta casus"; refers to the duration or arc of time from the beginning of an eclipse until the start of totality.</note> are recorded. For this reason, in order to determine the timing, the commentators on the tables advise that these minutes of incidence be divided by the Moon's true <term>excess</term><note>original: "superationem"; the relative velocity by which the Moon outpaces the Sun in its orbit.</note> in one hour. Alternatively, they suggest adding a twelfth part to those minutes and dividing the total by the Moon's true hourly motion. Neither of these methods would be correctly understood if the minutes of incidence were defined according to this man’s opinion.

**L.** I am fully persuaded. Nor do I think one should judge differently concerning the "minutes of half-duration" <note>Referring to the period of totality in an eclipse.</note>: they must be defined by the Moon's excess, not by its full motion. Therefore, I believe the following passage should be ignored, where he says: "if these minutes are divided by the uniform motion of the Moon in one hour, the time will result in which the Moon passes through those minutes."

**A.** You are correct. For even if such a division yields the time in which the Moon traverses those specific minutes, it is not actually the time of incidence or of half-duration. Instead, it is necessary to divide such minutes by the Moon's hourly excess, just as we advised above. But now let us proceed more quickly, for the hour warns us; let us set aside those trivial matters.

**L.** Do you wish for me to move on to the <term>latitudes</term><note>The angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic (the Sun's path).</note> of the planets?

**A.** That pleases me very much. Read this first.

**L.** "And if," he says, "we take the <term>declination</term><note>The angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator.</note> of the degree of the <term>zodiac</term><note>original: "orbis signoz"; the circle of signs.</note> in which the Moon is located, and in that same hour we take the latitude of the Moon from the zodiac—that is, from the path of the Sun—and if both the declination and the latitude are northern, or both are southern, we shall add them together. The result will be the Moon's declination from the <term>equinoctial</term><note>The celestial equator.</note>. If they are different, we shall subtract the smaller from the larger. The declination for other planets is found in the same way."

**A.** Who, I ask, could bear this with a calm mind? For latitude is an arc of a <term>great circle</term><note>A circle on the surface of a sphere whose plane passes through the center of the sphere, like the equator or a meridian.</note> passing through the poles of the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The path of the Sun through the stars.</note>, intercepted between the center of the star and the ecliptic itself. Declination, however, is considered on a great circle passing through the poles of the celestial equator and the center of the star; it is the arc of that circle intercepted between the star and the equator. These two circles are always different, except when the star is at the "head" of **Cancer** or **Capricorn** <note>The solstices, where the ecliptic and the circles of declination align perpendicularly.</note>. Only then do those circles coincide, and only then does this man's teaching hold true. Elsewhere, if you place a star, the declination of its true position and the star's own latitude (if it has any) will always meet at an angle. Therefore, from the combination of the aforementioned declination and latitude...

<summary>This page critiques an astronomical author for incorrectly calculating a planet's declination by simply adding its latitude, failing to account for the complex geometry of the celestial sphere. Regiomontanus explains that these two measurements exist on different circles that only align at the solstices.</summary>
<keywords>minutes of incidence, lunar motion, celestial latitude, declination, zodiac, ecliptic, equinoctial, Cancer, Capricorn, spherical geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical critique of astronomical definitions, moving from the timing of eclipses to the geometric relationship between the eccentric and the epicycle. a single continuous arc will not be formed. Hence, anyone would foolishly think that one [arc] should be subtracted from the other so that the <term>declination</term><note>The angular distance of a star or planet north or south of the celestial equator.</note> of the star remains. All of which, if it were necessary, could be more clearly shown by its own illustration. 

**L.** Set aside the illustration. For your argument is clear. Move on to the rest of this chapter. But I often wonder at this one thing: that the tables of "two-part" and "four-part" numbers <note>original: "tabulas bipartialis ⁊ q̄dripartialis numeri"; likely referring to table layouts that use different increments or divisions of planetary cycles.</note> have been so abolished, even though that "good man" <note>An ironic reference to the author of the text they are critiqued.</note> spends his entire discussion of this chapter on them. 

**A.** Learned astronomers do not reject those tables without reason, since they are far from the truth. This will be clearly established if we compare the latitudes derived from them to those calculated through the tables of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential ancient astronomer whose mathematical models defined the field for over a millennium.</note>. 

But let us return to the text. Among other things concerning the tables of latitudes, that man [the author] says: "and the latitude which is written in the tables of the binary [number] is the distance of the parts of the circumference of the <term>epicycle</term><note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one (the eccentric).</note> from the circumference of the <term>eccentric</term><note>The main orbital circle of a planet, whose center is offset from the Earth.</note>: a distance, I say, computed toward the path of the sun <note>original: "viam solis"; referring to the ecliptic, the apparent path of the Sun across the sky.</note>." 

**L.** What do you think should be noted there? 

**A.** If that were so, it would be necessary for the center of the planet never to be found on the surface of the eccentric. For at every <term>equated argument</term><note>The adjusted position of a planet used as an entry point for calculating positions in astronomical tables.</note> in the table of the two-part number, some value is placed which—as he understands it—denotes the distance of the planet from the eccentric. 

**L.** That would certainly follow. 

**A.** Now listen to his other words: "The epicycle, however, is inclined from the eccentric in such a way that the planet will always be between the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The plane of the Earth's orbit (or the Sun's apparent path), used as the primary reference for planetary latitude.</note> and the center of the epicycle, except when the center of the epicycle is in the Head or Tail of the Dragon <note>original: "capite vel cauda draconis"; the lunar nodes, where the orbit crosses the ecliptic.</note>. For then the epicycle is aligned directly in the eccentric." What do you think? 

**L.** Since the center of the epicycle is always on the surface of the eccentric, it follows that the planet would always be between the two planes of the ecliptic and the eccentric. 

**A.** But that is absurd. For in the three <term>superior planets</term><note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, which are further from the Sun than Earth is.</note>, the surface of the epicycle always intersects the flat surface of the eccentric such that its upper part, which contains the <term>apogee</term><note>original: "augem"; the point in the orbit furthest from the center of motion.</note> of the epicycle, is contained between the two surfaces of the eccentric and the ecliptic. The remaining part of the surface of the epicycle, however, is further removed from the ecliptic than the surface of the eccentric itself. Whence a planet existing in this lower part of the epicycle is also further removed from the ecliptic than the eccentric itself; and therefore, it is not necessarily found between the ecliptic and the surface of the eccentric. Finally, it is falsely asserted that when the center of the epicycle is in the Head or Tail of the Dragon...

<meta>This page continues the technical critique of a text on planetary latitudes. The speakers are refuting the idea that an epicycle's surface remains in the plane of the eccentric when at its nodes, and they are now debating the mathematical interpretation of latitude tables attributed to Azarchel.</meta>

...[with the center of the epicycle] existing, its surface would lie directly in the surface of the <term>eccentric</term><note>A circular orbit where the center of motion is slightly offset from the Earth.</note>. For if the center of the <term>epicycle</term><note>A smaller circle that moves along the circumference of a larger circle (the deferent).</note> were positioned in one of the <term>nodes</term><note>The two points where a planet's orbit crosses the plane of the Sun's path (the ecliptic).</note>, a planet located outside the true <term>apogee</term><note>original: "augem"; the point in an orbit furthest from the center of motion.</note> of its epicycle, or at the opposite of the apogee, would always obtain a latitude from the ecliptic. But this is surely false. For according to the testimony of **Ptolemy**, while the epicycle exists in one of the nodes, no latitude occurs for the planet, wherever it may be on the epicycle. Therefore, it is necessary for the entire surface of the epicycle to lie in the plane of the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky; the reference plane for measuring planetary latitude.</note>, not the eccentric, when the center of the epicycle is in one of the nodes.

**L.** But why do we treat the delusions of this man at such length? Let us look at his next opinions. 

"And when," he says, "the planet is in the apogee of the epicycle, it then declines most from the path of the Sun; for this reason, the greatest latitude is found in the table."

**A.** Nonsense. For the latitude of any of the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> is greater while it exists in the opposite of the apogee of the epicycle than in the apogee itself. The same also always happens for Venus and Mercury, except when the center of the epicycle is in the <term>dragon’s belly</term><note>original: "ventre draconis"; the point of maximum latitude, 90 degrees away from the nodes (the "head" and "tail" of the dragon).</note>. For there, the apogee of the epicycle and its opposite grant equal latitudes to the planet, as Ptolemy explains all these things.

**L.** Why then is a larger number found at the beginning of the table of the "binary number" than at the end? It is as if a greater latitude occurs for a planet positioned in the apogee of the epicycle than in its opposite. For the beginning of the table is said to correspond to the apogee, and the end to the opposite of the apogee.

**A.** The numbers written in this table, my friend, are not the actual latitudes, as that man will also assert a little further below. Instead, the function of numbers of this kind, according to the mind of **Azarchel**<note>Al-Zarqali (1029–1087), a prominent Arab astronomer whose "Toledan Tables" were widely used in medieval Europe.</note>, is to divide other numbers and to draw out the latitude itself. But if you divide the same number by two unequal numbers, the larger divisor yields a smaller quotient, while the smaller divisor yields a larger one. This is easily proven from the twenty-seventh proposition of the seventh book of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Euclid’s *Elements*, a foundational text of geometry and number theory.</note>. Thus, the larger number offered at the head of the "two-part" table of numbers will return a smaller latitude than the smaller number at the end of the same.

**E.** You have convinced me that nothing appears in this chapter on latitudes other than follies, and indeed intolerable ones. Nevertheless, if you command it, I will pronounce his following text.

**A.** Proceed.

<summary>The speakers use Ptolemaic principles to debunk the author's claims about planetary latitudes, explaining that the numbers in Azarchel's tables are mathematical divisors rather than direct measurements of latitude.</summary>

Mars, at last, has not unjustly scorned the said tables: since, as Ptolemy affirms, the maximum northern latitude it has is four degrees and twenty-nine minutes, but the southern latitude is seven degrees and thirty minutes.

From these tables, however, it will assume an equal distance from the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky, used as the zero-point for measuring planetary latitude (north/south distance).</note> here and there. But neither Venus nor Mercury will keep silent about the false claims made against them. For while the center of the <term>epicycle</term><note>A small circular orbit whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle (the eccentric).</note> is in the <term>apogee</term><note>original: "auge"; the point in an orbit furthest from the Earth.</note> of the <term>eccentric</term> and Venus herself is in the opposite of the apogee of the epicycle, the aforementioned tables show a latitude greater than thirteen degrees; which, however, according to the mind of Ptolemy, will not be more than ten minutes.

Mercury, truly, in a similar position of its circles, will have according to the often-mentioned tables a latitude greater than nine degrees. In truth, however, it recedes from the path of the Sun by only forty-five minutes. This is stated assuming that the two <term>nodes</term><note>The points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic.</note> are equally distant from the <term>dragon's belly</term><note>original: "ventre draconis"; the point of maximum latitude, 90 degrees from the nodes.</note>, as all astronomers believe.

Thus that peevish composer of tables neglected the most truthful Ptolemy, whom <term>Albategnius</term><note>al-Battani (c. 858–929 AD), a famous Arab astronomer and mathematician whose work was foundational to medieval European astronomy.</note>—the father of all who handle tabular numbers—strove to imitate. **A.** What follows.

**L.** "When truly the center of the epicycle is in the nodes, there are no latitudes; for then the epicycle is upright in the eccentric and the center of the epicycle is in the path of the Sun."

**A.** We have long since dismissed that claim as worthless<note>original: "floccifecimus"; literally "to value at a tuft of wool," meaning to consider something of no account.</note>. For then the epicycle is not accustomed to lie in the surface of the eccentric, but is established in the plane of the ecliptic, according to the testimony of Ptolemy. Likewise, if you are not yet weary: I will show that this "good man" is by no means consistent with himself. For with the center of the epicycle existing in the nodes, he says there are no latitudes, adding the reason that the epicycle is then "upright" in the eccentric. Which, if it is so, it follows that every point of the epicycle is found outside the ecliptic except those which are in the common section of the epicycle and the ecliptic; just as no point of the eccentric is in the ecliptic except those which lie in the common section of the eccentric and the ecliptic. Wherefore, with the center of the epicycle being in one of the nodes, the planet will not be deprived of latitude unless it is in the apogee of the epicycle or its opposite; for these two places are in the aforesaid common section. But let us see what he says about the motion of the head and...

<meta>This page continues the technical critique of the Alfonsine Tables (or similar contemporary astronomical tables), specifically focusing on the movement of the planetary nodes—the "Head and Tail of the Dragon"—for the inferior planets, Venus and Mercury. ...of the dragon's tail. **C.** He says, however, that the Head <note>The "Head and Tail of the Dragon" refers to the nodes of a planet's orbit—the two points where the planet's path crosses the ecliptic (the Sun's path). The "Head" is the ascending node, and the "Tail" is the descending node.</note> and Tail of the three superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are those further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> are immobile, except for the motion of the eighth sphere <note>The "eighth sphere" is the celestial sphere containing the fixed stars. Its "motion" refers to precession, the slow shift of the stars' positions over centuries.</note>. But the Head and Tail of Venus and Mercury move in such a proportion that the true position of the Head of each is always as far from them as the true position of the Head written in the rule <note>original: "canone"; a "canon" in this context is a set of instructions or a mathematical rule for using astronomical tables.</note> of the tables is from the position resulting from the mean motion of the Sun and the equated argument of these planets.

**A.** Nonsense! <note>original: "Fabulae," literally "Fables" or "Stories," used here as a dismissive exclamation.</note> For in truth, the Head and Tail of Venus and Mercury do not move differently than the heads and tails of the three superior planets. Everyone agrees on this. Even the composer of the often-mentioned tables seems to have thought the same, since in his own rule he records the true positions of the Heads for Venus and Mercury as if they were immobile. However, according to the trifles of this "good man," the Head of Venus moves almost as much against the succession of the signs <note>i.e., in a "retrograde" or backward direction through the Zodiac.</note> as the star itself moves in its <term>epicycle</term><note>A small circular orbit whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle (the eccentric). This model was used to explain the complex, looping motions of planets.</note> relative to its true <term>apogee</term><note>original: "augis"; the point in an orbit furthest from the Earth.</term>, though sometimes subtracting or adding the equation of the argument from it. I believe no one is ignorant of how far this is from the truth. Finally, the same thing would have to happen for Mercury.

How that follows will be explained in the figure. Let the circle **a.b.c.** represent the <term>zodiac</term><note>The belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs, used as a coordinate system.</note>: where **a** is the beginning of <term>Aries</term><note>The first sign of the zodiac, used as the zero-point for astronomical measurements.</note>, **b** is the position of the Head of Venus, and let the line of the mean motion of the Sun be understood to end at that point **b** while Venus herself is at the true apogee of the epicycle; which is indeed possible, since those two motions do not have equal periods of completion. If, therefore, we add the mean motion of the Sun, which is the arc **a. b.**, with the equated argument

<column-break/>

of Venus: so that we may follow the form of the rule, although in this case there is no such argument; nothing will result except the arc **a. b.**, from which if we subtract the true motion of the head, nothing will remain. For which reason the star will be at the node of the head, namely point **b**. Then, after some time has passed, let the line of the mean motion of the Sun reach point **c**, namely the apogee of the <term>eccentric</term><note>A circular orbit whose center is offset from the Earth.</note>.

<meta>This page continues a technical debate regarding the motion of planetary nodes, specifically criticizing the internal logic of a certain set of astronomical tables. ...so that the process may be easier: let arc $c.d.$ be similar to the equated argument <note>The "equated argument" is a corrected angle used in medieval astronomy to calculate a planet's position relative to its epicycle.</note> of Venus. If, therefore, from the entire arc $a.c.d.$—which consists of the mean motion of the Sun and the equated argument of Venus—we subtract arc $a.b.$ of the head <note>The "head" refers to the ascending node of the planet's orbit.</note>, there will remain arc $b.d.$, which is equal to the distance of the head from the star itself, according to the mind of this man. Therefore, let the star be placed according to its true motion at point $f$, so that arc $c.f.$ is similar to the equation of the argument; this equation of the argument is necessarily always smaller than the equated argument itself. And let arc $b.e.$ be equal to arc $d.f.$; and therefore, by a common conception of the mind, arc $f.b.e.$ is equal to arc $b.f.d.$ It will be necessary, then, for the head of the dragon to be at point $e$ now, which previously was at point $b$. The head of the dragon of Venus, therefore, has moved against the order of the signs <note>This means the node has moved in a "retrograde" or westward direction through the zodiac.</note> through arc $b.e.$, namely the difference between the equated argument and the equation of that same argument. And this happens in only a quarter of a solar year, which is a most great inconvenience.

**L.** I think that is what should be done. Have you, I pray, seen any tables composed for Arim? <note>Arim was a mythical city, often identified with Ujjain in India, believed by medieval geographers to be located at the precise center of the world on the equator.</note>

**L.** It is a wonder that there were so many composers of tables—as he asserts—at Arim, and yet examples of them are found nowhere. 

**A.** I know that Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Alexandrian polymath whose geocentric model dominated astronomy for over a millennium.</note> indeed established his tables for Alexandria, which is situated neither under the equinoctial <note>The "equinoctial" is the celestial equator.</note> nor in the middle between the east and the west. Albategnius <note>Al-Battani (c. 858–929 AD), a celebrated Arab astronomer who refined the measurements of the solar year and the obliquity of the ecliptic.</note>, however, for the city of Arata <note>Ar-Raqqah in modern Syria.</note>, as is drawn from the first chapter of his book. But Arata itself does not stand in the middle of the world, since it is in the East; Alexandria has ten degrees [of longitude] and a latitude of thirty-six degrees from the equinoctial. 

**L.** If you please, let us hear this man's opinion on the variation of the altitudes of the Sun taken on the back of the astrolabe and on its face, and furthermore on the motion of its apogee <note>original: "augis," the point in the Sun's orbit furthest from Earth.</note>. 

**A.** Let him listen.

And it is the opposite when it is in the other half of the eccentric..."

<meta>This page continues a technical critique of astronomical methods, specifically addressing the measurement of the Sun's apogee and eccentricity. In the previous page, the author attacked calculations involving the nodes of Venus. Here, the dialogue between 'A' (the critic) and 'L' (the respondent) shifts to the misuse of the astrolabe and a refutation of an unnamed author’s claims regarding solar observations, which he falsely attributes to the famous Arab astronomer Al-Battani.</meta>

...and on whichever day the distance between these two altitudes is greatest, on that same day the Sun will be in the apogee of the eccentric <note>The "apogee" (Latin: *auge*) is the point in a planet's or the Sun's orbit where it is furthest from the Earth.</note> in the first half mentioned above; because as great as the distance is, so great is the eccentricity; and it is nearly two degrees.

**A.** Stop, I beg you, stop presenting so many trifles from this man. Is it not true that if an astrolabe is correctly made, the point of the ecliptic <note>The circular path on the celestial sphere that the Sun appears to follow over the course of a year.</note> on the rete <note>The "rete" is the rotating openwork plate of an astrolabe that represents the positions of the stars and the ecliptic.</note> where the Sun is said to be is elevated on the meridian line of the instrument only as much—proportionally—as that celestial point which is represented by the aforementioned point on the meridian of the observer’s location?

**L.** Indeed it is.

**A.** Moreover, the center of the Sun is on a straight line that extends from the center of the world to the aforementioned point on the ecliptic.

**L.** I admit it.

**A.** In fact, all points on that same line going out from the center of the horizon toward the heavens obtain the same altitude from the horizon itself, the center of the horizon alone being excluded.

**L.** That is certain. For a line of this kind encompasses a single angle with the common intersection of the horizon and the circle of altitude passing through that same mentioned line.

**A.** Therefore, the center of the Sun has the same altitude as the point of the celestial ecliptic under which it exists; and for that reason, the altitude of the Sun in truth is as great as the point of the instrumental ecliptic shows on the meridian line.

**L.** No one will deny it; for whatever things are equal to one and the same thing are also considered equal to each other.

**A.** But on the back of the astrolabe, the true altitude of the Sun is detected, except for how much the distance between the centers of the world and of the instrument, or the refraction <note>The bending of light as it passes through the atmosphere, which makes celestial bodies appear slightly higher than they actually are.</note> of the solar ray, might vary. Philosophers consider the former of these as nothing, because the Earth has an almost imperceptible size compared to the sphere of the Sun; the latter is shown by certain reasons to be nearly imperceptible. For this reason, the two solar altitudes detected on the back and the face of the astrolabe differ imperceptibly; and therefore, they must be held as equal.

**L.** You have clearly refuted this raving man.

**A.** Whatever remains of his writing, therefore, must collapse. For the application of the Sun to the apogee of its eccentric will by no means be known by this evidence; neither will the eccentricity become known, nor will he perceive the motion of the apogee in a year—things which he dreams that the most learned Arab, Albategnius <note>Al-Battani (c. 858–929 AD), a renowned Arab astronomer and mathematician whose observations were used by scholars like Copernicus and Tycho Brahe.</note>, observed in this manner.

<summary>This page contains a dialogue refuting a contemporary's astronomical theories by arguing that the front and back measurements of an astrolabe are essentially identical. The speaker 'A' demonstrates that solar altitude measurements cannot be used to determine eccentricity or the movement of the solar apogee as his opponent claims.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes a dialogue between two scholars, 'A' and 'L,' regarding astronomical observations and instruments. ...with an astrolabe, namely one for common use or of a larger size. But I seem to hear some kind of commotion. Go, <note>original: "I." — the imperative form of the Latin verb "to go."</note> I beg you, to see what the matter is; meanwhile, I will investigate as quickly as possible if there are any further points to be noted.

**L.** I shall go, and gladly.

**A.** That small matter regarding the aspects <term>Aspects: In historical astronomy, these are specific angular distances between planets (like 90 or 180 degrees) used to calculate their influence.</term> of the planets, although it is little in use today, is correctly taught, especially when the planet is on the meridian. <note>The meridian is the imaginary great circle passing through the celestial poles and the zenith of the observer's location.</note> For if it were on the horizon, other astronomers command that the aspects be investigated through oblique ascensions or descensions. In middle locations, they achieve this through mixed ascensions or descensions. But our friend returns. What is it? What is happening?

**L.** People are running together from all sides; the masters are about to depart.

**A.** Then we too, putting an end to the game we have begun, must follow at once.

**L.** How conveniently that time corresponded to our conversations, so much so that we have left almost nothing unexamined, since no more leisure remains.

**A.** We have passed over many things said rather loosely in silence, not as if they were entirely approved, but because they were either easily moderated or not worthy enough to be discussed; we chose to do this all the more deliberately so that we might not seem to bite greedily at any foreign words rather than inquire after the truth. Now it remains for each to happily return to his own hearth. May you be well then, sweetest of friends.

**L.** And may you fare well too.

## New Theories of the Planets by Georg Peuerbach,
## Most Celebrated Astronomer. On the Sun:

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'S' at the beginning of the text "Sol". The letter is entwined with elaborate foliate scrolls and floral patterns, set within a square double-line border.</image-desc>

The Sun has three spheres, <note>original: "orbes"</note> divided from each other in every way and yet contiguous to one another. The uppermost of these, according to its convex surface, is concentric to the world, <note>In the geocentric model, "concentric to the world" means the sphere's center is the exact center of the Earth.</note> but according to its concave surface, it is eccentric. <note>"Eccentric" means the center of this specific sphere is offset from the center of the Earth.</note> The lowest sphere, however, is concentric according to its concave surface, but eccentric according to its convex surface. The third, located in the middle of these, is eccentric to the world according to both its convex and concave surfaces. It is called...

<meta>This page transitions from the preceding dialogue between the scholars 'A' and 'L' directly into the formal astronomical text of Georg Peuerbach's *Theoricae Novae Planetarum*. Having concluded their discussion on instruments and planetary aspects, the text now begins the technical geometric description of the solar system's structure according to the Ptolemaic model, specifically defining the different types of celestial spheres.</meta>

...while a sphere concentric <term>Concentric: In geocentric astronomy, this refers to a celestial sphere whose center is exactly the same as the center of the Earth (the "center of the world").</term> with the world is one whose center is the center of the world. An eccentric <term>Eccentric: Literally "out of center." This refers to a sphere whose center is shifted away from the Earth's center, used to explain why the Sun appears to move at varying speeds through the seasons.</term> sphere, however, is one whose center is different from the center of the world. Therefore, the first two are eccentric in a certain respect, and they are called the spheres carrying the apogee <term>Apogee: Referred to in the original text as "aux" (from the Arabic 'auj'), this is the point in the Sun's orbit where it is furthest from the Earth.</term> of the sun. For by their motion, the apogee of the sun is changed. The third is truly eccentric in the absolute sense, and it is called the sun-carrying sphere <note>original: "orbis solem deferens." This is the "deferent" which physically transports the body of the Sun through space.</note>; for by its motion, the solar body fixed within it is moved. These three spheres occupy two centers. For the convex surface of the highest sphere and the concave surface of the lowest have the same center, which is the center of the world. Hence the entire sphere of the Sun, just as that of any other...

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the solar spheres from Peuerbach's "Theoricae Novae Planetarum." The previous page established that the Sun’s system consists of three distinct spheres: two "apogee-carrying" spheres and one "Sun-carrying" (deferent) sphere. This page explains how these spheres share a common eccentric center and move in coordination with the celestial movements of the stars.</meta>

...since the whole sphere of the planet is said to be concentric with the world. But the concave surface of the highest and the convex surface of the lowest, together with both surfaces of the middle sphere, have another single center, which is called the center of the eccentric <term>Eccentric: A circle or sphere whose center is offset from the Earth. This was used to explain why the Sun appears to move faster or slower at different times of the year.</term>.

¶ The spheres carrying the apogee <term>Apogee: Termed "aux" in the original Latin (from Arabic), this is the point in a planet's orbit furthest from the Earth.</term> of the Sun are moved by their own proportional motions in such a way that the narrower part of the upper sphere is always positioned over the wider part of the lower one. And they complete their revolutions at the same rate as the change in the motion of the eighth sphere <note>The "eighth sphere" in this cosmological model carries the fixed stars. Its motion refers to the precession of the equinoxes, a very slow wobble in the Earth's orientation that makes the stars appear to shift over centuries.</note>, about which more must be said later. However, the poles of this motion are the poles of the ecliptic <term>Ecliptic: The great circle representing the Sun's apparent annual path against the background of the stars.</term> of the eighth sphere. --- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical geometric description of the solar system following Peuerbach's "Theoricae Novae Planetarum." The previous page established that the Sun is carried by a specific eccentric sphere nested within other spheres. This page provides the specific daily rate of the Sun’s motion and defines the key points of its orbit—the Apogee, Perigee, and Mean Distance—relative to the center of the Earth.</meta>

around its own center—namely, that of the eccentric—regularly according to the succession of the signs <note>original: "successionem signorum"; this refers to the order of the Zodiac signs (Aries, Taurus, etc.), moving from west to east.</note> daily by approximately 59 minutes and eight seconds of the parts of the circumference described through the center of the solar body in one completed revolution. The poles of this motion are distant from the poles of the previous spheres; they are the ends of the axis of that sphere, namely the line passing through the center of the eccentric parallel to the axis of the spheres carrying the apogee. From this it appears that because of the motion of the spheres carrying the apogee—which they possess by virtue of the motion of the eighth sphere <note>The eighth sphere carries the fixed stars. Its slow motion represents the precession of the equinoxes.</note>—the axis of the sphere carrying the Sun, along with the center of the eccentric circle and its pole, describes the circumferences of small circles around the axis of the spheres carrying the apogee, according to the amount of eccentricity. Since, however, the solar center is moved regularly about the center of the eccentric by the motion of the sphere carrying it, it must be that it moves irregularly about any other point. For this reason, the Sun describes unequal angles about the center of the world in equal times, and covers unequal arcs of the circumference of the zodiac. Thus, the <term>Eccentric Circle: A circle whose center is offset from the center of the Earth (the "center of the world"). This was the primary tool for ancient astronomers to explain why the Sun appears to speed up and slow down throughout the year.</term>—also called the circle of the "departing point" or "departing center"—is defined as a circle whose center is different from the center of the world, yet still surrounds it.

¶ We imagine that an eccentric circle is described in the Sun by a line extending from the center of the eccentric to the solar center, moving regularly about the center of the eccentric in one completed revolution; this circle is always a part of the surface of the ecliptic <term>Ecliptic: The apparent path of the Sun against the background of the stars.</term> of the sphere of signs of the eighth sphere.

¶ The apogee <note>original: "Aux solis"; from the Arabic "al-awj," meaning the highest point.</note> of the Sun, in its primary meaning, or the "longer distance," is the point of the circumference of the eccentric furthest removed from the center of the world. It is determined by a line drawn from the center of the world through the center of the eccentric and extending to both sides; this is called the "line of the apogee."

¶ The opposite of the apogee, or the "nearer distance" <note>This is known today as the perigee.</note>, is the point of the circumference of the eccentric closest to the center of the world; it is always diametrically opposed to the apogee.

¶ The mean distance is the point of the circumference between the apogee and the opposite of the apogee. In the case of the Sun, it is determined by a line which, starting from the center of the world, forms right angles with the line of the apogee. Only two such points are found in the same eccentric.

It introduces the technical definitions for apogee (farthest point), perigee (closest point), and mean distance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric description of the solar system following Georg von Peuerbach's "New Theory of the Planets" (Theoricae Novae Planetarum). The previous page discussed the Sun's daily rate of motion (approx. 59 minutes) and the physical rotation of its eccentric sphere. This page defines the abstract geometric lines and arcs that allow astronomers to calculate the Sun's position relative to the Earth and the Zodiac.</meta>

¶ The line of the **mean motion of the Sun** is a line extended from the center of the world to the zodiac, parallel to the line drawn from the center of the eccentric to the solar center. These two lines, however, are [coincident as] one twice in a year, as when the Sun is at the **apogee** <note>original: "auge"; the point in the orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> of the eccentric or at its **opposite** <note>The perigee, or the point in the orbit closest to the Earth.</note>. Just as one of them revolves regularly around its own center <note>the center of the eccentric</note>, so also the other revolves around its own <note>the center of the world</note>. For they always make equal angles with the line of the apogee whenever they differ from it.

¶ The **mean motion of the Sun** is the arc of the zodiac beginning from **Aries** <note>The "First Point of Aries," or the vernal equinox, which serves as the zero-point for celestial measurements.</note> computed according to the **succession of the signs** <note>Moving in the natural order of the zodiac (Aries, Taurus, Gemini, etc.), which represents the Sun's eastward progress against the stars.</note> as far as the line of mean motion.

¶ The **apogee of the Sun**, in its second meaning <note>Referring here to the specific coordinate or location of the apogee on the zodiac, rather than the physical distance or the line itself.</note>, is the arc of the zodiac starting from Aries, following the succession of the signs, as far as the line of the apogee.

### ¶ Theory of lines and motions.

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric definitions for calculating the Sun's position and begins the description of the Moon's more complex system of celestial spheres. On the previous page, the author defined the mean motion and the apogee. This page concludes the solar section by defining the "argument" (the angle from the apogee), the "true motion" (the actual position), and the "equation" (the correction factor between mean and true motion). It then introduces the lunar model, which requires four main spheres and one small epicycle to account for its varied movements.</meta>

The **argument of the Sun** is the arc of the zodiac between the line of the apogee and the line of the mean motion of the Sun, following the succession of the signs <note>original: "argumentum Solis"; the angular distance the Sun has traveled from its highest point (apogee).</note>. This arc is always similar to the arc on the eccentric circle between the apogee of the eccentric and the center of the Sun, falling in the same order of signs. From this, the reason is clear why, when the Sun's apogee (in its second meaning) is subtracted from the Sun’s mean motion—or from the mean motion plus a full circle—the argument of the Sun remains.

¶ The **line of the true motion of the Sun** is a line extended from the center of the world through the center of the solar body and out to the zodiac. It happens that when the Sun is at its apogee or at the opposite point, this line is exactly the same as the line of mean motion.

¶ The **true motion of the Sun** is the arc from the beginning of **Aries** <note>The vernal equinox point.</note> to the line of the true motion. Only when the Sun is at its apogee or the opposite point are the mean motion and the true motion identical. In any other place, they always differ.

¶ The **equation of the Sun** is the arc of the zodiac falling between the lines of the mean and true motion <note>original: "aequatio Solis"; also known as the prosthaphaeresis, this is the geometric correction needed to find the Sun's actual position compared to its average position.</note>. It happens that this equation is zero when the Sun is at its apogee or the opposite point.

¶ The **greatest equation** happens when the Sun is located at its **mean distances** <note>original: "longitudinibus medijs"; the points halfway between the apogee and the perigee.</note>. In other locations, the equation increases or decreases according to the variation of the argument. For the closer the Sun is to the apogee or its opposite, the smaller the equation is; the closer it is to the mean distances, the larger it is. While the argument is less than **six signs** <note>180 degrees, or half the circle.</note>, the line of mean motion precedes the line of true motion; therefore, in that case, the equation is subtracted. But when the argument is greater than six signs, the opposite occurs; then the equation is added to the mean motion so that the true motion of the Sun may be found.

## On the Moon.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'L' at the start of the paragraph, featuring foliate scrollwork and vine motifs within a rectangular border.</image-desc>
The Moon has four orbs and one small sphere. First, it has three orbs arranged in a configuration like the Sun’s: namely, two that are "eccentric in a certain respect" <note>original: "eccentricos secundum quid"; these spheres are not centered on the Earth, but their thickness is uniform relative to the Earth.</note>, which are called the orbs carrying the lunar eccentric's apogee; and a third, "simply eccentric" orb located in the middle of these, which is called the **deferent of the epicycle** <note>The sphere that carries the small circle (epicycle) upon which the Moon itself sits.</note>. Then, it has an orb concentric with the world that surrounds the aggregate of the other three; this is called the **deferent of the head of the dragon** <note>original: "deferens caput draconis"; the sphere responsible for the movement of the lunar nodes, where the Moon's path crosses the Sun's path.</note>. Finally, it has a small sphere which is called the **epicycle**...

<keywords>argument of the sun, true motion, equation of the sun, prosthaphaeresis, lunar theory, deferent, epicycle, head of the dragon, nodes</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the solar model to the lunar model. The previous page concluded the definitions of the Sun's motion (apogee, argument, and equation) and introduced the concept that the Moon requires a more complex arrangement of spheres—specifically four main spheres and one epicycle—to explain its observed movements in the sky.</meta>

...of the third sphere, in which epicycle the lunar body itself is fixed.

¶ Now, the spheres carrying the **apogee of the eccentric** <note>The "apogee" is the point in the orbit furthest from Earth. In the lunar model, this point itself moves.</note> move against the succession of the signs <note>original: "contra successionem signorum"; this refers to movement from East to West, opposite to the Sun's path through the zodiac.</note> simultaneously and uniformly around the **center of the world** <note>In this geocentric model, the Earth is the center of the world.</note>. This motion occurs independently of the daily rotation, at a rate of approximately 11 degrees and 12 minutes in a natural day. The axis of this movement intersects the axis of the zodiac at the center of the world; therefore, its poles deviate from the poles of the zodiac, and the amount of this tilt is always a constant five degrees <note>This explains the inclination of the Moon's orbit relative to the Sun's path, which is why eclipses do not happen every month.</note>. 

The sphere carrying the **epicycle** <note>term: epicycle; a small circular orbit whose center is carried around the circumference of a larger circle (the deferent).</note> moves according to the succession of the signs <note>Moving Eastward, following the order of the zodiac signs.</note> uniformly around the center of the world, so that in every natural day the center of the epicycle travels approximately 13 degrees and 11 minutes.

->**Theory of the Lunar Spheres**<-
<note>original: "Theorica orbium Lunae"</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram illustrating the "Theory of the Lunar Spheres" based on the Ptolemaic geocentric model. The diagram consists of several concentric and eccentric circles. A vertical line serves as an axis through the centers. A small epicycle containing nested circles is located at the top of an inner sphere. A wide circular band within the diagram is shaded dark grey. Several points are labeled along the central vertical axis: "center of the carrying sphere" (c deferēt), "center of the world" (c mūdí, located within a small central circle representing Earth), and "opposite point" (punctus oppositus) near the bottom of the inner shaded sphere.</image-desc>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the lunar model. The previous page introduced the four spheres and the basic rates of motion for the Moon's apogee (moving Westward) and the epicycle (moving Eastward). This page details the geometric consequences of these movements and the specific synchronicity between the Moon and the Sun.</meta>

However, the axis of this motion moves through the center of this orb—which is called the **center of the eccentric** <note>The "eccentric" is a circle whose center is offset from the Earth. This allows astronomers to explain why the Moon appears to move at different speeds even if its actual motion is uniform.</note>—parallel to the axis of the orbs carrying the apogee. As a result, the poles of this motion will also be distant from the poles of the apogee-carrying orbs by the amount of the eccentricity.

¶ From these facts it follows first that although the **eccentric** orb carrying the **epicycle** <note>term: epicycle; a small circle on which a planet or the Moon moves, while the center of this small circle itself moves around a larger circle (the deferent).</note> moves upon its own axis and poles, it does not move uniformly around them.

¶ Second, the closer the Moon's epicycle is to the **apogee** <note>original: "aux"; the point in the orbit furthest from Earth.</note> of the orb carrying it, the faster its center appears to move. Conversely, the closer it is to the opposite of the apogee <note>The perigee, or the point in the orbit closest to Earth.</note>, the slower it moves. This is demonstrated because if you draw equal angles from the **center of the world** <note>The Earth, at the center of the universe in this model.</note> toward the apogee and its opposite, the angle toward the apogee encompasses a larger arc of the eccentric than the angle toward the opposite side.

¶ Third, the center of the Moon's eccentric revolves around the center of the world, and the axis of that same orb revolves around the axis of the spheres carrying the apogee, and its poles revolve around their poles, tracing circles **against the succession of the signs** <note>Moving Westward, opposite to the Sun's path through the zodiac.</note> at a uniform rate.

¶ Fourth, the apogee of the Moon's eccentric will move uniformly, likewise progressing against the succession of the signs, and will pass across the **ecliptic** <note>term: ecliptic; the apparent path of the Sun across the sky; the plane of the Earth's orbit.</note>. Thus, it is found sometimes within that plane and sometimes away from it, toward either the South or the North. Consequently, the center of the eccentric also moves away from the plane of the ecliptic in opposite directions.

¶ Fifth, the plane of the ecliptic will not always divide the plane of the eccentric into two equal halves. When the apogee of the eccentric is at a certain latitude <note>A measurement of how far North or South an object is from the ecliptic.</note>, the larger portion of the eccentric's surface will be toward the apogee. For the plane of the eccentric is intersected by the plane of the ecliptic along a diameter of the ecliptic that passes through the center of the world.

¶ The "surface of the eccentric" is defined as the circle described by a line extending from the center of the eccentric to the center of the epicycle as it completes one full revolution. The parts of this circumference are called the apogee, the opposite of the apogee, and the "mean lengths," just as they are named in the solar model.

Indeed, the aforementioned orbs of the Moon are linked to the Sun's motion in such a way that the line of the **mean motion of the Sun** <note>The average position of the Sun, calculated as if it moved at a perfectly constant speed.</note> is always positioned exactly in the middle between the center of the Moon's epicycle and the apogee of its eccentric. They might also be together in the same place, or at the opposite point from both when they are aligned. Consequently, at every **mean conjunction** <note>A "new moon" calculated using average speeds rather than the Moon's actual, fluctuating speed.</note> of the Sun and Moon, the center of the lunar epicycle, the line of the Sun's mean motion, and the apogee of the lunar eccentric all meet at a single point.

<summary>This page describes the complex geometry of the lunar eccentric and epicycle, explaining how the Moon's speed varies based on its distance from Earth. It also establishes a mathematical link between the positions of the Sun and the Moon's orbital apogee.</summary>

<keywords>lunar theory, eccentric, epicycle, apogee, perigee, ecliptic, mean motion, mean conjunction, planetary poles, celestial geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric description of the lunar model, specifically focusing on how the movement of the Moon's eccentric orb is synchronized with the position of the Sun. It concludes the previous discussion on the movement of the axis and begins a new section on the axes and poles of these celestial spheres.</meta>

...of the zodiac according to longitude. For this reason, it happens that in all mean quadratures <note>Positions where the Moon is 90 degrees away from the Sun, such as the first and last quarter phases.</note> the center of the Moon's **epicycle** <note>term: epicycle; a small circle on which the Moon moves, while the center of this small circle itself moves around a larger circle (the deferent).</note> is at the point opposite the **apogee** <note>The point in the orbit furthest from Earth; the opposite point is the perigee, or the point closest to Earth.</note> of its eccentric, and in every mean opposition <note>The Full Moon phase.</note> it is again at the apogee.

¶ From this, the reason is clear why, when the mean motion of the Sun is subtracted from the mean motion of the Moon, their mean **elongation** <note>term: elongation; the angular distance between two celestial bodies as seen from Earth.</note> remains; and when that value is doubled, the position of the center of the Moon's [eccentric] is reached.

¶ Furthermore, the distance of the line of the Moon's mean motion from the line of the Sun's mean motion—following the succession of the signs <note>Moving through the zodiac in the standard order: Aries, Taurus, Gemini, etc.</note>—is called their mean **elongation**.

¶ Moreover, the distance of the line of the Moon's mean motion from the apogee of the eccentric, following the succession of the Moon's center, is called either the **double longitude** or the **double interval** <note>original: "duplex interstitium". In Ptolemaic astronomy, the Moon's eccentric moves at twice the speed of its elongation to account for the "evection" or the change in the shape of the Moon's orbit.</note>.

¶ It is also clear that in every lunar month the center of the Moon's epicycle passes through the orbs twice.
