<meta>This page continues the technical description of the lunar orbs, shifting from the general motion of the eccentric to the specific movements of the fourth concentric orb and the lunar epicycle. It explains how the intersection of these circles creates the Moon's complex path.</meta>

...carrying the **apogee** <note>The point in the orbit furthest from Earth.</note> of the eccentric. But the fourth orb, which is concentric <note>Sharing the same center as the Earth.</note> and carries the **Head of the Dragon** <note>original: "caput draconis"; the North Node of the Moon's orbit, where it crosses the path of the Sun.</note>, moves on the axis of the zodiac around the center of the world. It moves regularly against the succession of the signs <note>In a westward or retrograde direction, opposite to the Sun's usual path.</note> by nearly three minutes every natural day. By this continuous motion, it carries around the entire assembly of the three orbs which it surrounds. From this, it happens that the circumference of the eccentric continually intersects the surface of the **ecliptic** <note>The apparent path of the Sun among the stars.</note> at different points moving toward the west. ¶ It also follows that by such motion, the poles of the orbs carrying the apogee describe the circumferences of circles while moving around the poles of the zodiac.

The **epicycle** <note>term: epicycle; a small circle on which the Moon moves, while the center of this small circle itself moves around a larger circle (the deferent).</note> moves around its own center, carrying the lunar body fixed within it. In the upper part, it moves against the succession of the signs; in the lower part, it moves according to the succession. It moves on its own axis, which lies perpendicular to the circumference of the eccentric, so that the flat surface of the epicycle's circumference (which the center of the Moon’s body describes by the motion of the epicycle) remains within the flat surface of the eccentric, never deviating from it.

However, the epicycle revolves in such a way that it moves irregularly on its own center and axis. But this irregularity is reduced to uniformity in this way: that from the point of the **mean apogee** of the epicycle—wherever that may be—it regularly moves away nearly thirteen degrees and four minutes every natural day.

¶ Moreover, the **mean apogee** <note>original: "aux media"; the theoretical highest point of the epicycle, used for calculation.</note> of the epicycle is the point on the epicycle's circumference indicated by a line drawn from a point diametrically opposite the center of the eccentric in the small circle through the center of the epicycle.

¶ But the **true apogee** <note>original: "aux vera"; the highest point of the epicycle as seen from the observer's perspective.</note> of the epicycle is the point of the same circumference indicated by a line drawn from the center of the world through the center of the epicycle. These two apogees are a single point when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee of the eccentric carrying it or at the opposite point. Elsewhere, they differ everywhere.

¶ From these things it is clear that no single point of the concavity in which the epicycle is situated remains continuously over the mean or true apogee of the epicycle. For such a point of the concavity—which, when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee or the opposite of the eccentric carrying it, would be over the mean and true apogee—is always determined, wherever the center of the epicycle may be, by a line drawn from the center of the eccentric through the center of the epicycle. However, such a point, when the center of the epicycle exists elsewhere than in the apogee or opposite point...

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric explanation of the Moon's motion, specifically focusing on how the "apogee" (the highest point) of the lunar epicycle shifts and how astronomers calculate the difference between the Moon's average (mean) position and its actual (true) position.</meta>

...it is not above the mean apogee of the epicycle nor the true one, but rather both the true and the mean apogees are then beneath different points of the same concavity. For the three lines showing the aforementioned points will then intersect one another at the center of the epicycle. Yet it will be so that whenever the true **apogee** <note>original: "aux"; the point in an orbit furthest from the center of motion</note> differs from the mean apogee, it lies between the mean apogee and the point of the concavity under which the true apogee is usually found when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee or the opposite point of the **deferent** <note>The large circle that carries the smaller epicycle</note>. Wherefore it follows that both the mean apogee of the epicycle and the true one are continually varied.

¶ It is also inferred from this that the revolution of the epicycle around its own center is faster when the center of the epicycle runs through the upper half of the eccentric, but slower through the lower half.

¶ Therefore, the **line of the mean motion of the moon** is that which is drawn from the center of the world <note>The Earth, at the center of the universe in this model</note> all the way to the zodiac through the center of the epicycle.

¶ The **mean motion of the moon** is the arc of the zodiac from the beginning of Aries <note>The traditional starting point for measuring celestial longitude</note> to the said location. The center of the moon is clear from what has been said.

¶ The **line of the true place** or **true motion of the moon** is that which is extended from the center of the world through the center of the lunar body to the zodiac.

¶ The **true motion of the moon** is the arc of the zodiac from the beginning of Aries to the said line.

¶ The **equation of the center** <note>term: equation; a mathematical correction or "adjustment" applied to a calculation to account for an irregularity in movement</note> is the arc of the epicycle falling between its true and mean apogee. This becomes zero when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee or the opposite of the eccentric; however, it is at its maximum when it is slightly below the middle longitudes of the deferent.

¶ The **mean argument of the moon** <note>term: argument; the angle or distance along an orbit used to determine a planet's precise position</note> is the arc of the epicycle calculated from the mean apogee of the epicycle, following the motion of the center of the lunar body, to that same lunar center.

¶ However, the **true argument** is stretched from the true apogee to the center of the moon’s body. Therefore, the difference between these arguments when they differ is the equation of the center. When the center of the moon's epicycle is less than six signs <note>180 degrees, or half of the zodiac</note>, the true argument is greater than the mean. Therefore, the equation of the center is added to the mean argument. But when it is more than six signs, the opposite occurs; wherefore it is then subtracted to obtain the true argument.

¶ The **equation of the argument** is the arc of the zodiac lying between the lines of the mean and true motion. It happens that this is zero while the center of the lunar body is at the true apogee of the epicycle or the opposite [point]...

The amount by which the former exceeds the latter is called the **diversity of the diameter of the small circle** <note>original: "diversitates diametri circuli brevis"; a technical term for the variation in the Moon's apparent size or distance</note>. Moreover, the line drawn from the **center of the world** <note>the Earth</note> to the **apogee of the deferent** <note>the furthest point of the main carrier circle</note> is longer than the line extending from that same center to the point opposite the apogee. 

¶ The difference between these two lines, when divided into sixty equal parts, is called **proportional minutes** <note>original: "minuta proportionalia"; these are units used to scale the correction values based on the Moon's distance from Earth</note>. This value is double the **eccentricity** <note>the distance between the center of the Earth and the geometric center of the orbit</note>. 

¶ For the line of the **mean motion** <note>the calculated average position</note> of the Moon, when directed toward the apogee of the eccentric, contains none of these parts outside the boundary of the eccentric circle; instead, they are all held within it. However, that line which extends toward the point opposite the apogee...

### Theory of the Proportional Minutes of the Moon.

<image-desc>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical geometric explanation of the Moon's motion, specifically how astronomers use "proportional minutes" to adjust calculations based on the Moon's distance from Earth. It then transitions into a discussion of the lunar nodes, traditionally called the "Dragon."</meta>

...has all of these parts outside, and none within. But those lines which extend to other locations on the **eccentric** <note>an off-center circular orbit</note> have some of those parts outside. There are more of these parts the closer the center of the **epicycle** <note>the small circle carrying the Moon</note> is to the point opposite the **apogee** <note>the point furthest from Earth</note>, and fewer the closer it is to the apogee itself. 

¶ The **equations of the arguments** <note>original: "aequationes argumentorum"; the correction factors used to find the Moon's true position</note> which are written in the astronomical tables are those that occur when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee of the **deferent** <note>the large carrier circle</note>. However, as previously stated, these values are smaller than the equations produced when the epicycle's center is located elsewhere. Therefore, when the epicycle center is positioned elsewhere—which happens when the Moon's center is at a certain distance—one takes the **proportional minutes** from the table based on the center’s position. Then, using the **true argument** <note>the Moon's adjusted position on its small circle</note>, one finds the **diversity of the diameter** <note>the difference in the Moon's apparent size/distance</note>. This entire value is added to the equation of the argument previously found in the table if the proportional minutes are 60. But if they are less than 60, not the whole amount is added, but only a portion proportional to the value of the minutes relative to 60. In this way, the **true equation of the argument** is calculated for that specific position of the epicycle.

## On the Moon’s Dragon

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring scrolling vine and floral motifs.</image-desc>
The surface of the Moon's eccentric orbit, as has been said, intersects the surface of the **ecliptic** <note>the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note> along the diameter of the world, due to the tilt of the poles of the spheres carrying the apogee. Consequently, one part of the orbit will tilt toward the north and the other toward the south from the ecliptic. That intersection of the Moon’s eccentric circumference with the surface of the ecliptic—the point where the center of the epicycle begins to move toward the north—is called the **head of the dragon** <note>original: "caput draconis"; known today as the ascending node</note>. The other intersection is called the **tail** <note>original: "cauda"; the descending node</note>. 

¶ These intersections move daily, in addition to the common daily rotation, toward the west by approximately three minutes. This is caused by the movement of the sphere that encompasses the Moon’s three other spheres. 

¶ Therefore, the **mean motion** <note>the average calculated position</note> of the Moon’s **head of the dragon** is the arc of the **zodiac** measured from the beginning of **Aries** <note>the traditional starting point of the astronomical coordinate system</note> against the succession of the signs <note>moving clockwise or retrograde</note> up to line *a*, which is drawn from the center of the world through the intersection of the "head." 

¶ The **true motion** of the "head," however, is the arc of the zodiac measured from the start of Aries according to the succession of the signs <note>moving counter-clockwise</note> to that same line. The same principle applies to the "tail." From this, it is clear that by subtracting the mean motion of the head from the twelve signs <note>a full 360-degree circle</note>, its true position [is revealed]...

<vocab>Epicyclus (Epicycle), De Dracone lunae (On the Moon's Dragon), ecliptica (ecliptic), caput draconis (dragon's head/ascending node), cauda draconis (dragon's tail/descending node), Aries, zodiacus (zodiac), motus diurnus (daily motion)</vocab>

From this comes the common saying that the **Head of the Moon** <note>original: "caput Lune"; the Ascending Node, or the point where the Moon's path crosses the Sun's path moving northward</note> travels as much by **mean motion** <note>average or idealized movement</note> against the **firmament** <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note> as it truly travels with the firmament. This is understood as follows: the mean motion of the Moon’s Head through the **succession of the signs** <note>the standard order of the zodiac: Aries, Taurus, Gemini, etc.</note> points toward the same spot that the true motion reaches according to the succession of the signs.

### Concerning the Three Superior Planets
<note>The "Superior Planets" are Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, which were known in antiquity to be further from the Sun than Earth.</note>

Each of the three superior planets has three spheres separated from one another, following the conceptual model <note>original: "imaginationem"</note> of the Sun's three spheres. However, in the middle sphere—which is simply **eccentric** <note>a circle whose center does not coincide with the Earth</note>—each planet has an **epicycle** <note>a small circle that carries the planet while moving along the path of a larger circle</note>. Just as was mentioned in the discussion of the Moon, the body of the planet is fixed within this epicycle. The spheres, however, that carry the **apogees** <note>the points in the orbit furthest from Earth</note> by the power of motion...

<summary>This page explains the movement of the Moon's nodes (the "Dragon") relative to the stars and introduces the orbital models for the superior planets—Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—which utilize both eccentric orbits and epicycles.</summary>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the movements of the celestial spheres, specifically focusing on how the orbits of the planets (eccentrics and epicycles) relate to the ecliptic and the motion of the fixed stars.</meta>

...of the eighth sphere <note>In the Ptolemaic system, the eighth sphere is the firmament containing the fixed stars</note> move upon the axis and poles of the ecliptic. But the orb carrying the <term>epicycle</term> <note>A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one (the deferent)</note> moves upon its own axis, which intersects the axis of the zodiac, following the succession of the signs: and its poles are at a distance from the poles of the zodiac that is not equal. Because of this, it happens that the <term>apogees</term> <note>Latin: *auges*; the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note> of these <term>eccentrics</term> <note>Orbits whose center is not the Earth</note> never cross the ecliptic, but always remain away from it—towards the north and its opposite towards the south: so that the apogees (or the opposites of the similar epicycle-carriers), as well as the centers and poles of the eccentric carriers, describe circumferences parallel to the surface of the ecliptic by the power of the motion of the eighth sphere. Just as in those cases, the surfaces of the eccentrics recede unequally from the surface of the ecliptic.

...and they leave larger portions toward the <term>apogee</term> <note>original: *augem*; the point in an orbit furthest from the Earth</note> and smaller portions toward the opposite side <note>the perigee, or the point closest to Earth</note>.

¶ The motion of the orb carrying the <term>epicycle</term> <note>A small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle (the deferent)</note> around its own center and poles is non-uniform <note>original: *difformis*; meaning the planet does not move at a constant speed relative to the center of its own circular path</note>. However, this non-uniformity follows a specific rule of regularity: the center of the epicycle moves at a constant speed <note>original: *regulariter*</note> around a certain point located on the line of the apogee. This point is situated as far from the center of this orb <note>the deferent</note> as that center is from the center of the world <note>the Earth</note>. For this reason, that point is called the **center of the equant** <note>original: *centrum equantis*; the "equant" is an imaginary point used by Ptolemaic astronomers to account for the observed speeding up and slowing down of planets</note>. And the circle imagined around that center, which is the same size as the deferent and lies in the same plane, is called the **eccentric equant**. Consequently, the opposite of what occurred in the case of the Moon happens here: namely, the closer the center of the epicycle is to the apogee...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of planetary orbits, specifically describing how the speed of a planet varies based on its distance from the apogee. It transitions into a detailed description of the epicycle's motion and its mathematical relationship to the Sun's motion, which was central to the Ptolemaic system for the superior planets.</meta>

...of the <term>deferent</term> <note>The large circular path that carries the smaller epicycle</note>, it moves that much slower; but the closer it is to the opposite point <note>the perigee, or the point closest to Earth</note>, the faster it moves.

¶ The **epicycle**, moreover, has two motions, of which one is in longitude <note>east-west along the zodiac</note> and the other is in latitude <note>north-south deviation from the ecliptic</note>. We will speak of the second one later.

¶ Its motion in longitude, however, is that by which the body of the planet, fixed within it, moves around its [the epicycle's] center. It carries the planet forward in the upper part in the **order of the signs** <note>original: *secundum successionem*; moving west to east through the zodiac</note>, and in the lower part it carries it in the opposite direction. Thus, in this respect, it behaves in the opposite manner to the epicycle of the Moon <note>The Moon's epicycle was thought to rotate in the opposite direction compared to the other planets</note>. The axis of this motion lies transversely across the circumference, sometimes parallel to the axis of the ecliptic and sometimes not, as will be seen. 

And this motion is non-uniform <note>irregular</note> relative to the center of the epicycle. However, this non-uniformity follows this rule: that the body of the planet, wherever it may be, moves away at a uniform rate from the point of the **mean apogee** of the epicycle. Therefore, just as in the case of the Moon, it follows of necessity that the mean apogee of the epicycle and the true apogee vary continuously. Furthermore, the motion of the epicycle's revolution upon its own center is faster through the upper half of the deferent, and slower through the lower half.

The revolution of the epicycle has this measure: it revolves precisely once in the amount of time that passes from one **mean conjunction** <note>The moment when the average positions of the Sun and a planet align</note> of the Sun and that planet to the next one following. So it is that in every mean conjunction, the center of the planet's body is at the mean apogee of the epicycle. Consequently, in every such mean opposition, the planet will be at the point opposite the apogee of the epicycle <note>the point closest to the Earth on the epicycle</note>.

Let it be, therefore, that the center of the planet's body is always as many degrees and minutes away from the mean apogee of the epicycle as the line of the Sun's mean motion is from the line of the planet's mean motion. Therefore, when the mean motion of the planet is subtracted from the mean motion of the Sun, the result must be the **mean argument** <note>original: *argumentum medium*; a mathematical value used to find the planet's position on its epicycle</note> of the planet. 

From this it appears to happen that the slower the center of the planet's epicycle travels around [its orbit], the faster its epicycle revolves. For because of such slowness, the mean conjunction of the Sun's motion with it returns more quickly. Also, the mean motion of any of these three <note>referring to the superior planets: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, when added to its motion in its own epicycle, is equal to the mean motion of the Sun in degrees and minutes.

¶ The **mean apogee** of the epicycle is shown by a line drawn from the center of the equant through the center of the epicycle. 

¶ But the **true apogee** is shown by a line drawn from the center of the world <note>the Earth</note> through the center of the epicycle. 

Between these two, along the longitude of the epicycle, nothing intervenes <note>i.e., they align perfectly</note> when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee or the opposite point of the deferent. But [this difference] is greatest...

<summary>This page explains the complex rotations of planetary epicycles, highlighting how a planet's position on its small circle (the epicycle) is mathematically tied to the Sun's mean motion. It also introduces the distinction between the mean and true apogees of the epicycle based on different geometric centers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of planetary motion within the Ptolemaic system, moving from the general movement of apogees to the specific geometric lines and arcs used to calculate a planet's position. It establishes the vocabulary for the "equation of the center," which is the correction required to reconcile the planet's uniform theoretical motion with its observed variable motion.</meta>

...they differ when they are near the **mean distances** <note>The points in an orbit where the planet is at its average distance from the center</note> of the deferent, which are determined by a line drawn perpendicularly from the center of the eccentric <term>deferent</term> <note>The large circle along which the epicycle travels</note> to the line of the apogee.

¶ The **apogee** <note>original: *Aux*; the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note> of a planet, in its second meaning, is the arc of the zodiac from Aries to the line of the apogee.

¶ The **line of the mean motion** of a planet or an epicycle is the one drawn from the center of the world <note>the Earth</note> to the zodiac, which is parallel to the line extending from the center of the <term>equant</term> <note>A mathematical point used to account for the observed slowing and speeding up of planetary motion</note> to the center of the epicycle.

¶ The **line of the true motion** of the epicycle is the one that goes out from the center of the world through the center of the epicycle to the zodiac.

¶ The **line of the true position** or motion of the planet is the one that extends from the center of the world through the center of the planet's actual body to the zodiac.

¶ The **mean motion** of a planet or epicycle is the arc of the zodiac measured from the beginning of Aries in the **order of the signs** <note>original: *secundum successionem*; moving west to east through the zodiac</note> to the line of the mean motion of the planet.

¶ The **true motion of the epicycle**, however, is measured up to the line of its true motion. But the **true motion of the planet** is calculated up to the line of the true motion of the planet.

¶ The **mean center** of a planet is the arc of the zodiac from the line of the apogee to the line of the mean motion of the epicycle.

¶ The **true or equated center** is counted from the line of the apogee to the line of the true motion of the epicycle.

¶ The **equation of the center** <note>original: *aequatio centri*; a mathematical correction added or subtracted to find the true position from the mean position</note> in the zodiac is the arc of the zodiac between the line of mean motion of the epicycle and the line of its true motion. This is zero when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee or the opposite point <note>the perigee, or the point closest to Earth</note> of the deferent.

¶ It is at its maximum, however, when it is at the **mean distances**. When the mean center is less than **six signs** <note>180 degrees, or half the zodiacal circle</note>, it is greater than the true center. Similarly, the mean motion of the planet is greater than the true motion of the epicycle. In this case, the equation of the center in the zodiac is subtracted from the mean center and also from the mean motion of the epicycle so that the true center and true motion of the epicycle remain. The opposite happens when the mean center is more than six signs.

¶ The **equation of the center in the epicycle** is the arc of the epicycle lying between its mean and true apogees. This is likewise zero when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee or its opposite point on the deferent; it is at its maximum at the mean distance of the deferent.

Just as is the proportion of the equation of the center in the zodiac to the whole zodiac, so is the proportion of the equation of the center in the epicycle to the whole epicycle. This is because, due to the parallel lines, the angle of the one is equal to the angle of the other. Therefore, once one is found in such cases, the other is obtained. While the equation of the center in the zodiac is subtracted from the mean center to obtain the true center, the equation of the center in the epicycle [is used] with the **mean argument** <note>the angle of the planet on its epicycle</note> to obtain the true argument...

<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of planetary motion within the Ptolemaic system. It specifically defines the "argument" (the position of the planet on its smaller circle) and the "equation" (the correction needed to find the actual position). It includes a diagram illustrating the relationship between the Earth, the eccentric center, and the equant.</meta>

...it adds, and conversely when this one adds, the other subtracts. For they alternately exceed and are exceeded by one another in equal measure.

¶ The **mean argument** <note>original: *argumentum medium*; in historical astronomy, an "argument" is an angle or arc used as an input to calculate a planet's position</note> of a planet is the arc of the **epicycle** <note>The smaller circle whose center moves along the path of the larger circle</note> measured from the mean **apogee** <note>original: *auge media*; the point on the epicycle farthest from the center of the equant</note> according to its motion toward the center of the planet's body.

¶ The **true argument**, however, is calculated from the **true apogee** <note>The point on the epicycle farthest from the center of the Earth</note>.

¶ The **equation of the argument** <note>original: *aequatio argumenti*; "equation" here means a "correction" or the difference between the mean and true positions</note> is the arc of the zodiac lying between the lines of the planet's true position and the epicycle's true position. Just as with the Moon, this equation is zero whenever the center of the planet's body is at the true apogee of the epicycle or the opposite point <note>the perigee, or the point closest to the Earth</note>. It is at its maximum, however, when the planet's body is on a line drawn from the **center of the world** <note>the Earth</note> tangent to the circumference of the epicycle, while the center of the epicycle is at the point opposite the apogee of the **deferent** <note>the perigee of the large circle along which the epicycle travels</note>.

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of planetary positions. The previous page established the definitions of the mean and true argument on the epicycle. This page explains how to correct for the changing distance of the epicycle from the Earth, which causes the planet’s apparent motion to speed up or slow down—a concept known as "proportional minutes."</meta>

...existing. But when the **equated argument** <note>original: *argumentum equatum*; the position of the planet on its small circle (epicycle) after correcting for the center of the larger circle</note> is less than six signs <note>180 degrees, or half a circle</note>, the line of the planet's true motion precedes the line of the epicycle's true motion. Therefore, the **equation of the argument** <note>the correction value</note> is then added to the true motion of the epicycle so that the true motion of the planet results; conversely, it happens that it is subtracted when the argument is more than six signs.

Moreover, it happens that the equations of the argument vary in these planets, just as in the **Moon**, because of the approach of the center of the epicycle to the center of the world <note>the Earth</note>. Hence, the equations of the individual arguments are larger when the center of the epicycle is at the point opposite the **apogee** <note>original: *oppositum augis*; the perigee, or the point on the large circle closest to Earth</note> of the **deferent** <note>the large path carrying the epicycle</note> than when it is at its **mean distances** <note>original: *longitudinibus mediis*; the points where the planet is at its average distance from Earth</note>. There they are also larger than when the epicycle is at the apogee of the deferent, always comparing the relative values with their corresponding relatives.

¶ Therefore, the excess of the equations of the arguments that occur when the center of the epicycle is at the mean distance of the deferent over the equations occurring while it is at the apogee are called **variations of the diameter at the greater distance** <note>original: *diversitates diametri longiores*; these values account for how the epicycle looks smaller when it is far away</note>. But the excess of those which occur when the center of the epicycle is situated at the point opposite the apogee over those occurring at the mean distance are called **variations of the diameter at the closer distance**.

Because the line extended from the center of the world to the apogee of the deferent is longer than the line drawn from the same center to the mean distance of the deferent: the excess of the former over the latter, divided into sixty equal parts, is called **longer proportional minutes** <note>original: *minuta proportionalia longiora*; these are "interpolation minutes" used to scale the correction values based on the planet's current distance</note>.

¶ Accordingly, while the line of the true motion of the epicycle is at the apogee of the deferent, it has all of these within the periphery of the deferent; but at the mean distance, it has none within, but all outside. In intermediate places, some are inside and some are outside; and they are more inside as much as the center of the epicycle is closer to the apogee of the deferent.

Similarly, the line extended from the center of the world to the mean distance of the deferent is longer than the line drawn from the same center to the point opposite the apogee of the deferent. The excess of this over that, divided into sixty equal parts, is called **proportional minutes at the closer distance**.

¶ Therefore, while the line of the true motion of the epicycle is at the mean distance, it has none of them outside the periphery of the deferent; but at the point opposite the apogee, it has all of them.

¶ However, the **equations of the arguments** <note>original: *aequationes argumentorum*; the geometric corrections used to find the planet's true position</note> that are recorded in the tables correspond to the center of the epicycle being located at the **mean distance** <note>original: *longitudine media*; the point where the planet is at its average distance from Earth</note> of the **deferent** <note>the large circular path that carries the epicycle</note>. But these, as has been said, are greater than those which occur while the epicycle is at the apogee; conversely, they are smaller than those occurring at the opposite of the apogee.

This diversity is categorized as "farther" if the proportional minutes relate to the farther distances, or "closer" if they relate to the closer distances. A proportional part of this diversity...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for adjusting the position of a planet based on its distance from Earth. The previous page explained that tables provide "equation" (correction) values for the planet when it is at its average distance, and that these must be adjusted if the planet's carrying circle (the epicycle) is at its farthest point (apogee) or nearest point (perigee).</meta>

...calculated according to the ratio of the **proportional minutes** <note>original: *minuta proportionalia*; a sexagesimal (base-60) scale used to interpolate between a planet's minimum and maximum distances</note> to sixty, along with the **equation of the argument** <note>original: *aequatio argumenti*; the geometric correction for the planet's position on its small circle</note> found in the table; this must be added to or subtracted from it. Specifically, it is added if the diversity is "nearer" and subtracted if it is "farther." Thus, the **true and adjusted equation of the argument** will be produced for that specific location of the center of the **epicycle** <note>the smaller circle that carries the planet around its main orbit</note>.

## On Venus.

**V**enus has three **orbs** <note>original: *orbes*; the nested celestial spheres that carry the planet</note> along with an epicycle, which are arranged for its position and movement in **longitude** <note>the position along the zodiac</note> just like those of the superior planets <note>the planets further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>.

For the orbs carrying the **apogee** <note>original: *aux/augem*; the point in the orbit farthest from Earth</note> move upon the axis of the zodiac according to the movement of the **eighth sphere** <note>the sphere of the fixed stars, which accounts for the slow drift known as precession</note>. This is done in such a way, however, that the apogee of its **eccentric** <note>original: *eccentrici*; a circular orbit whose center is not the Earth</note> is always located at that point of the zodiac beneath which the apogee of the Sun's eccentric lies. Therefore, once the Sun's apogee is known in its second sense, the apogee of Venus is also known to be the same. 

The orb carrying the epicycle, however, has two movements. One by which it proceeds in longitude toward the east, moving regularly upon the center of the **equant** <note>original: *equantis*; a mathematical point used to explain the varying speeds of planets</note> as with the superior planets; yet it does so in such a way that the center of the epicycle completes one revolution in precisely the same time that the orb carrying the Sun completes one. Indeed, Venus relates to the Sun in this: the line of its **mean motion** <note>the average speed of a planet, ignoring periodic fluctuations</note> terminates in the same place in the zodiac, according to longitude, as the line of the Sun's mean motion. Thus, once the Sun's mean motion is obtained, that of Venus is also obtained. Therefore, their **mean conjunction** <note>when two celestial bodies share the same average position</note> is constant. 

Furthermore, the movement of this **deferent** <note>the large circular path that carries the epicycle</note> in longitude always occurs on its imaginary axis, whose poles approach and recede from the poles of the zodiac in either direction. This is due to a separate movement of the eccentric in **latitude** <note>the distance north or south of the Sun's path</note>, which will be discussed later. For this reason, that which happens to the superior planets—namely, that the apogee of the eccentric does not cross the **ecliptic** <note>the Sun's path across the sky</note>—does not apply here; rather, it sometimes declines toward the south and sometimes toward the north, as will become clear. 

But its epicycle moves with a double motion: that is, in longitude and in latitude. In longitude, it moves like the epicycles of the superior planets, yet it completes one revolution approximately every nineteen solar months. Hence, in this regard, it does not mirror the Sun as the superior planets do. The definitions of terms here are the same in all respects as in the three superior planets.

<summary>This page concludes the calculation method for planetary equations and begins the description of Venus's unique orbital mechanics, specifically how its motion is tied to the Sun's position.</summary>
<keywords>Venus, epicycle, mean motion, apogee, eccentric, zodiac, equant, longitude, latitude, planetary theory</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general discussion of planetary equations and the specific model for Venus to the unique and more complex orbital theory of Mercury. It establishes the physical arrangement of the "orbs" (celestial spheres) that account for Mercury's observed motion.</meta>

# On Mercury.

**Mercury** has five **orbs** <note>original: *orbes*; the nested, physical celestial spheres thought to carry the planets</note> and one **epicycle** <note>the smaller circle upon which the planet itself rotates while being carried by a larger circle</note>. Of these orbs, the two outermost are **eccentric** <note>having a center point that is not the Earth</note> in a certain way <note>original: *secundum quid*; meaning they are not perfectly eccentric in every respect</note>. Specifically, the outer convex surface of the highest orb and the inner concave surface of the lowest orb are **concentric** with the world <note>meaning their center is the Earth</note>. However, the inner concave surface of the highest orb and the outer convex surface of the lowest orb are eccentric to the world, yet they are concentric to one another.

The center of these surfaces is separated from the center of the **equant** <note>an imaginary point used to account for the observed changes in a planet's speed</note> by the same distance that the center of the equant is separated from the **center of the world** <note>the Earth</note>. This point is the center of the **small circle** <note>original: *parvi circuli*; a unique feature of the Mercurial model where the center of the main carrying circle itself revolves in a small loop</note> which the center of the **deferent** <note>the main circle that carries the epicycle</note> traces, as will be seen. These orbs are called the "carriers of the apogees" <note>original: *deferentes auges*; referring to the highest points of the orbit</note> of the equant and are moved according to the motion of the **eighth sphere** <note>the sphere of the fixed stars, which accounts for the slow drift of precession</note> upon the axis of the zodiac.

Between these two outermost orbs are two others of a similarly irregular thickness <note>original: *difformis spissitudinis*; meaning the "walls" of the spheres vary in thickness to accommodate the eccentricities</note> within themselves...

- **Center of the Deferent** (c Def)

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Mercury's unique orbital model, specifically detailing the movement of the "fifth orb" and its relationship to the "small circle" (the mechanism that accounts for Mercury's unusual path). It transitions from the physical arrangement of the spheres to the mathematical synchronicity between Mercury, Venus, and the Sun.</meta>

...placing the **fifth orb**, namely the one **carrying the epicycle** <note>the "deferent" orb that holds the smaller circle upon which the planet sits</note>. For the convex surface of the upper [carrier] and the concave surface of the lower have the same center as the **small circle** <note>a specific feature of Mercury's model where the center of its orbit itself moves in a tiny loop</note>.

But the concave surface of the upper and the convex of the lower, together with both surfaces of the fifth orb, have another, mobile center: which is called the **center of the deferent**. These two orbs are called the "carriers of the eccentric apogees" <note>original: *auges eccētrici*; the points where the eccentric orbit is furthest from the center</note>. They move regularly around the center of the small circle **against the succession of the signs** <note>original: *contra successionem signorum*; meaning they move clockwise, or East to West, relative to the stars</note> with such speed that precisely in the time that the line of the **mean motion of the Sun** <note>the average position of the Sun if its speed were constant</note> completes one revolution, these orbs also complete one in the opposite direction. And this motion occurs on an axis that is sometimes parallel to the axis of the **zodiac** and passing through the center of the small circle. Now, from the movement of these orbs it follows that the center of the orb carrying the epicycle similarly traces the circumference of a certain small circle regularly in that same amount of time.

The radius of this small circle is equal to the distance by which the **center of the equant** <note>the mathematical point used to regulate the planet's speed</note> is separated from the **center of the world** <note>the Earth</note>. Therefore, this circumference will pass through the center of the equant.

¶ But the fifth orb carrying the epicycle, located within the two second [orbs], moves in longitude **following the succession of the signs** <note>original: *secundum successionem signorum*; West to East</note> by carrying the center of the epicycle regularly around the center of the equant. This equant center is located exactly in the middle between the center of the world and the center of the small circle. It has such speed that the center of the epicycle revolves once in the time that the line of the mean motion of the Sun completes one revolution. For Mercury relates to the Sun in this regard just as **Venus** does. For let it always be that the mean motion of the Sun is also the mean motion of these two planets.

¶ From these things and what was said above, it is clear that each of the six planets <note>the five visible planets plus the Moon</note> shares something in their motions with the **Sun**: and that its motion is like a certain common mirror and measuring rule for their motions. Moreover, the motion of this orb carrying the epicycle occurs on an imaginary axis whose ends—just as appeared in Venus—approach the poles of the zodiac and recede from them, because of another motion it has in **latitude** <note>the planet's North-South movement relative to the ecliptic</note>. Nevertheless, this axis is mobile in its entirety according to the motion of the center of the deferent in the small circle.

¶ It is clear, therefore, that just as in the **Moon** the center of the epicycle passes the carriers of the eccentric apogees twice in a lunar month, so in Mercury the center of the epicycle traverses the carriers of the apogees of the epicycle-carrying orb twice in a year. Not however...

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric explanation of Mercury's orbit. It follows the description of the "small circle" mechanism that moves the center of Mercury's main carrying circle (the deferent), explaining how this causes the planet's highest point (apogee) to oscillate rather than rotate fully like other celestial bodies.</meta>

...is in the **apogee of the deferent** <note>the point in the orbit furthest from Earth</note> only once. ¶ For the **apogee** <note>original: *aux*; the "highest" point in an eccentric orbit</note> of the **deferent of Mercury** does not move in a circular fashion, completing full circular revolutions as happens with the **Moon**. Instead, because of the motion of the center of the deferent within a **small circle** <note>a unique device in Mercury's model where the center of its main orbit itself moves in a tiny loop</note>, it proceeds sometimes **according to the succession of the signs** <note>moving forward through the zodiac from West to East</note> and sometimes **against it** <note>moving backward, or East to West</note>. Indeed, it has certain limits which it cannot cross when moving away from the **apogee of the equant** <note>the highest point relative to the mathematical "equant" point that regulates the planet's speed</note>. Rather, it continually turns and returns, rising and falling, within an arc of the **zodiac** contained between two lines drawn from the **center of the world** <note>the Earth</note> to the zodiac that touch the edges of that small circle. For whenever the **center of the epicycle** <note>the center of the smaller circle upon which the planet itself sits</note> is at the apogee of the deferent, it will also be at the apogee of the equant due to the synchronization of these movements.

...and the **center of the deferent** <note>the center of the main carrying circle</note> is at the **apogee** <note>the highest or furthest point</note> of its **small circle**. Because of this, the **center of the epicycle** <note>the smaller circle that carries the planet itself</note> will then be at its greatest possible distance from the **center of the world** <note>original: *centro mundi*; the Earth</note>. At this point, the **center of the deferent** will be twice as far from the **center of the equant** <note>a mathematical point used to explain the varying speed of planets</note> as the **center of the equant** is from the **center of the world**.

...line touching the circle on the western side. This happens when it has moved **four signs** <note>120 degrees; in ancient astronomy, a "sign" is a 30-degree segment of the zodiac</note> from the **apogee** <note>the highest or furthest point</note> of the small circle. At that time, similarly, the **center of the epicycle** <note>the smaller circle carrying the planet</note> will be four signs distant from the **apogee of the equant** <note>the mathematical point of uniform motion</note> toward the east. The **apogee of the deferent** <note>the main carrying circle</note> will then be at its greatest distance from the **apogee of the equant** toward the west. In this position, the **center of the epicycle** reaches its greatest proximity <note>original: *accessione*; literally an "approach"</note> to the **center of the world** <note>the Earth</note>. However, it will not yet be at the point directly opposite the **apogee of the deferent**, nor on the line drawn from the **center of the world** touching the small circle.

For after the **center of the deferent** descends toward the **apogee of the equant**, the **center of the epicycle** will move proportionally in the other half of its path toward the point opposite the **apogee of the equant**. Consequently, it will move further away from the **center of the world** and will not reach the point opposite the **apogee of the deferent** until it is itself at the opposite of the **apogee of the equant**. This will occur when the **center of the deferent** reaches the **center of the equant**; at that moment, the **apogee of the deferent** will also align with the **apogee of the equant**. Since both the **deferent** and the **equant** are established as equal in size, they will form a single circle. At this time, the **center of the epicycle** will be further from the **center of the world** than it was when it was positioned four signs from the **apogee of the equant**.

From this point, when the **center of the deferent** recedes from the **center of the equant** by ascending in its circle, the **center of the epicycle** will move away from the opposite of the **apogee of the equant** and **deferent**, and it will continually get closer to the **center of the world**. But the **apogee of the deferent** will move away from the **apogee of the equant** toward the east continually until the **center of the deferent** reaches the line touching the small circle on the eastern side. This point of contact is also **four signs** distant from the **apogee** of the small circle toward the east. For then, the **apogee of the deferent** will be at its greatest distance from the **equant's apogee** toward the east, and the **center of the epicycle** will again be at its greatest possible proximity to the Earth. Even so, it will not be at the point opposite the **apogee of the deferent**.

From this location, as the **center of the deferent** ascends toward the **apogee** of the small circle, the **apogee of the deferent** will continually return toward the **apogee of the equant**. The **center of the epicycle** will move further away from the **center of the world** by ascending toward the **apogee of the equant** until the **center of the deferent** reaches the **apogee** of the small circle. For then, the **apogee of the deferent** will be with the **apogee of the equant**, and the **center of the epicycle** similarly both in...

It describes the complex geometry where the deferent and equant circles occasionally align or diverge to account for Mercury's unique motion.</summary>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Mercury's complex orbital path. It follows from the previous page's discussion of the center of the deferent moving toward a specific geometric alignment.</meta>

...the **apogee** <note>original: *auge*; the furthest point from the center</note> of the **deferent** <note>the main carrying circle</note> and the **equant** <note>the mathematical point of uniform speed</note>. From this point, it will again be at its maximum distance from the **center of the world** <note>the Earth</note> just as it was at first; and then, the same cycle of change as already described will return.

¶ From these observations, it appears first that only once in a year is the center of the **deferent** identical to the center of the **equant**. At all other times, however, the center of the **deferent** is always further from the **center of the world** than the center of the **equant**. Therefore, the opposite of what occurs with the higher planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> and Venus happens here: namely, that the closer the **center of the epicycle** <note>the smaller circle upon which the planet itself moves</note> is to the **apogee of the equant**, the faster it moves; and the closer it is to the opposite point, the slower its motion.

Second, although the **center of the epicycle** is at its greatest distance from the **center of the world** only once a year, it happens to reach its closest possible distance <note>original: *maxima appinquatione*</note> twice. Similarly, while it reaches its maximum approach twice a year, it is found only once a year at the point directly opposite the **apogee of the deferent**.

Third, it is necessary that the point opposite the **apogee of the deferent** always stays between the **center of the epicycle** and the point opposite the **apogee of the equant**, while the **center of the epicycle** is located outside the **apogee of the equant** or its opposite; sometimes it turns toward the **center of the epicycle**, and sometimes away from it, rolling along by both preceding and following it.

Fourth, just as the **apogee of the deferent** moves to certain limits on either side of the **apogee of the equant**, the same holds true for the point opposite the **apogee of the deferent** in relation to the point opposite the **apogee of the equant**. However, the arc of this motion of the **apogee of the deferent** is larger than the arc of motion of its opposite point. Thus, the motion of one will be faster than the motion of the other.

Fifth, even if the **center of the epicycle** happens to be at the point of the **deferent** furthest from the **center of the world**, it is never at that point of the **deferent** which happens to be closest to the **center of the world**. For while the **center of the epicycle** is at the **apogee of the deferent**, the arrangement of the **deferent** is such that its opposite point <note>the perigee</note> is so close to the **center of the world** that in no other arrangement of the **deferent** can any point be found closer or even as close to the **center of the world**. Yet, at the time that point is at its closest, the **center of the epicycle** is not there, but rather at the opposite side.

Sixth, from what has been said, it clearly appears that the **center of the epicycle** of Mercury, because of the motions described above, does not describe a circular circumference of the **deferent** as happens with other planets, but rather a shape resembling a **flat oval** <note>original: *plana ovali*</note>.

It concludes with the significant observation that Mercury's path is oval rather than perfectly circular.</summary>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Mercury's orbital mechanics, specifically detailing how its distance from the Earth is calculated using proportional minutes, and begins a new section defining the standard movements of all planets (direct, retrograde, and stationary).</meta>

...the [greatest] distance occurs when the **center of the epicycle** <note>original: *centrum epicycli*</note> has moved four signs away from the **apogee of its equant** <note>original: *auge equantis*; the point in the orbit where the planet is furthest from the center of uniform speed</note>. In the case of other planets, however, this occurred when the center of the epicycle was at the point opposite the apogee of the equant. 

Therefore, the **longer proportional minutes** are the difference between the greatest distance of the center of the epicycle and its mean distance, divided into sixty equal parts. But the **closer proportional minutes** are defined as the difference between the mean distance of the center of the epicycle and its minimum distance, likewise divided into 60 equal parts. According to this, a double variation of the diameter is defined. However, because the **closer proportional minutes** for Mercury decrease as it moves from the point of its greatest approach toward the point opposite the apogee—having previously increased continuously from the mean distance to the point of greatest approach—it is said that for Mercury, the **proportional minutes** behave in a threefold manner. In Venus and the three **superior planets** <note>original: *tribus superioribus*; Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> they behave in a twofold manner, while for the Moon they are simple, as has been clearly shown.

### On the Various Conditions of the Planets.

<image-desc>Decorative drop-cap letter P featuring dense foliate scrollwork inside a rectangular border.</image-desc> A planet is said to be **direct** <note>original: *directus*</note> when the line of its true motion proceeds according to the succession of the signs of the zodiac. It is **retrograde** <note>original: *retrogradus*</note>, however, when it moves against that succession. It is **stationary** <note>original: *stationarius*</note> while this line appears to stand still. 

The **first station** in its first meaning is the point on the epicycle where the planet begins to move retrograde. The **second station** in its first meaning is the point on the epicycle where the planet begins to move direct. These stations—when the center of the epicycle is at the same location on the **deferent** <term>deferent: the large circular path that carries the smaller epicycle around the Earth</term>—are equally distant from the point opposite the **true apogee of the epicycle** <note>original: *auge vera epicycli*</note>. 

The **first station** in its second meaning is the **arc of the epicycle** lying between the true apogee of the epicycle and the point of the first station. The **second station** in its second meaning is the arc of the epicycle from the true apogee, through its opposite point, to the point of the second station. 

The **arc of direction** <note>original: *arcus directionis*</note> is the arc of the epicycle from the second station, through the apogee, to the first station in the first sense. The **arc of retrogradation** <note>original: *arcus retrogradationis*</note>, however, is the arc of the epicycle from the point of the first station, through the point opposite the apogee, to the point of the second station. These arcs increase or decrease because of the variation of the aforementioned points. For as much as the center of the epicycle...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary movements, moving from the specific calculations of Mercury’s distances to the general theory of stationary points and retrogradation for all planets. It links the position of the epicycle relative to the equant to the timing and duration of these astronomical events.</meta>

...the closer it is to the point opposite the **apogee of the equant** <note>original: *opposito augis equantis*; the perigee, or the point in the orbit closest to the center of uniform motion</note>, the closer the **stationary points** <note>original: *puncta stationum*; the positions where a planet appears to stop before changing direction</note> are to the point opposite the **true apogee of the epicycle** <note>original: *verae augis epicycli*</note>. This same effect occurs more significantly the larger the planet's **epicycle** <note>original: *epicyclum*; the smaller circle on which the planet moves</note> is and the slower its **motion of argument** <note>original: *motum argumenti*; the planet's movement along its epicycle</note> becomes. For this reason, the durations of **direct motions** <note>original: *directionum*</note> or **retrogradations** <note>original: *retrogradationum*; the apparent backward movement of a planet</note> vary in their total amounts. Such a duration is determined by dividing the arc of that motion by the planet's daily motion of argument.

¶ From what has been said, it follows that if the **first station** <note>original: *statio prima*; the point where the planet stops before turning retrograde</note> is subtracted from the full circle, the **second station** <note>original: *statio secunda*; the point where it stops before returning to direct motion</note> remains. Furthermore, by subtracting the first station from the second station, one will have the **arc of retrogradation** <note>original: *arcus retrogradationis*</note>. If this is then taken away from the full circle, the **arc of direction** <note>original: *arcus directionis*; the path of normal forward motion</note> remains.

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the general discussion of planetary motions, moving from the mathematical variables of retrogradation to the specific case of the Moon and a series of definitions for planetary speed, visibility, and rising/setting conditions.</meta>

Although the **Moon** <note>original: *Lunae*</note> also possesses an **epicycle** <note>original: *epicyclum*; a smaller circle upon which a planet moves, which itself moves along a larger circle</note>, just like the other five planets, a **stationary point** <note>original: *statio*; when a planet appears to stop moving</note> or **retrogradation** <note>original: *retrogradatio*; the appearance of a planet moving backward through the stars</note> does not occur for it. This is due to the high speed of the motion of the center of its epicycle. For the center of its epicycle always describes a larger arc of the **zodiac** <note>original: *zodiaci*</note> each day in the **order of the signs** <note>original: *secundum successionem*; "in succession," referring to direct motion through the zodiac from Aries to Taurus, etc.</note> than the corresponding arc of the zodiac traveled by the center of the Moon’s body on any given day as it moves through the upper part of its epicycle. Nevertheless, it is necessary that the Moon becomes slow in its course while it is in the upper half of the epicycle, and fast in its course while in the lower half.

¶ Planets are said to be **slow** and **diminished in course** when the line of their true motion moves more slowly than the line of their **mean motion** <note>original: *medij motus*; the theoretical average position of a planet</note>, or when it moves against the order of the signs.

¶ They are **fast** and **increased in course** when they move more quickly in the order of the signs.

¶ They are **increased in number** when the **equation** <note>original: *equatio*; the mathematical correction added to the mean position to find the true position</note> is added to the mean motion, and **diminished** when it is subtracted.

¶ They are **increased in light** when they move away from the **Sun** <note>original: *Sole*</note> or the Sun moves away from them. They are **diminished in light** when they approach the Sun or the Sun approaches them.

¶ They are **oriental and morning-stars** when they rise before the Sun. They are **occidental and evening-stars** when they set after the Sun.

¶ Those **rising in the morning** are they who, emerging from beneath the Sun’s rays because of their distance from the Sun, begin to appear in the evening after sunset.

¶ Those **setting in the morning** are they who enter the rays of the Sun and, because of their approach to the Sun, begin to be hidden in the morning.

¶ Those **setting in the evening** are they who enter the Sun’s rays and, because of their approach to the Sun—or the Sun’s approach to them—begin to be hidden in the evening after sunset. The three **superior planets** <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> do not set in a morning setting, nor do they rise in an evening rising; but **Venus**, **Mercury**, and the **Moon** do.

¶ There is a threefold reason why the Moon, after its **conjunction** <note>original: *coiunctione*; when two celestial bodies reach the same longitude</note> with the Sun, appears sometimes sooner and sometimes later. One is the **declination** <note>the angle of the body relative to the celestial equator</note> or the obliquity of the zodiac and the **horizon**. For if the conjunction occurs under the **ecliptic** <note>original: *ecliptica*; the path of the Sun</note>, specifically in the half of the sky from the end of **Sagittarius** to the end of **Gemini**, then when the Sun is at the horizon while setting, there will be more degrees in the circle of the Moon’s revolution from the Moon to the horizon than there are on the zodiac from the Moon to the Sun. Hence, in the northern **climates** <note>original: *climatibus*; latitudinal zones of the Earth</note>, the Moon can be seen sooner than if it had been in the other half of the zodiac. The second reason is the **latitude** <note>distance north or south of the ecliptic</note> of the Moon from the ecliptic. For if after conjunction...

It also begins an explanation of why the new Moon's first visibility varies based on its position in the zodiac.</summary>
<keywords>Moon, epicycle, retrogradation, zodiacal succession, mean motion, planetary visibility, morning stars, evening stars, superior planets, conjunction, ecliptic, Sagittarius, Gemini</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of lunar visibility following a conjunction (New Moon) and introduces the geometric relationships (aspects) between planets.</meta>

If the Moon moves in a northern latitude <note>The "latitude" refers to the Moon's distance north or south of the ecliptic, the sun's apparent path.</note>, it can be seen again more quickly than if it were moving in a southern latitude. The third cause is the velocity of the Moon’s true motion. For if it is swift in its motion, it appears sooner than if it were slow. It happens, therefore, that sometimes all these causes concur: then the Moon appears as both "true" and "new" on the same day. Sometimes only two causes concur: then it appears on the second day after the conjunction. Sometimes, however, only one cause is present: then it is seen on the third day. Occasionally, the opposite of all these conditions occurs: then it happens that the Moon appears only on the fourth day.

The **Trine** aspect of the planets occurs when their true positions are distant by a third part of the ecliptic <note>120 degrees</note>. The **Square** aspect occurs when they are distant by a fourth part <note>90 degrees</note>. The **Sextile** aspect occurs when they are distant by a sixth part of the ecliptic <note>60 degrees</note>. A **mean conjunction** of the planets occurs when the lines of their mean motions <note>The theoretical average position of a planet, ignoring its elliptical irregularities</note> meet.

### Theory of aspects and rays.

<image-desc>A circular diagram representing the theory of astronomical aspects and planetary "rays." The outer circumference is marked with the twelve signs of the zodiac, arranged clockwise starting with Cancer (♋︎) at the top: Cancer, Gemini, Taurus, Aries, Pisces, Aquarius, Capricorn, Sagittarius, Scorpio, Libra, Virgo, and Leo. Inside the circle, a network of intersecting lines connects the signs to demonstrate angular relationships. Symbols for the primary aspects are placed on these lines: a triangle (△) for the trine aspect (120°), a square (□) for the quadrature or square aspect (90°), and a six-pointed asterisk (*) for the sextile aspect (60°). Small diamond shapes (◊) are also distributed among the internal lines. The center of the circle features a stylized conjunction symbol (☌) or similar geometric focal point.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary and lunar positions, specifically focusing on the geometric definitions of conjunction and opposition as viewed from Earth versus the center of the universe.</meta>

their positions are joined according to the longitude of the zodiac. However, a **true conjunction** occurs when the lines of their true motions meet in this way. But a **visible conjunction** <note>Also known as an apparent conjunction, which accounts for the observer's specific location on Earth's surface.</note> occurs when the lines drawn from our eye through the centers of their bodies are joined into one. The same must be said concerning **mean** and **true opposition**. And these positions are observed within the same sign, degree, and minute. From this, it is often evident that a true conjunction exists when the mean conjunction has already passed or is yet to come; often also that a true conjunction exists when a visible one does not; and sometimes the visible precedes the true, while at other times it follows. ¶ The **true place** of a star is the point on the firmament <note>The sphere of the fixed stars.</note> determined by a line drawn from the center of the world <note>The center of the Earth in this geocentric model.</note> through the center of the star. However, the **visual** or **apparent place** is defined by a line drawn from the eye through the center of the star.

## ¶ Theory of the conjunction and opposition of the luminaries. <note>The "luminaries" refers specifically to the Sun and the Moon.</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of celestial measurements, specifically focusing on the geometric definition of parallax and its application to the Moon and Mars.</meta>

The parallax <note>original: "Diversitas astri"; literally "diversity of the star," the standard medieval term for parallax</note> of a celestial body is the arc of a great circle passing through the zenith and the true position of the body, intercepted between the true and apparent positions of said body. From this, it is evident that the closer a body is to the center of the world <note>the Earth</note> and to the horizon, the greater its parallax will be. This is found to be greatest in the Moon. In Mars, however, it is not easily perceptible. For the semi-diameter of the Earth has a significant size relative to the semi-diameter of the Moon's orbit, but it does not have a very perceptible size relative to the semi-diameter of the orbit of Mars.

The parallax of a celestial body in longitude is the arc of the ecliptic <note>the sun's apparent path</note> intercepted between two great circles, one of which passes through the poles of the ecliptic and the position <insert>of the body</insert>

### Theory of the Lunar Eclipse

<note>The following diagram illustrates the geometric path of the Moon as it enters and exits the Earth's shadow. The grid lines help the astronomer calculate the exact duration and depth of the eclipse based on the principles of longitude and latitude described above.</note>

<keywords>parallax, longitude, latitude, zodiac, ecliptic, lunar eclipse, Earth's shadow, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astronomical calculations, specifically focusing on the geometry of solar and lunar eclipses and the measurement of celestial bodies.</meta>

The solar parallax <note>original: "diversitas aspectus"; the difference in the apparent position of a celestial body when viewed from the Earth's surface versus its center</note> is the excess of the lunar parallax over the solar parallax. If the true conjunction of the luminaries <note>the Sun and Moon</note> occurs between the ascending degree of the ecliptic and its ninetieth degree from the ascendant, their visible conjunction precedes the true one. However, if it occurs between that same ninetieth degree and the western degree <note>the descendant</note>, the visible conjunction will follow the true one. But if it happens exactly at the ninetieth degree, then the visible conjunction will occur simultaneously with the true one, and no parallax in longitude will occur. For the ninetieth degree of the ecliptic from the ascendant is always on the circle passing through the zenith and the poles of the zodiac.

The visible latitude of the Moon is the arc of a great circle passing through the poles of the zodiac and the true or visible position of the Moon, intercepted between the ecliptic and a circle parallel to it passing through the visible position.

Ecliptic digits <term>Digiti ecliptici: a unit of measurement equal to one-twelfth of the diameter of the solar or lunar disk</term> are defined as twelfths of the diameter of the solar or lunar body being eclipsed.

The minutes of the fall <note>the duration from the start of an eclipse to its midpoint</note> in a lunar eclipse are the minutes of the zodiac which the Moon traverses while overtaking the Sun, from the beginning of the eclipse until its middle—if it is a partial eclipse, or a total one without delay <note>without staying in the shadow</note>. Or, from the beginning until the start of total obscuration, if it is a total eclipse with delay.

The minutes of half-delay are the minutes of the zodiac which the Moon traverses while overtaking the Sun from the beginning of total obscuration until its middle.

The minutes of the fall in a solar eclipse are the minutes which the Moon completes by its overtaking of the Sun from the beginning of the eclipse until the middle. Therefore, if these minutes are divided by the Moon's hourly rate of overtaking, the time it takes to traverse them will result.

The visual diameter of the Sun at the apogee <term>auge: the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</term> of the eccentric measures 31 minutes; but at the opposite point <note>perigee</note>, thirty-four. However, the proportion of 5 to 66 is always the same as the ratio of the Sun's hourly motion to its visual diameter. For the Moon, at the apogee of the eccentric and the epicycle <term>epicycli: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one in Ptolemaic astronomy</term>, it is 29 minutes; but at the apogee of the eccentric and the opposite of the apogee of the epicycle, it is thirty-six. However, the proportion of 48 to 47 is always the same as the ratio of the Moon's hourly motion to its visual diameter. From this it follows that it is even possible for a total solar eclipse to occur sometimes.

<summary>This text defines the geometric principles of eclipses, explaining how parallax affects the timing of conjunctions and providing specific ratios for calculating the diameters of the Sun and Moon based on their orbital positions.</summary>
<keywords>parallax, conjunction, ecliptic, digits, solar eclipse, lunar eclipse, apogee, epicycle, celestial mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
Nevertheless, it can never naturally appear, by reason of the diversity of aspect <note>original: "diversitatis aspectus"; this refers to parallax, the difference in the apparent position of an object viewed along two different lines of sight</note>, that the entire Sun is universally eclipsed to the whole Earth at once. When the Sun is at the apogee <note>original: "auge"; the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note> of its eccentric circle, the diameter of the shadow at the place of the Moon's passage relates to the visual diameter of the Moon as thirteen is to five. Moreover, the excess of its diameter while the Sun is at the apogee, compared to its diameter when the Sun is elsewhere in the eccentric, is tenfold to the difference of the Sun's motions in the hour during which it moves at the apogee versus that other location.

## On Declination and Latitude.

The **declination** <note>original: "Declinatio"</note> of a star is its distance from the **equinoctial** <note>The celestial equator, the projection of Earth's equator into space.</note>. It is calculated on a circle passing through the poles of the world and the true location of the star, which is designated by a line drawn from the center of the world through the center of the body of the star.

The **latitude** <note>original: "Latitudo"</note> of a star, however, is its distance from the **ecliptic** <note>The sun's apparent annual path across the sky.</note> and is calculated on a circle passing through the poles of the ecliptic and the true location of the star, as just described.

From these points and what was said above regarding the **Sun**, it is manifest that the Sun has no latitude, although it does have declination. This is because the surface of its **deferent** <note>The orbital path or "carrying" sphere of a celestial body.</note> always remains within the surface of the ecliptic.

Various polar arcs converge at points labeled "pole of the world" and "pole of the zodiac." The diagram is oriented with "North" at the top left and "South" at the bottom right. # On the Latitudes of the Planets

The **Moon** and the other five planets possess latitude. In the case of the **Moon**, because the axis of the movers of its apogee <note>The "apogee" is the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> is inclined from the axis of the zodiac, the flat surface of its deferent <note>The "deferent" is the main circular path carrying the smaller epicycle.</note> always intersects the flat surface of the **ecliptic** <note>The ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun across the sky.</note> along the diameter of the world. It inclines from that ecliptic into opposite directions, while the quantity of its maximum inclination always remains invariably the same. Furthermore, the flat surface of its **epicycle** <note>A smaller circle whose center moves around the circumference of the deferent.</note> never recedes from the surface of the deferent. For this reason, the Moon has only one type of latitude: that which occurs because of the inclination of the deferent from the ecliptic. This is identified by the **true argument of latitude** of the Moon.

The **mean argument of latitude** of the Moon is the arc of the zodiac taken between the line of the true motion of the **Head of the Dragon** <note>original: "caput draconis"; this refers to the Ascending Node, where the Moon crosses the ecliptic moving north.</note> and the line of the mean motion of the Moon, following the succession of the signs.

The **true argument of latitude** of the Moon is the arc of the zodiac measured from the line of the true motion of the Head to the line of the true motion of the Moon, following the succession. Therefore, by subtracting the true motion of the Head from the true position of the Moon, or by adding the true motion of the Moon to the mean motion of the Head, the true argument of the Moon's latitude will be produced.

The **three superior planets** <note>Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</note> truly have a double latitude. One occurs because of the inclination of the surface of the deferent from the surface of the ecliptic into opposite parts, just as with the Moon; the maximum quantity always remains invariable. However, the intersections of the deferents with the ecliptic along the diameter of the world—which are also called the **Head** and **Tail** <note>The nodes of the orbit.</note>—do not move against the succession of the signs as they do in the Moon, but rather, as has been said, according to the motion of the **eighth sphere** <note>The sphere of the fixed stars.</note>. Thus, the apogees of those deferents always describe circumferences of the ecliptic equidistant from the north.

Although their apogees are always northern, the points of maximum latitude of the deferents from the ecliptic are not the same in all three. Indeed, only in **Mars** is it the case that the apogee of the deferent inclines most toward the north <note>original: "aquilonem"</note> from the ecliptic. But in **Saturn**, such a point is distant before the apogee of its deferent—that is, against the succession—by fifty degrees. In **Jupiter**, however, it is after the apogee—that is, following the succession—by twenty degrees. They have another latitude on the part of the flat surface of the epicycle, which at times inclines from the flat surface of the deferent.

In **Jupiter**, for the epicycle in latitude with respect to the apogee...
