of the true <term>apogee</term> <note>original: "aux"; the highest point in an orbit</note> upon its own axis passing through its center and the mean longitudes, in such a way that when the <term>center of the epicycle</term> <note>the small circle on which the planet moves</note> is in the <term>node</term> <note>the intersection point of the planetary path and the ecliptic</note> of the head or the tail <note>the ascending or descending nodes</note>, the true <term>apogee</term> and the opposite point of the <term>epicycle</term> are directly in the surface of the <term>deferent</term> <note>the large carrying circle</note> and the surface of the <term>epicycle</term> is in the surface of the <term>ecliptic</term> <note>the sun's apparent path</note>. However, after it recedes from the <term>node</term>, the <term>diameter</term> of the <term>apogees</term> of the <term>epicycle</term> begins to decline from the surface of the <term>deferent</term>, so that the opposite of the true <term>apogee</term> of the <term>epicycle</term> begins to be moved away from the surface of the <term>deferent</term> toward that part to which the half of the <term>deferent</term> (through which the <term>center of the epicycle</term> then begins to move) begins to move from the <term>ecliptic</term>: and the true <term>apogee</term> of the epicycle moves just as much toward the opposite part. And thus the <term>apogee</term> and the opposite of the <term>apogee</term> of the <term>epicycle</term> are continually moved away from the surface of the <term>deferent</term> until the <term>center</term> of the epicycle reaches the point of the <term>deferent</term> declining most greatly from the <term>ecliptic</term>, namely the midpoint between the two <term>nodes</term>. There, the surface of the <term>epicycle</term> with the said <term>diameter</term> declines most greatly from the <term>deferent</term>. From this place, however, the declination of the <term>epicycle</term> from the <term>deferent</term> is successively diminished until the center of the <term>epicycle</term> reaches the other <term>node</term>, in which again the entire surface of the <term>epicycle</term> will be in the surface of the <term>ecliptic</term>, and the <term>diameter</term> of the true <term>apogees</term> will be in the surface of the <term>deferent</term>. Whence the <term>axis</term> upon which this motion in <term>latitude</term> <note>north-south distance from the ecliptic</note> occurs will always be equidistant to the surface of the <term>ecliptic</term> as long as the <term>center</term> of the <term>epicycle</term> is outside the <term>nodes</term>.

From these things it appears, first, that the <term>axis</term> (as was said above) upon which the revolution of the <term>epicycle</term> in <term>longitude</term> occurs will sometimes be equidistant to the axis of the <term>ecliptic</term>; at other times, however, it will not; but it will never be equidistant to the axis of the <term>eccentric</term> <note>the off-center circle of the planet's orbit</note>.

Second, the body of the <term>planet</term>, as long as it is in the upper half of the <term>epicycle</term> while the center of the epicycle is outside the <term>nodes</term>, will always be between two surfaces, namely the <term>ecliptic</term> and its own <term>deferent</term>. However, when it is in the lower half of the epicycle, it will be further from the <term>ecliptic</term> than the <term>deferent</term> is from the same. Therefore, the star will not always be found between the <term>deferent</term> and the <term>ecliptic</term>.

Third, the true and mean <term>apogees</term> of the <term>epicycles</term> are not always the endpoints of the lines that are drawn through the center of the <term>epicycle</term>. Nevertheless, it happens that they are determined by such lines. Whence the mean <term>apogee</term> of the <term>epicycle</term> is always in a flat surface cutting the surface of the <term>deferent</term> orthogonally <note>at a right angle</note> in the line of the mean <term>apogee</term>; and the true <term>apogee</term> of the <term>epicycle</term> is in a similar surface cutting the <term>deferent</term>.

with respect to the true apogee <note>original: "augis vere"; the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note>, which is called the reflection. For the surface of the deferent <note>the large circular path that carries the smaller epicycle</note> moves in latitude, sometimes toward the north and sometimes toward the south, upon the diameter of the world. The poles of this motion are distant ninety degrees from the apogee of the equant <note>a mathematical point used to account for observed planetary speeds</note> on both sides of the ecliptic; for there the "head" and "tail" <note>the nodes where the paths intersect</note> are formed. However, this motion in latitude is proportioned to the motion of the center of the epicycle in such a way that when the center of the epicycle is in one of the nodes—namely, ninety degrees away from the apogee of the equant—there is no deviation of the deferent, but its entire surface lies within the surface of the ecliptic. Then, as the center of the epicycle moves away from the node, the deferent begins to deviate so that the half of it which the center of the epicycle enters always declines toward the north <note>original: "aquilonem"</note> in the case of Venus, but always toward the south <note>original: "austrum"</note> in the case of Mercury. And this deviation increases successively until the center of the epicycle reaches the apogee of the deferent or its opposite point. For then the deviation is at its maximum: ten minutes for Venus, but forty-five minutes for Mercury. This then continuously decreases until the center of the epicycle reaches the other node, where again no deviation occurs. Afterward, it happens again as before. From this it is clear that just as the center of the epicycle of Venus never deviates toward the south from the ecliptic, so the center of the epicycle of Mercury never happens to deviate toward the north. It is also manifest that the motion of the revolution of the center of the epicycle on the deferent is equal to the return of the deferent in latitude. From this it likewise appears that the poles upon which the motion of the deferent in longitude occurs (as was said above) now approach the poles of the zodiac and now move away from them. On account of these said deviations, it seems necessary to add another sphere concentric to the world, including all the aforementioned ones, by whose motion of trepidation <note>a back-and-forth oscillation</note> the predicted deviations occur. But the flat surface of the epicycle moves by declining this way and that from the surface of the deferent: first, upon the diameter of the epicycle passing through the mean longitudes from the apogee. By this motion, it happens that the diameter of the true apogee and its opposite cuts the surface of the deferent so that the true apogee declines from the deferent in one direction and the opposite point in the other; yet this declination is proportioned to the motion of the center of the epicycle in such a way that

Whenever the center of the <term>epicycle</term><note>A small circle on which a planet moves, while the center of that circle itself moves around a larger circle (the deferent).</note> is located at the <term>apogee of the equant</term><note>The point in the orbit furthest from the center of motion.</note>, the aforementioned diameter never deviates from the <term>deferent</term><note>The large circular path that carries the epicycle.</note>, but is positioned exactly within its surface. However, as the center of the epicycle moves away from that point, the true apogee of the epicycle begins to deviate from the surface of the deferent: in the case of **Venus**, it moves toward the north; in the case of **Mercury**, it moves toward the south. The point opposite the true apogee moves toward the opposite direction.

This <term>declination</term><note>A tilting or "leaning" away from the primary orbital plane.</note> increases continuously until the center of the epicycle reaches the <term>descending node</term><note>original: "nodum caudae" (the tail node). The point where the orbit crosses the ecliptic moving south.</note>, namely, when it is ninety degrees away from the apogee of the equant following the succession of the signs. At that point, the maximum declination of the said diameter occurs. Afterward, it continuously decreases until the center of the epicycle reaches the point opposite the apogee of the equant, where once again the said diameter does not deviate at all but is positioned within the surface of the deferent.

From there, as the center of the epicycle moves toward the other node, the true apogee begins to deviate from the surface of the deferent: in **Venus** toward the south, and in **Mercury** toward the north. The opposite point of the apogee moves toward the opposite side, and the declination increases successively until the center of the epicycle reaches the other node, where it again becomes maximal. From then on, it decreases until it comes to the apogee of the equant, where, just as at first, the said diameter will be in the surface of the deferent. From that point, the first arrangement returns. Therefore, whenever the maximum <term>deviation</term> of the deferent occurs, the epicycle has no declination; and when the deviation is zero, the declination is at its maximum.

Secondly, the flat surface of the epicycle moves by deviating from the surface of the deferent upon the diameter of the epicycle that passes through the true apogee and its opposite point. By this motion, it happens that the diameter of the epicycle passing through the <term>mean longitudes</term><note>The points 90 degrees away from the apogee and perigee of the epicycle.</note> from the true apogee sometimes intersects the surface of the deferent, such that the left half of the epicycle is reflected toward one side and the right half toward the other from the deferent. I call "left" that part which exists after the apogee of the epicycle according to the succession of the signs. This aforementioned <term>reflection</term><note>original: "reflexio." A second type of tilting motion specific to the inner planets.</note> of the diameter is also proportioned to the motion of the center of the epicycle in such a way that whenever the center of the epicycle is at the <term>ascending node</term><note>original: "nodum capitis" (the head node). The point where the orbit crosses the ecliptic moving north.</note>—that is, at the intersection ninety degrees before the apogee of the deferent against the succession of the signs—there is no reflection of the said diameter, but it is located in the same surface as the deferent. But as the center of the epicycle moves from here...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary latitudes, specifically focusing on the complex "reflexion" or tilting of the epicycles for the inner planets, Venus and Mercury.</meta>

As the center of the epicycle moves toward the apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth; Latin: *augem*</note>, the left or eastern half of the diameter—measured from the surface of the deferent <note>The large circular path that carries the smaller epicycle</note>—begins to tilt. In the case of **Venus**, it tilts toward the north; however, in **Mercury**, it tilts toward the south. The other half of the diameter moves toward the opposite direction. This "reflexion" <note>A specific type of latitudinal swaying or tilting of the epicycle's plane</note> increases continuously until the center of the epicycle reaches the apogee of the equant <note>The mathematical point used to account for the observed non-uniform motion of planets</note>, where the tilt becomes greatest.

After this point, as it moves toward the other node <note>The intersection of the planetary path and the ecliptic</note>, the tilt decreases until the center of the epicycle reaches that node, where no reflexion occurs. But from this location, as the center of the epicycle passes toward the opposite point of the apogee <note>The perigee, or closest point to Earth</note>, the left half of the diameter—passing through the mean longitudes—begins to tilt again: in **Venus** toward the south, but in **Mercury** toward the north. This will increase until it reaches the opposite point of the apogee, where it again becomes greatest. From here, it decreases successively until the center of the epicycle returns to the ascending node <note>Latin: *nodum capitis*, literally 'node of the head'</note>, where no reflexion will occur, and the prior state returns.

It is manifest, therefore, that at the location on the deferent where no "declination" <note>The primary slant of the epicycle</note> of the epicycle happens, its maximum "reflexion" occurs. Thus, "deviations" are measured from the **ecliptic** <note>The sun's apparent path</note>, while "declinations" and "reflexions" are calculated from the **deferent**. The values written in the tables are those that occur when these motions are at their maximum.

When the maximum reflexion occurs—namely, when the center of the epicycle is at the apogee or its opposite point—the very tip of the diameter that is tilting has a smaller reflexion than many other parts of the epicycle's circumference located toward the opposite of the apogee. However, the point on the circumference of the epicycle touched by a line drawn from the center of the world <note>The Earth</note> tangent to the epicycle then has a greater reflexion than the others.

Just as the motion of the epicycle's declination occurs upon the diameter that tilts, so conversely the motion of the epicycle's reflexion occurs upon the declining diameter. Thus, in turn, one is the axis for the motion of the other. Therefore, in these planets, unlike in the superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, the axis upon which the inclination of the epicycle occurs (when it is outside the nodes) does not need to be parallel to the surface of the ecliptic. Because of these stated inclinations and reflexions of the epicycle, some astronomers propose "small orbs" <note>Small auxiliary spheres or epicyculets</note> locating the epicycles within themselves, by whose motion these same phenomena occur.

<summary>This text explains the complex latitudinal "reflexion" (tilting) of Venus and Mercury, detailing how their epicycles sway north and south relative to their position from the apogee. It distinguishes between different types of orbital deviation and mentions the use of small auxiliary orbs to mathematically model these movements.</summary>

<keywords>Venus, Mercury, epicycle, apogee, reflexion, declination, deferent, equant, planetary latitude, celestial kinematics</keywords>

## On the motion of the eighth sphere.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'O' containing floral and scrollwork patterns, set within a square border.</image-desc> To the eighth sphere—by whose motion, as has often been said, the orbs carrying the <term>apogees</term><note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> of the planets are changed—there belong three types of motion. The first is indeed from the <term>prime mover</term><note>The outermost moving sphere in the Ptolemaic system, which drives the daily rotation of the heavens.</note>, namely the daily motion, by which it revolves once in a natural day around the poles of the world. The second is from the ninth sphere, which is called the second mover; this is always according to the succession of the signs <note>original: "secundum successionem signorum," meaning moving West to East through the Zodiac.</note> against the first motion, around the poles of the regular zodiac, such that in any two hundred years it progresses by one degree and nearly twenty-eight minutes. This is called the "motion of the apogees and fixed stars" in the tables. It is the arc of the zodiac of the prime mover between the beginning of Aries of the prime mover and the beginning of Aries of the ninth sphere. For the surface of the <term>ecliptic</term><note>The sun's apparent path across the sky.</note> of the ninth sphere is always within the surface of the ecliptic of the prime mover. The third motion, however, is proper to itself, which is called the **motion of trepidation**, or the **access and recess** <note>A historical theory suggesting the equinoxes "vibrate" or oscillate back and forth rather than moving in a continuous circle.</note> of the eighth sphere. This occurs upon two small equal circles described in the concavity of the ninth sphere, centered upon the beginnings of Aries and Libra of that same sphere, so that two fixed points of the eighth sphere—which are called the beginnings of Aries and Libra—being diametrically opposite, regularly describe the circumferences of these two circles of the ninth sphere. Along with this, the ecliptic of the eighth sphere always intersects the ecliptic of the ninth; it intersects it at least at the beginnings of Cancer and Capricorn of the ninth sphere, which are diametrically opposite. From this it follows that when one of these same points of the eighth sphere is in the southern half of its circle, the other will be in the northern half of its circle. Also, the ecliptic of the eighth sphere will always divide the ecliptic of the ninth into equal parts when it intersects it, and the portions of the small circles will be alternately equal.

¶ The rule for the velocity of this motion is this: that each of the two points completes the circumference of its small circle in which it is carried in precisely seven thousand years. Although by this motion the two aforementioned points—namely the beginnings of Aries and Libra of the eighth sphere—describe two equal circumferences of circles, it happens that no other points of it describe circumferences of circles. Indeed, the beginnings of Cancer and Capricorn of the eighth sphere, having as it were conoidal shapes <note>original: "figuras conoidales," referring to the cone-like path traced by the poles during this oscillation.</note> for their base lines...

it is necessary to complete the curves on both sides from the heads <note>The "head" refers to the starting point or 0° mark of a zodiac sign</note> of Cancer and Capricorn of the ninth sphere. From this, they will sometimes precede them, sometimes follow them, and sometimes they are joined together. For the head of Cancer of the eighth sphere and the head of Cancer of the ninth sphere are joined when the head of Aries of the eighth sphere is at its maximum latitude from the ecliptic <note>The ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note> of the ninth. This occurs on the great circle passing through the poles of the zodiac of the ninth sphere and the centers of the small circles. Furthermore, the poles of the ecliptic of the eighth sphere—improperly called poles—sometimes approach the poles of the ecliptic of the ninth, sometimes they are directly beneath them, and sometimes they are moved away from them. --- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial mechanics, specifically focusing on the shifting relationship between the fixed stars (the eighth sphere) and the primary motion of the universe (the first mobile).</meta>

At the heads of **Aries** and **Libra** <note>The points of the spring and autumn equinoxes</note> of the **primum mobile** <term>First Mobile: the outermost celestial sphere that carries all others from east to west</term>, the stars are sometimes seen to move away from them, proceeding sometimes according to and sometimes against the succession of the signs. From this, it happens that the maximum declinations <note>The angle between the celestial equator and the ecliptic</note> of the **zodiac** are variable. Therefore, it is believed to have happened that different astronomers at different times found the quantities of these same maximum declinations of the zodiac to be unequal. For they were found to be greater by **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer</note> than by **Almeon** <note>Likely referring to the Caliph Al-Ma'mun or the astronomer Al-Ma'mun, whose 9th-century observations showed a smaller axial tilt than Ptolemy's</note>. Since both proceeded with similar methods and means, this could hardly have happened otherwise than by such a diversity of motion, or something similar, as has just been described.

Moreover, from the variation of the intersection between the **ecliptic** <term>Ecliptic: the sun's apparent path across the sky</term> of the eighth sphere and the **equinoctial** <note>The celestial equator</note> with respect to the **Aries** of the **primum mobile**, it necessarily follows that the **equinoxes** and likewise the **solstices** are continually changing. Hence, it is not always necessary for the equinox to occur when the Sun is at the head of **Aries** in the **primum mobile**; rather, it may have happened before or be about to follow after—namely, when the Sun is at the aforementioned intersection.

¶ From this, as was said above, since the orbits carrying the **apogees** <note>The point in an orbit farthest from Earth</note> of the Sun move upon the axis of the ecliptic of the eighth sphere according to the motion of that same sphere, and the orbit carrying the Sun moves upon an axis equidistant to the aforementioned axis, it necessarily follows that the center of the solar body is always found on the surface of the ecliptic of the eighth sphere. However, this surface is often—indeed, frequently—outside the head of **Aries** of the **primum mobile**; therefore, the conclusion follows. The reasoning regarding the variation of the solstices is similar.

¶ From these points, it is first concluded that it is not necessary for the Sun, when it is at the head of **Aries** or **Libra** of the **primum mobile**, to have zero declination from the equinoctial. Secondly, it is likewise not necessary for the Sun, when at the head of **Cancer** or **Capricorn** of the **primum mobile**, to have its maximum declination from the equinoctial. For it is possible for the Sun to be on the circle passing through the poles of the ecliptic of the **primum mobile** and the head of its **Aries**, and yet be outside the plane of the equinoctial. Similarly, it is possible for it to be on the circle passing through the poles of the zodiac of the **primum mobile** and the head of its **Cancer**, and yet not have its maximum declination from the equinoctial then, but to have been at it before or to be at it in the future.

¶ It also follows from this that the **tropics** of **Cancer** and **Capricorn** are continually varying with respect to the equinoctial: now indeed approaching toward it, now moving further away from it.

Al-Farghani <note>original: "Alfraganus"; a 9th-century Persian astronomer</note> thought that they completed one degree every hundred years, always toward the east. ¶ Therefore, the mean motion of the **accession and recession** <note>also known as trepidation, a theory that the equinoxes vibrate back and forth rather than moving in a continuous circle</note> of the eighth sphere is the arc of a small circle, calculated from the highest point of the fourth part following the succession of the signs <note>the zodiacal order</note> up to the "Head of Aries" <term>Head of Aries: the vernal equinox point</term> of the eighth sphere. ¶ The **equation of the eighth sphere**, however, is the arc of the ecliptic of the ninth sphere lying between the center of the small circle and a great circle passing from the poles of the ninth sphere's ecliptic through the Head of Aries of the eighth sphere. When the mean motion of the accession and recession is zero or a semicircle, this equation becomes nothing.

--- Page 126 ---
But if it is at 90 degrees or 270 degrees, the equation itself will be at its maximum. When such a motion of "access and recess" <note>original: "accessus & recessus"; a medieval astronomical theory where the stars appear to move back and forth rather than in a continuous circle</note> is less than a semicircle, the equation must always be added; but when it is greater, it must be subtracted.

¶ Thabit <note>Thabit ibn Qurra (c. 826–901), a famous Sabian astronomer and mathematician from Harran</note> however, stated that the eighth sphere <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note> possesses only a twofold motion: one is the daily motion derived from the "first movable" <term>Primum Mobile: the outermost moving sphere in the geocentric model that drives the rotation of all others</term> without a ninth sphere; the other is its own specific motion, namely "trepidation," which occurs upon small circles.

¶ He asserted a twofold ecliptic <note>the path of the sun</note>: one fixed in the ninth sphere, and the other mobile in the eighth sphere. He proposed this so that the points of the mobile Aries and Libra <note>the equinoxes</note> are carried around in two small circles, the centers (or poles) of which are the very points of Aries and Libra on the fixed ecliptic. The arc of the fixed ecliptic between the poles of these small circles and their circumferences measures four degrees, eighteen minutes, and 43 seconds.

¶ He said, moreover, that the mobile points of Aries and Libra are carried around in such a way that when the mobile head of Aries is at the western intersection of the small circle and the equator, it moves into the half of the small circle that is north of the equator. Meanwhile, the mobile head of Libra moves through the half of its own small circle that is south of the equator. And when the mobile head of Aries is at the eastern intersection of the equator and its small circle, it moves into the half of the small circle that is south of the equator; the mobile head of Libra then revolves through the northern half of its small circle.

But when the mobile head of Aries is at either of the two points where the fixed ecliptic intersects the small circle, the mobile ecliptic will be positioned directly within the plane of the fixed ecliptic; this occurs twice in one revolution of the mobile head of Aries in its small circle. In all other positions, however, with the mobile head of Aries located on the periphery of its small circle, the mobile ecliptic will intersect the fixed ecliptic at the points of the mobile heads of Cancer and Capricorn. For these two points of the mobile ecliptic always cling to the circumference of the fixed ecliptic during this motion, so that they never depart from it. Nevertheless, it happens that they move away from the fixed heads of Cancer and Capricorn toward the east or west by a quantity of four degrees, eighteen minutes, and 43 seconds.

¶ Wherever the intersection of these ecliptics occurs, it must necessarily be a quarter of a great circle distant from the mobile beginnings of Aries and Libra. Although in one revolution of the mobile head of Aries in its small circle...

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "trepidation" or the "motion of access and recess" of the eighth sphere (the fixed stars), a theory used by medieval astronomers to explain why the positions of stars and the equinoxes seemed to shift over long periods of time.</meta>

It happens through this small circle that the heads <note>The "head" refers to the starting point or 0° mark of a zodiac sign</note> of the mobile Cancer and Capricorn are positioned beneath the heads of the fixed Cancer and Capricorn; however, the heads of the mobile Aries and Libra never reach the heads of the fixed Aries and Libra. For while the mobile ecliptic <note>The ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note> touches the small circle from the northern part at the point of mobile Aries, the mobile heads of Cancer and Capricorn are joined with the fixed heads. A similar thing happens at the southern point of contact. But the heads of Aries and Libra are always distant from the fixed heads by the quantity already mentioned. 

The fixed ecliptic also always intersects the equator at the fixed heads of Aries and Libra at a constant angle, namely 23 degrees, 33 minutes, and 30 seconds. But the mobile ecliptic intersects the equator successively at individual points contained within two arcs, which the mobile ecliptic separates from the equator at the two positions of contact; the quantity of each is about 21 degrees and 30 minutes. For the maximum distance of the head of mobile Aries from the intersection of the ecliptic with the equator is ten degrees and forty-five minutes. 

From this, the maximum declination <note>The distance of a celestial object north or south of the celestial equator</note> of the mobile ecliptic from the equator is variable: sometimes greater than the declination of the fixed ecliptic, sometimes less than it, and sometimes equal to it. For it is equal to it when the mobile ecliptic is beneath the surface of the fixed one. It is greater, however, at the positions of contact. Hence Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer</note> found it to be 23 degrees, 51 minutes, and 20 seconds. It is smaller when the head of mobile Aries is at the intersection of the equator and the small circle; for then the intersection of the ecliptics will be at the point of the mobile ecliptic that declines the most, which declines less than the fixed head of Cancer and Capricorn.

The **equation of the eighth sphere** is therefore the arc of the mobile ecliptic intercepted between the head of mobile Aries and the intersection of that same ecliptic with the equinoctial <note>The celestial equator</note>. But the **motion of access and recess** <note>Also known as trepidation; a theory that the equinoxes vibrate back and forth rather than moving in a continuous circle</note> is the arc of the small circle between the head of mobile Aries and the intersection of the equator and the small circle, proceeding through the northern half of the circle. By this motion, it happens that the fixed stars seem to move now toward the east, now toward the west; sometimes with a fast motion, and sometimes with a slow motion. For when the head of mobile Aries is in the quarters of the small circle from the equator—namely near the positions of contact of which we spoke—

<summary>This page describes the geometry of the "trepidation" theory, explaining how the mobile ecliptic moves in relation to the fixed celestial equator and the resulting variations in the obliquity of the ecliptic.</summary>
<keywords>trepidation, eighth sphere, ecliptic, declination, Ptolemy, Aries, Cancer, Capricorn, celestial equator, access and recess</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "trepidation" or oscillation of the eighth sphere (the firmament of fixed stars), explaining why the rate of stellar motion appears to change over long periods of time.</meta>

The stars seem to be moved slowly toward that part toward which their motion is directed when the <term>equation of the eighth sphere</term><note>The "equation" here refers to the mathematical correction used to account for the wobbling motion of the celestial sphere.</note> increases or decreases only slightly. But when the <term>Head of Aries</term><note>The "Head of Aries" refers to the moving vernal equinox point in this model of the universe.</note> is at or near either of the intersections of the equator and the small circle, the stars will be seen to move quickly toward that part toward which their motion is directed, because at those positions the equation of the eighth sphere increases or decreases the most. From this, a manifest diversity in their motion has been found.

For **Ptolemy** compared their locations, verified in his own time, to the locations found by **Hipparchus** and others; he found them moved by a slow motion, namely one degree in one hundred years. For at that time, the Head of Aries was separated from the quarter-point of the small circle, approaching the equator from the south. But later observers, while it approached more closely, found them to move one degree in sixty-six years.

Now in our time, namely in the **Year of our Lord 1460**, the northern Head of Aries has been made to stand nearly sixty-six degrees distant from the intersection of the small circle and the equator. Whence it is also distant from the intersection of the moving <term>ecliptic</term><note>The sun's apparent path across the sky.</note> with the equator by nearly 90 degrees and 48 minutes. Therefore, the intersection now occurs at the 20th degree and 12th minute of **Pisces** on the moving ecliptic.

The maximum equation of the eighth sphere, however, is reached when the moving Head of Aries is upon the quarter-points of the small circle, measured from its intersections with the equator, and it is ten degrees and forty-five minutes. Whence any point from the 19th degree and 15th minute of Pisces up to the 10th degree and 45th minute of Aries on the moving ecliptic can become the place of intersection which is the point of the <term>vernal equinox</term><note>The first day of spring, when day and night are equal.</note>. The same is to be understood regarding the point of the **autumnal equinox** in the opposite arc.

It is also established that the <term>tropic points</term><note>The points of the summer and winter solstices.</note> are not always at the moving Head of **Cancer** or **Capricorn**, but at points distant by a quarter-circle from the intersection of the equator with the moving ecliptic. **Ptolemy**, therefore, judging in his time that the stars moved from west to east, believed there was only one fixed zodiac—namely, one which would always have the same declination from the equator. From this follows what he said: for since the southern stars, receding from the winter tropic, were approaching toward the point of the vernal equinox, and between this point and the summer tropic they were receding from the equator toward the north, he indicated that they moved according to the succession of the signs.

<summary>This page explains the variable speed of the precession of the equinoxes using the theory of trepidation, noting that the rate of stellar motion changed from Ptolemy's 100 years per degree to 66 years per degree by 1460.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Hipparchus, 1460, trepidation, eighth sphere, vernal equinox, ecliptic, Aries, Pisces, celestial kinematics</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page concludes a discussion on the motion of the eighth sphere (the fixed stars) and provides the colophon for the printed edition.</meta>

But assuming this motion in its own time, they were in truth moving against the succession of the signs <note>original: "contra successionem signorum" - this refers to retrograde motion or motion moving from East to West relative to the zodiac</note> of the fixed ecliptic. Nor is it the case that they were seen to move according to the succession of the signs because of the then-decreasing equation of the eighth sphere. This was because at the intersection of the mobile ecliptic with the equator, the head of Aries <note>The first point of the zodiac</note> was thought to be in the immobile zodiac, which intersection he always estimated to be fixed. All the lower spheres follow this motion in their own movements, so that in respect to this mobile ecliptic, the apogees <term>auges: the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</term> of the carrying circles and their declinations are always invariable.

### Final theory of the eighth sphere.

<image-desc>
An astronomical diagram depicting the "Final theory of the eighth sphere." The diagram consists of several intersecting celestial circles. A central vertical axis is labeled "North pole of the world" at the top and "pole of the fixed ecliptic" at the bottom. The primary intersecting circles are labeled "ecliptic" and "equinoctial" (the celestial equator). Four cardinal points around the main circular path are marked with the abbreviation "fixed" and the symbols for the zodiac:
- Top: Cancer (♋) fixed.
- Left: Libra (♎) fixed.
- Right: Aries (♈) fixed.
- Bottom: Capricorn (♑) fixed.
On the right side of the diagram, there is a circular ink stamp from the "National Central Library of Florence" featuring a crown and the Roman numeral "XV".
</image-desc>

->Ratdolt of Augsburg. July 6th, in the Year of Salvation 1482.<-
